A root that the stop of the machine cut has a wrong check: the root before it names blocks that no commit changed. So the disk always has the state of the last commit or of the one before. The old root names the state before the last commit: versions (P9) can grow from it, with a rule that keeps old blocks.
151. **Property 9 says "the first version is SQLite on the raw block device". This step does not do that.** The agent that built the step had a wrong rule from the coordinator, "Odin standard library only". The owner never gave that rule: the recorded rule is about the kernel tool only (corrected by the owner on 2026-11-10). So this reason is false, and nothing forbids SQLite. What stays true: the decision of 2026-11-09 also says "write a new engine under MIT", and this step writes it now, small (800 lines with its exports), in place of a first version that a later step must replace. The owner then decided on 2026-11-10 to keep this engine and to remove SQLite from the system (properties 9 and 12).
152. **The private store is named by the root service, not by the program.** The command Connect on the service ring has two more fields: the name of the caller as the root service names it (32 bytes), and a grant: an amount and the flag "acts for the principal" (`isolated.CALLER_AMOUNT_MASK`, `CALLER_ACTS_FOR_PRINCIPAL`). The storage service uses the name as the name of the store and the amount as its limit of blocks. An export takes the name of an object and never the name of a store: a program reaches its own store only (P8). A program that the root service starts again has the same name and finds its data. `system.service_caller` gives the connection of the call that runs. The fields are in the generic contract of a service: any service can know who calls it, and the caller cannot choose.
153. **The storage service is a service with exports**, as `paint` and `tally`: `write`, `read`, `size`, `remove`, `objects`, `stores`. The console calls them with `connect("storage")` and the descriptions of the file; a program calls them with `support/storage`. Each change is one commit on the disk when the call returns: the system has no save action (P9). One thread serves the calls one at a time; a call waits for its block requests, with all the blocks of the commit in flight at the same time.
154. **A service that starts when a program asks for it** (`on_demand` in the root service, P16 "hundreds of background processes"). `connect("storage")` starts the storage service and the block service of the data disk, then connects. The storage service reads the disk before it serves: the call of the first connection waits in its ring.
155. **The limit of a store is a number of blocks** (P20, line 16): its table, its lists and its data. A change that makes the store larger than its limit gives -2 and writes nothing. The root service gives each program its limit by name (`store_limit_of`): 4 blocks for the console, so that the test reaches it, 64 for the others.
156. **The person sees each store and revokes a grant.** `storage.stores()` lists each store of the disk with its objects, its blocks, its limit and the count of connections that hold its grant; only a caller with `CALLER_ACTS_FOR_PRINCIPAL` sees it. `grants` shows "store", and `revoke(name, "store")` sends the command `Grant` with no amount for each connection of that program to the storage service: the store stays on the disk, the calls of the program give -1, and the program continues. The kernel does not revoke the memory of the connection here, because the program would stop on a page fault at its next call (choice 108); the store is data of the storage service, so the storage service is the place that ends the grant.
157. **A program receives its store when it starts.** A program with the role Store and not Person (`notes`) has its connection to the storage service in its context: the call ring, the call region, the storage service with the right Read, and the file of the storage service mapped read-only at `storage.FILE_ADDRESS` (`support/storage`). The root service starts the storage service first if it does not run. `support/storage` finds each export by its name in that file and encodes the call with its description, as the console does: the program has no copy of the numbers of the exports. A program with no ring to the root service cannot ask for other connections.
158. **The persistent program writes each change** (`programs/notes`): each key of the note is one commit of the object "note", and each step of the count one commit of the object "count". The count is three blocks that each hold the count in each of their 512 numbers: a store that showed a part of one commit with a part of another would show blocks that do not agree. One commit costs 3.1 to 3.2 ms in the test machine: two flushes, and QEMU makes each flush a sync of the file of the disk on the host.
159. **The test of a crash ends QEMU, and the test of a cut write damages the disk.** The tool ends QEMU in the middle of 1000 commits: the next start reads each block with its check and finds the last commit. Ending QEMU loses no write that QEMU already gave to the host, so it cannot cut a block; the tool cuts the root block of the last commit itself (`root_cut`): the next start does not use it and finds the commit before, with each block of it unchanged. The tool knows two facts of the format for this: the root blocks are blocks 0 and 1, and the generation is at byte 8.
160. **A loader loads imports** (`support/system/load.odin`). Before, `system.program_start` and `system.module_load` refused a module with an import, and the console and the call host had no way to find the file of an import. Now a module space holds the modules that a loader put in one program, each one time, with a count of uses. The loader puts the module first, then each import after it, then binds the slots of the module with the addresses of its imports. `module_space_release` removes a module with no use, and its imports lose one use. On a failure, the space is as it was before the load: the loader removes what it mapped and gives back the counts. With a table of prepared modules (choice 136), the pages that do not change after the load also hold the bound slots: a prepared module is for one base and one binding (the hash and the base of the module of each import), and the table keys it by the hash of the module, its base and its binding. A module that a program loads into itself (the console, a call host) has no table: it is a copy of its own, bound like the others.
161. **The load source is a grant** (P20). The loader finds a file only in what the party that starts the program gives: files in the form of the boot image. It checks the hash of each file before it uses it. The root service and the console give the boot image. A call host receives one page from the console, with the list of its files, and the files only: the module and its imports (`system.source_closure`).
162. **Refusals name the module.** A load that fails names the stage and the module, and the space is as it was before. 164 gives the form of the record.
163. **`load` of a module that a module of the console imports gives the same copy**: `load("calendar")` after `load("agenda")` adds the commands of calendar, and the two names call one copy with one set of data. `unload` of such a module removes its commands and keeps the module.
164. **The error of a load is a record that the caller owns** (owner, 2026-11-10: errors that are robust, descriptive and informative; no exception and no global error state). `module_space_load`, `module_space_release` and `program_start` take `^module.Load_Error` and give `false` or a status. The record is in the contract library (`contract/module/error.odin`), because a call host gives it to the console in the call region: the stage that failed, the module that was being loaded, the import concerned, a replacement on the path, the export, the slot, and for a check the difference with its two sides (type, rights or count, record name). Names come from the load source. `system.load_error_text` writes it as lines; the console prints each line after "error: ". An import cycle needs a replacement, because an import names a hash; the loader marks a module that loads its imports, and refuses an import of it with the stage Cycle. The first completion of a call host says if it loaded its module, so `isolate` reports a load error at once. `MODULE_CONTRACT.md`, "The error of a load", lists what the record does not explain.
165. **The kernel keeps the module records of each program** (operation `Program_Module`, number 26; `Inspect` kind `Modules`). The identity of a program that runs must be visible to the fault report and to `programs()`, for each program and each loader: the kernel already keeps the name that the creator gives. A record is 80 bytes: the base, the size, the hash, and the hash that it replaces. A program has 16. The operation needs Control on the program, or 0 for the caller. The kernel refuses two records that share an address. The kernel writes the record of the root service from the hash of its entry in the boot image. A fault names the module of the instruction and its offset, and the module that it replaces.
166. **The replacement table is one page of the root service.** It gives the page with the right Read to the console and to the manual, and the records to the loader of each program that it starts. A count of versions lets a reader copy the records while the root service writes them: the reader copies again when the count changed. A replacement applies to the loads that follow it, not to a program that runs. The console gives the records to a call host on the page of its files, with the module of each replacement.
167. **The check compares the descriptions of the exports that the slots use** (`module.functions_compare`, which gives the first difference): the count and the types of the parameters, the rights of each Handle, each Record with `module.records_equal`, the result. The names of the parameters and the help can differ: they are for a person. The loader binds by name one time, at load: the slot has the address of the export of the replacement, and a call is the same indirect call.
168. **The person sets a replacement by names, through the root service.** `Root_Command.Replace` carries the name of the replaced module and the number of the entry of the other module in the boot image: a ring record has 56 bytes of values, and two names of 32 bytes do not fit. Only a program with the role Replace can ask (the console). The root service refuses a chain: one record for one import, so the loader follows one step.
169. **The stop report.** The kernel keeps, for each program that stops on a fault, the stop code, the instruction, the address and the module record of the module of the instruction (`op.Stop_Report`). `Program_State` writes it at an address that the holder of the program handle gives. A fault report then names the module that ran, from the record that its loader wrote: the console prints it for a call host.
170. **Measured** (program `imports`, in the machine test, the least of 5 runs): a call through an import slot costs 3.5 ns to calendar and 2.7 ns to calendar_fixed in its place, for 1 000 000 calls: the same instruction, with two bodies that differ. The bind of agenda costs 372 ns with a direct import and 891 ns with the check of the replacement of one slot: the check adds about 520 ns for one slot. The load of agenda and its import costs 117 us and 154 us, with the hash of each file. Over five runs of the test, the bind took 216 to 377 ns and 857 to 904 ns, and the load 95 to 126 us and 120 to 155 us.
171. **The code of an imported module is one copy for all the programs of a table.** Choice 136 prepared the segments that do not change after the load at one base: an import is at another base in each program (after the module of the program), so two programs that import calendar had two copies. A prepared module now has two parts. The code (the segments with no Write) has no rebase and no slot: the table keys it by the hash only, and each program at each base maps it. The fixed part (read-only after the load) has the rebases and the slots: the table keys it by the hash, the base and the binding. A table has 64 places, two for each module. Measured by `imports`: it starts itself again and `agenda` with one table, the two import calendar at two places, and the table has one copy of the code of calendar (2 pages), mapped 4 times (2 segments in 2 programs). `frames` counts the pages of the two parts. The cost: the twins of `frames` (a module with no import, at one base) paid 313 pages with one part, and pay 315 with two parts, in one run each.
172. **A Table result** (`module.Type.Table`, owner item 94 (1) of `HANDOFF.md`: many records in one call, in memory of the caller). An export gives records of one description that follow each other: the caller gives a buffer `{address, capacity in records}` after the parameters, and the callee writes the records that fit and gives the count of the full table. It is the Text result with records in place of bytes: the call form, the isolated call (`isolated.result_table`) and the service (`export_serve`) have one more case each, and no new mechanism. A negative count is an error that the documentation of the export names. A record of a Table has no Handle, as a Record result. In Odin: `result: ^[]Name` as the last parameter.
173. **A Text field of a fixed size in a record** (`module.Field`: the type Text, the size in the detail). A row of a table needs names and hashes, and a record holds values only: the text is in the record, UTF-8 and zero bytes after it, and holds no address, so the record means the same in each program and travels as bytes. In Odin a field `[N]u8`, N a number from 1 to 256. A hash is in a row as 64 hexadecimal digits: one field form for all texts, which a person reads and the console compares and joins; the cost is 32 bytes for each hash. The other form, a field of raw bytes, needs a second field type in the contract and in each reader.
174. **The tool describes a record that a library declares.** The rows are Odin structs of `support/information`; the manual exports `modules :: proc "c" (result: ^[]information.Module) -> i64`. The tool reads the imports of each file, and reads the structs of an imported package of `support:` or `contract:` when an export names one. So a row has one declaration, and each service that serves it has its description in its own file.
175. **The information functions** (`support/information`). Each one takes the catalog (the boot image that the program was granted, and the records of the replacement table) and a slice of rows of the caller; it writes the rows that fit and gives the count of the full answer; it allocates nothing and keeps no state. A row holds numbers and short texts read from the tables; a long text is a slice of the file (`export_text`): the file stays the one source. The rows: `Module` (name, hash, file, code, constants, data, zero, entry, exports, imports, records, and a problem), `Export` (module, name, number, parameters, result, rights, signature), `Import` (module, number, hash, name, slots, replacement), `Slot`, `Segment`, `Running`, `Replacement`. The functions: `modules`, `exports`, `imports`, `slots`, `segments` (one module by name or by hash, or "" for each module), and the reverse questions `importers` (by hash), `exporters` (by the name of an export), `described` (a text in the help or the documentation), `handles` (a right that an export asks of a handle), then `running` and `replacements`.
176. **A malformed module gives a Problem, never a fault.** The tables of a module are input that nobody checked. `information.check` tests each rule that the functions rely on, in order: the header, the range of each table of the directory, the segments, the entry, the exports, the record descriptions, the descriptions of the exports, each name and text in the string table, the documentation, the slots. It gives the first problem: its kind, the table, the record, the byte of the file, the bad value and its limit; `problem_write` makes a sentence of it. `modules()` gives a row for a malformed module with the problem in four fields; a function on one module gives the Problem; the manual gives -2 and `problem(name)` the sentence. `odin test support/information` has 12 malformed cases, each with its table, record and byte, and 20 000 files with random damage, read by each function with the bounds checks of the test build.
177. **What runs comes from the module records of the kernel, not from a second table.** The first design of this step had a program table that the root service wrote. Choice 165 gives the same facts for each program, also for the programs that the root service did not start (call hosts, the nodes of `rings`), with the hash that a replacement changed: one mechanism for one concept, so the program table was removed before its first commit. `running(machine, catalog, rows)` reads `Program_Report` and the `Module_Report` of each program slot. The grant is the machine with the right Inspect (P20): the root service gives it to `manual` now. The manual gives `running()` only to a caller that acts for the principal (`CALLER_ACTS_FOR_PRINCIPAL`): it does not widen its own grant to any caller (P20, line 12). The root service now gives `CALLER_ACTS_FOR_PRINCIPAL` in each connection of such a caller, not only in a connection to `storage`.
178. **The manual is the service of the information of the modules.** It already had the boot image, the replacement table and the documentation of each module, and its exports are described: the console needs no code for each kind of row, and a program connects to it as to any service. A program that holds the grants itself compiles in `support/information` and calls the functions directly. 13 exports: `modules`, `exports`, `imports`, `importers`, `exporters`, `described`, `handles`, `slots`, `segments`, `running`, `replacements`, `problem`, `overview`. A Table result gives -1 for a module that the catalog does not have, -2 for a malformed module, -3 for a refused `running`.
179. **The console holds a table, and a few generic commands combine tables** (`programs/console/tables.odin`): `where(table, field, value)`, `sort(table, field)`, `join(left, right, field)`, `columns(table, "a b c")`, `first(table, count)`, `rows(table)`, `row(table, number)`. Each one reads only the record description, so it works on each Table of each service. `join` keeps each field of the left record and each field of the right record whose name the left does not have; `join` and `columns` write the new description in the format of the module contract, in the memory of the line: a header, a directory, and the tables Records, Fields and Strings. No second format and no query language exist. The count command is `rows`, because the module `probe` exports `count`, and a command of a module hides a command of the console with the same name. The console prints a table with a line of field names, one line for each row, and the count; a Text longer than 24 characters ends with "..".
180. **The replacements are in the answers.** An `Import` row has the module that the replacement table gives in place of the import; `importers` also gives the imports that a replacement changes to the module; a `Running` row has the hash that a replacement changed (`Module_Record.replaces`).
181. **The window.** The key Tab, or `manual.overview()`, shows the table of all the modules: the bytes of code, constants, data and zero data, the counts of exports and imports, the count of modules that import each one, and the count of programs that run it, with the totals; a module that a program runs is green, a library yellow, a malformed module red. The page of a module has three more parts: its sizes, the modules that import it (and through which replacement), and the programs that run it, with the slot, the address and the module that it replaces.
182. **Measured** (in the machine test, at the start of `manual`, the clock of 1 ms and repeats for 50 ms or more): all the 45 modules of the boot image and their 65 exports in 149 to 151 us, with the check of each file each time; one reverse question, who imports calendar (2 modules, one through the replacement), in 42 us. Two runs.
183. **A wrong block under a valid root damages one object, not the service** (`programs/storage/engine.odin`, `walk`, `mount`; found by the line-by-line read of Review 7). Before, a wrong block under each valid root stopped the service for every program. Now the walk continues: an object with a wrong list or wrong data is damaged, a store with a wrong table is damaged, and the others are served. The blocks that a wrong table or a wrong list named are not known, so they are free. A read checks the blocks again each time.
184. **Which root the service takes.** The valid root with the highest generation, when it names no wrong block. If it names one and the valid root before it names none, the root before it: the last commit was not whole on the disk. If each valid root names a wrong block, the newest one, with its damage: no root is better. Cost: a second walk, then a third one, in the rare cases.
185. **A damaged object is never an empty object.** `size` answers -8, `read` gives no bytes, `objects()` and `stores()` say "damaged". The client `support/storage` gives the status `Damaged`, also when the text of a read does not have the size that `size` gave. A write or a remove replaces a damaged object: a change does not need the old blocks. `notes` says in its window which object is damaged, and a key writes a new one. Before, a disk error on a read looked like an empty object, and `notes` wrote over its note.
186. **The service copies the name of an object one time** (`name_keep`), then checks and uses the copy. The bytes of a Text argument are in the call region, which the caller can change during the call. A name with a zero byte is refused (-3): a zero byte ends a name on the disk, and a first zero byte says that a place is free. The data of a write was already copied to the block before its check.
187. **A format that a stop cut is formatted again.** The first root goes to block 1 and the first commit to block 0. So block 0 of zero bytes and a first root with a wrong check prove that the disk holds no object. Before, the service refused such a disk for good.
188. **The format of the data disk is a contract** (`contract/store`): the records, the check, the validity of a root. A disk lives longer than the program that wrote it. The tool reads the same declarations to cut a root and to damage a block: before, the tool held its own offsets. The ERROR_ numbers of the service are in `support/storage`, for the service and the client.
189. **One build gives one hash: the compiler runs with one thread** (`tools/kerneltool/modules.odin`: `-thread-count:1 -no-threaded-checker`). The gap of step 34 said that the hash of a large module depends on the folder of the build. The cause was not the folder: with its threads, the Odin compiler puts the functions, the constants and the instances of a generic function in an order that changes from one build to the next. Measured: with neither option, 13 of 48 modules differ between two builds in one folder; with `-thread-count:1` only, 4 or 5 differ; with the two options, 0 differ in three builds and 0 between two folders. The cost is none: 2.3 s for `console`, against 2.5 s. The boot program gets the same options and `-source-code-locations:filename`: its file is the same bytes in two folders. The check builds the modules a second time and compares each file. One name still changes in the object (a number in the name of a variable of the runtime), in the symbol table, which is not in a module. **Corrected by 400 (2026-11-11)**: `-thread-count:1` did not give one thread. The stock compiler starts one worker beside the main thread, and the rare differing builds came from that worker. The flake now patches the compiler, and `-no-threaded-checker` is gone (it changes only one phase of the checker).
190. **The boot display is cards, a path and tiles, drawn with rectangles only** (`support/bootscreen/`; owner, 2026-11-10: the display must be original, and the steps must be an interface, not printed text). The display keeps its rule: each thing that it shows is a record of the kernel or of the boot program. What changed is the form. The head has the path of the start: ten steps of the start as squares, full when done, a ring for the step of now, red when the start failed there, with the count and the sentence under it. The stages are one tube on a line of time, each with its duration inside, and each program of the start is a mark at its creation. Before the kernel measures the stages, the same card lists the ten steps with "done" and "now". The memory is one band by address. Each processor is a square. Each module of the boot image is a tile: its width is its size and its color is the first byte of its hash, so a module that changes takes a new color. All of it is `fill` of rectangles with a color that goes from top to bottom, with no round corner (owner, 2026-11-10: the owner does not want round forms), and the 8x16 font, also at two and three times its size. No floating point, no image, no new operation. Measured on the host, 200 draws of a 1024 x 768 screen with the same compiler options: about 0.5 ms for one draw, against 0.33 to 0.40 ms for the display before. A start draws the display three times.

191. **The network is three programs, and the kernel has no network code** (P1, P5, P7). The frame service (`programs/frame`) is the device service of the virtio network device. The network service (`programs/network`) is its one client. A program with the grant Network (`net`) has one endpoint in the network service. The root service finds the device at the place 18 of the bus and starts the frame service as it starts a block service (choice 142): the same grants, the right Physical, one message interrupt for the two queues of the device. The kernel did not change.
192. **One package for virtio** (`support/virtio`): the capabilities in the copy of the configuration, the agreement on the features, the split virtqueue (start, give, notify, used). The block service uses it now: 324 lines in place of 507. `driver_start` of the root service takes the class of the device (`Driver`: the program, the device number of the class, the ring and its role).
193. **The frame contract** (`contract/frame`) has the form of the block contract: a frame ring, and a frame region of the client that the device reads and writes with no copy; Attach through the ring, proved with `Handle_State`; many requests in flight; a command is taken only when the completion queue has room for it. A Receive is a place that the client gives before a frame arrives (P14): the network service keeps 128 in flight. The frame service does not read a frame: it computes no checksum and checks no address. The header of 12 bytes that virtio puts before each frame stays in the memory of the frame service; one header of zero bytes serves each Send (no offload).
194. **Where the protocols run: the nearest form of property 5.** Property 5 says "the network stack is a library in the program that uses the network", and U7 of `ARCHITECTURE.md` says that the network service gives a program a queue of the card and leaves the data path. This step does not do that, and says so. The virtio network device of QEMU has one pair of queues: a program must read each frame to give it to its endpoint (open question 3 of `ARCHITECTURE.md`: virtio-net under QEMU needs a software switch). That program must also check the source address and port of each frame that a program sends against its grant, or a program could send as another one. To check the headers of UDP and IPv4 costs the same as to write them, so the network service writes them, and a program gives the data and the remote. What keeps property 5: the code of the protocols is a library (`support/network`) that does no input or output; the kernel is not on the path of a datagram (measured below); QUIC will be a library in the program over datagrams, as property 5 asks. The plan for TCP: a library in the program over an endpoint of segments; the network service checks the IPv4 header and the ports of each segment against the grant, and keeps no state of a connection. A device with several queues and filters lets the network service leave the data path; the first machine has none.
195. **A grant names one remote** (P20, lines 3, 4 and 11). The root service names, for each program by its name (`endpoint_of`), one remote address and one remote UDP port. The program sends only there, and receives only from there; Resolve and Echo accept only the address of the grant. The network service chooses the local port (49152 + the place of the endpoint): a program does not choose a port and knows no global port name. Two other forms were weighed. A local port that receives from any remote (a server) opens the program to each machine of the network. A set of hosts gives more than a client needs. One remote is the least that serves a client: the nearest form of "sandbox by default". A server (a local port and the remotes that it answers) is the next form, with TCP. Names are not in a grant yet: no DNS.
196. **The root service makes the datagram region, not the program.** In the first version, the program made its region and gave it through the ring, as the data region of the block contract. Found before the commit: the program then holds the origin of the mapping of the network service. A revocation of its own handle ends that mapping, and the next copy of the network service is a page fault that stops it (choice 108): one program stops the network of all programs. The block service has no such case: its device uses physical addresses and it maps no region of its client. Now the root service makes the datagram ring and the datagram region (128 pages, pages on first use), as it makes the call region of a connection (choice 53), and gives both to the two programs through the control ring. Property 14 holds for the device: the network service supplies the frame region to the frame service.
197. **One copy of the data, in the network service.** The data of a datagram is copied one time, from the datagram region to the frame region, or back, by the network service: 64 to 1472 bytes. The headers and the checksum are computed over bytes that the program cannot change after the check. The device reads and writes the frame region with no copy.
198. **Checksums in the network service, no offload.** The network service measures at its start the headers and the checksum of a datagram of 1472 bytes: the log has "network: the headers and the checksum of a datagram of 1472 bytes: N ns": 600 to 800 ns in three runs. The round trip of a datagram is 60 to 201 us: the checksum is not the cost to remove. An offload also makes the frame service read the frames.
199. **Errors are records.** (Changed on 2026-11-11, 385: the values of a refused completion are now an error record.) A refused request has the detail of the refusal in the values of its completion: Not_Granted gives the remote of the request and the remote of the grant; Too_Large the count of bytes and the limit; Out_Of_Range the place, the count and the size of the region; No_Answer the address, the milliseconds and what did not answer. A frame from the wire that breaks a rule gives a `datagram.Problem`: the layer, the kind, the byte of the frame, the value and the limit (the record of the module information, choice 176, for frames). The network service counts each kind in each layer with its last Problem, and the command Problems writes the table in the datagram region (`Problem_Row`, 32 bytes): a table of fixed-size records in memory of the program; choice 205 says which rows an endpoint reads. `network.problem_write` makes a sentence of one: "ipv4, byte 24: the checksum is 0x77bd, the bytes give 0x22bd".
200. **A frame from the wire never faults the network service.** `network.parse` checks each length before it reads a field, and gives a Packet or a Problem. `odin test support/network` has two known answers (the sum of RFC 1071, an IPv4 header with its checksum), the frames that the library writes read back, 19 malformed frames each with its layer, kind, byte, value and limit, a sentence, and 20 000 frames with random damage and random ends, read with the bounds checks of the test build. In the machine, the peer sends 11 malformed frames, and the network service refuses each one in the expected row.
201. **The peer: QEMU `-netdev dgram` to the kernel tool.** The QEMU of the flake (10.2.4) has the backends user, socket, dgram, stream and others. User networking answers ARP and echo at 10.0.2.2 and gives a datagram to 10.0.2.2 to the loopback of the host, but the machine must know the port, and several tests run at the same time on one host: they would share it. `guestfwd` is for TCP only. With `dgram`, QEMU sends each frame as one UDP datagram to a port that the tool chose (port 0: the host gives a free one), from a port that the host gives. So no frame leaves the host, each run has the same answers, and tests that run at the same time do not meet. The peer (`tools/kerneltool/peer.odin`, `core:net`, one thread) is the gateway 10.0.2.2 and an echo server on port 7. It reads each frame with its own code, not with `support/network`: a wrong checksum of the library is a fault that the peer finds. The test fails when the peer receives one wrong frame, or when the machine did not answer the echo request of the peer. The cost: about 400 lines of the tool check the same rules a second time.
202. **The network is a machine of its own in the test** (`scenario_network`): the main scenario, that other steps change, does not change, and the frames of the peer go to one machine. The cost: one more start of the machine in each test.
203. **Measured** (the test machine, QEMU with KVM, 4 processors; ten runs, most of them during the tests of other agents): the round trip of a datagram of 64 bytes, 1000 times: 60 to 88 us at least, 83 to 201 us in the middle. With 64 datagrams in flight for one second: 57 886 to 81 733 datagrams sent and answered, 0 lost. Kernel entries for each datagram: 0.18 to 0.24 of `net`, 0.35 to 0.57 of the network service, 0.71 to 0.98 of the frame service: no kernel entry carries the data, an entry is a wake or a wait, and one serves several datagrams. The frame service enters most: one interrupt for each group of frames; it does not ask the device for fewer interrupts. The first ARP took 1.9 to 2.8 ms, the echo 192 to 302 us, the first datagram 221 to 316 us. The round trip includes the peer, a thread of the tool on the host, and two passes through QEMU.
204. **The grant Network is revoked by the network service** (`Root_Grant.Network`). `revoke("net", "network")` makes the root service send Endpoint_Grant with no remote. The network service then refuses each request that names a remote (Not_Granted with the grant 0 and 0), and gives no datagram to the endpoint. The endpoint stays, as a store stays after its grant (choice 156). The console prints "net lost the grant network, holdings ended: 1": the count is of endpoints, and the console names each count "holdings".

205. **A refused frame belongs to one endpoint or to the machine, and no endpoint reads the row of another** (P20, line 1; correction of the coordinator before the merge). The first version had one table for all endpoints: a row Not_Granted held the port of the grant of another endpoint in its limit, and its count told a program of the traffic of another program. Now a datagram that reached the local port of an endpoint from a remote that its grant does not name is counted in the table of that endpoint (scope Endpoint), with the datagrams dropped with no place. A frame that no endpoint owns (a malformed frame, a port of no endpoint) is a fact of the machine (scope Machine). An endpoint reads the facts of the machine only with the right Inspect of its grant (`datagram.GRANT_INSPECT`): Problems then also gives the rows of the machine, and Statistics the kernel entries of the two services and the echo requests answered; without it they are 0. The root service gives this right only to a program that holds the machine with the right Inspect, which reads the reports of all programs already: the right adds no fact that the program cannot read, as `running()` of the manual needs a caller that acts for the principal (choice 177). The other form, the facts of the machine on the log only, was refused: the log is not an output (choice 51), and `net` must show its cases. `problem_rows` (`support/network/tables.odin`) takes the table of the asking endpoint and the table of the machine only: it cannot reach a table of another endpoint. A host test has two endpoints and the machine: the endpoint without the right reads no row, with the right it reads the rows of the machine and not the row of the other endpoint.
206. **The table of hardware addresses learns only what the network service asked** (`network.neighbor_learn`). An ARP frame from an address is taken only if a request of this service waits for that address (a Send, a Resolve or an Echo before its echo request). So an ARP frame that nobody asked for changes nothing, also not the entry of the gateway; an ARP request for the address of the machine gets its answer and teaches nothing. When the table is full, the answer replaces the oldest entry whose address no waiting request uses; when each entry is in use, the answer is not kept, and the waiting request still gets it. Before, the 17th address replaced the first entry in silence. Three host tests: not asked, a false answer for the gateway, a full table with entries in use.
207. **The window of `net` follows `DESIGN.md` where it is cheap now**: rectangles only, positions on the grid of 8 pixels, the colors of the rules (background, card for one band of two), the name of a case and of a measure in the dim color, a value in the text color, the answer of a case in the color of a program that runs when it is the expected one and in the color "failed" with the words "not expected" when it is not. No card, title or caption yet: the window keeps its form.
208. **The time page has the frequency of the cycle counter.** `Time_Record.cycle_frequency` is the cycles in one second, as the kernel measured them at boot; `system.cycle_frequency()` reads it with no kernel entry. The reason is a failure of the machine test: with a host load average of 24, `limits` printed "used of limit: 72550 of 50000, 273981 of 200000, 841881 of 600000, the program 2121319 of 1500000", each budget about 1.4 times its limit. The kernel was right: it counts processor time with the cycle counter and gives a budget its part of the true length of a period. The program was wrong: it divided that time by an interval in milliseconds of the time page, which count ticks, and the ticks came late. `limits` now measures its interval with the cycle counter. The frequency is a fact of the machine that a program can already measure (the cycle counter against the milliseconds), so the page gives no new knowledge (P20), and a program no longer needs the machine with the right Inspect only to measure. Gap: the milliseconds of the time page still lose time when ticks come late.
209. **The notice of a virtqueue is the number of the queue.** `virtio.notify` wrote 0 to the notice register of each queue. The rule of virtio 1.x (4.1.5.2) is the number of the queue. QEMU finds the queue from the address of the register and ignores the value, so the send queue of the frame service (queue 1) worked in each test. The queue now keeps its number and writes it. Found by the read of Review 8, after the merge of step 40.
210. **The kernel lock is split by data** (owner decision "Full queues and speed", item 8). This replaces choice 30. `ARCHITECTURE.md`, "Locks of the kernel", has the table of all kernel data, with who reads and writes it, from which path, and the lock or the rule that protects it. Eight kinds of lock, in one order: control, program (one for each program), origin, region (one for each memory object), frame, log, wait (one for each bucket of the wait table), run (one for each processor). A processor takes a lock only if it holds no lock of a later kind; the kernel checks it at each take and stops the machine with a report if not. So no deadlock exists by construction. The check costs about 10 % of a cheap kernel entry (measured: 2.19 to 2.40 million entries in one second with one locker without it, 1.51 to 2.07 million with it); it stays on.
211. **A lock is a ticket lock.** Processors get it in the order of their request. With the old lock (one exchange in a loop), the two threads of the word wait made almost no progress while two lockers entered the kernel. A processor that waits for a lock answers `processor_sync`, so that two processors that synchronize each other do not wait for ever.
212. **The control lock is the one coarse lock, for the paths that cross programs**: create, start and stop a program; create and stop a thread; give a handle to another program; map or unmap in another program; revoke; close a handle of a program, a thread, a budget, an interrupt or ports; create a budget or a device object; read the state or the report of another program; the stop on a fault. It stays in the final design: these paths reach an unbounded set of programs (a revocation follows the origins through any program, a stop closes handles that stop other programs), one order among them makes each one atomic for the others, and they are rare (property 3). An operation asks for it before it changes anything, and runs again with it (`STATUS_RETRY`, never seen by a program). Measured: in the measures with 4 lockers the control lock is taken 1 time with only the desktop (by another program), 53 to 64 times in the machine test (the reports of `monitor`), and it waited 0 times; the lockers never take it.
213. **A lock for each program** protects its handles, its mappings and its page table. An operation of a program on its own objects takes it and nothing global: two programs never wait for each other there. Two program locks at one time only under the control lock. The lock is re-entrant on one processor (a stop inside a close of the same program), and it is out of the program record, so a new program in a slot does not reset it.
214. **A running thread belongs to its processor.** Another processor that stops it sets `stop_requested` and sends an interrupt; the processor ends the thread itself. Before, the stopper wrote 0 in the current thread of the other processor and waited for it in `processor_sync`, which needed the one lock (choice 32; `processor_sync` now only makes processors reload their page table). A program ends when the last processor that runs one of its threads leaves it: `holds` counts them, and the stop adds a mark in the same atomic addition, so exactly one processor cleans the program up, after no processor uses its page table.
215. **Pages in one section of the frame lock.** The frame lock protects the frames, the reserved pages, the pages of the budgets and the slots of the memory objects. Each move of pages is one section, so `System_Report` reads "free pages = pages of budgets + reserved pages" exactly, at any time, and `locks` checks it with four programs that create and free memory. A frame is zero filled after the section. A page fault takes the lock of its program in place of the kernel lock: the five points of choice 132 hold with it. A lock for each memory object gives a page on first use one frame when two programs fault on it at one time.
216. **The wait table.** A thread that waits on a word has an entry in one of 64 buckets, chosen by the physical address of the word, each with its wait lock. A wake looks at one bucket, not at all threads. An entry carries the wait serial of its thread, which changes when the wait ends: a late wake of an old wait does not count a thread that runs, so `Word_Wake(word, 1)` does not lose its one wake on a thread that does not wait.
217. **The tick takes no global lock.** It adds the processor time to the budgets with atomic additions. The log limit is one word with the second and the count; a record is accepted only if each budget of the chain counted it under its limit. A budget that ends keeps its parent in its slot, and the slot serves again only after `processor_sync`: a tick that walks through it has finished.
218. **The origin tree has its own lock.** A revocation holds the control lock, sets `revoking`, and ends each holding under the lock of its program, after it checked that the tree did not change. While `revoking` is set, a map or a copy with no control lock does not add a holding: it runs again after the revocation. So a program cannot keep a holding by copying it during a revocation.
219. **Ports.** A processor writes only its own port bitmap, in `run`, when the program or its `ports_serial` changed. A change of the Ports handles of a program adds 1 to `ports_serial` and synchronizes all processors. Before, a processor wrote the bitmap of another processor.
220. **The scheduler has one run queue for each processor** (`kernel/schedule.odin`). The choice of the next thread is in `queue_pick` and `schedule_pick`: the step of the scheduling strategies changes them. A processor takes a thread from another queue when that processor has a higher load than its own queue: the first rule (a difference of 2) failed one test run of three ("at most 3 of 4 processors ran the program at the same time"), with two threads of `processors` in one queue.
221. **The measures and the counts** (program `locks`, `kerneltool measure`, choice of the owner "the test measures"). Before the split (the kernel of `master`, three runs with only the desktop): kernel entries in one second 2.53 to 2.70 million with 1 locker, 3.54 to 3.68 with 2, 2.78 to 3.25 with 4; pages on first use 1.42 to 1.61 million, 1.96 to 2.03, 1.62 to 1.82; the word wait 60 000 to 64 000 wakes in one second alone, 94 000 to 116 000 with two lockers. After the split (three runs): kernel entries 1.51 to 2.07 million, 3.31 to 3.79, 6.68 to 7.24; pages on first use 1.15 to 1.37 million, 1.84 to 2.00, 3.32 to 3.63; the word wait 466 000 to 556 000 alone, 227 000 to 327 000 with two lockers; 0 wakes lost. In the machine test (with the other programs of the scenario, three runs): kernel entries 1.06 to 1.56 million, 1.55 to 3.09, 3.64 to 4.38 (before: 1.70 to 1.97, 2.76 to 3.46, 2.30 to 2.78); pages on first use 0.69 to 0.97 million, 0.64 to 1.81, 1.28 to 2.05. What scales: entries on own objects scale with the processors (4 lockers: 3.5 times 1 locker); the lockers wait 0 to 3 times for a program lock in a measure. What does not scale: one cheap entry costs more than before (more locks, the order check); pages on first use scale less (2.8 times with 4), because the frame lock is one lock for all the frames: it waited in 16 to 26 % of its takes with 4 lockers; the word wait with two lockers in the machine test falls to 186 to 2 690 wakes in one second, because a woken thread waits in its queue for the end of a slice when each processor runs a thread (a gap of the scheduler). The test checks the counts, not the speeds.
222. **A defer inside an `if` block** ran at the end of the block, not of the procedure: the first version of `locks` waited for its lockers before its measure of the word wait, and measured 0 wakes. Found and corrected before the split; the numbers of `master` above are from the corrected program.

223. **A review found seven faults that the tests did not reach; each one was real** (2026-11-10). (1) A thread was Ready, in no queue and on no processor between two sections of the run lock: a stop ended it, the cleanup freed the page table, and a processor ran it. Now `queue_pick` takes the thread and gives it to the processor in one section. (2) The close of the last handle freed the slot of a Stopped program whose thread still ran; its cleanup then freed it a second time. Now a slot is free only after the cleanup (`cleaned`). (3) `Thread_Create` made a thread in a program that a stop had marked. Now it gives `Bad_State`. (4) A processor that ended its thread finished it after a wait for the control lock; in that wait the cleanup could finish it and a new thread take the slot. Now the serial of the thread is checked. (5) The references of a budget were a plain count under the control lock, and a copy of a Budget handle added 1 atomically with no control lock. Now the count is atomic. (6) The undo of a mapping that failed on a later page cleared entries with no `processor_sync` (also on `master` before the split). Now it synchronizes as `unmap` does. (7) `program_event` of a program could read a slot of a finished thread with the old program and the new `events` of a thread of another program. Now `thread_finish` writes `program` 0 under the program lock. The search for the same three forms (`ARCHITECTURE.md`, "Three rules") found two more: (8) the revocation checked the tree from the time of its first step, not from the choice of the holding, so it could close a new handle in the slot of a closed one; (9) `Thread_Stop` and `Program_Stop` returned while a stopped thread could still run some instructions on its processor (since the split): `thread_end` of the library then unmapped the stack under a running thread. Now the operation waits, with no lock, until the threads left their processors (`stop_wait`). And `stack_guard`, in a report, could make a table of the kernel half: now it only reads.
224. **The stress case of `locks`.** After the measures, 40 rounds of 3 churn lockers (`locker` with the role Churn): each one has two threads that compute, and a first thread that makes threads that end at once and stops and makes again a computing thread. The second and the third locker of a round stop the one before them after 1 to 4 ms. `locks` stops each one, or a second thread of `locks` stops it while the first thread closes the last handle, or the close of the last handle stops it. At the end the slots of programs and threads are back in the budget of `locks` exactly, and the free pages are the pages of the budgets and the reserved pages. The budget of `locks` is short of 2 pages: page tables of `locks` itself (the loader maps each module in it at a new address, and `Memory_Unmap` does not free an empty table); the check permits fewer than 30 pages (a quarter of one page for each stopped program). With the check of finding (2) removed, one run of two failed.
225. **The window of `locks` follows `DESIGN.md` where it is cheap**: rectangles only, positions and lines on the grid of 8 pixels, the colors of the rules (background, card for one band of two), the name of a row in the dim color and a value in the text color, the result in the color of a program that runs, or in the color "failed". No card, title or caption yet.
226. **A second read of the corrections found one more fault.** `program_stop` returned at once for a program that another stop had marked, so a second `Program_Stop`, or the close of the last handle, returned while a thread of the program could still run on its processor. The second stop now records the program as the first one does, and `stop_wait` waits until the threads left. `stacks_free` no longer makes a kernel page table while it frees. The coordinator corrected both from the report of the reviewer.
227. **The error record: one form of 56 bytes, seven Integer fields** (`contract/errors`; owner, 2026-11-10: errors that diagnose, no exception, no global `errno`). The fields: kind, unit, operation, argument, value, limit, found. 56 bytes are the values of a completion record, so a kernel operation needs no other memory. Integer fields only make it a described record (`module.Record`): the console and any language read it with its description. It has no field for the source: the caller knows where it called. It has no name: the names of a kernel operation and of its parameters are in the operation table, those of an export in its description. The design is section 8 of `MODULE_CONTRACT.md`.
228. **A kernel operation gives its error record in its completion record.** The direction told to the owner was "a record for the thread, in memory of the thread". The completion record is in the kernel ring of the thread, so it is memory of the thread, and it is the answer of one command: a batch with several failures has one record for each, and no value outside the call exists (the defect of `errno`). The kernel finds the record after the operation failed (`kernel/diagnose.odin`, about 400 lines): it reads the form of each parameter in the operation table and checks the parameters in order, as the operation does. The operations do not change; the success path has one more comparison of the status, in `enter`. An operation that the table does not explain gives the kind `Refused` with the status.
229. **One operation table** (`contract/operation/forms.odin`): the name of each operation and the name and form of each parameter (a handle of a kind with rights, a handle or 0, an address, a count with a limit, flags, a choice). The kernel reads the forms; a program reads the names for the sentence.
230. **A handle slot keeps how its handle ended**: closed or revoked, in a byte of the slot that was padding, until a new handle takes the slot. A handle of the generation before then gives "was closed" or "was revoked"; an older handle gives "ended, and its place has a newer handle".
231. **No ambient fact in a record** (P20): the record names the handles of the caller and what it gave. It gives what a budget holds only when the handle has the right Read (else the kind `Budget_Hidden`), and the state of a program only when the handle has Read. A failure of `Handle_Give` through memory that no one other program maps or holds says so, and names no program.
232. **An export declares its error record** as its last parameter, `error: ^errors.Error_Record`, after the buffer of a result. `Function.error_record` (the field `reserved_details` before, zero in old files) is the index of its description. A direct call has one more register; an isolated call has one more field of the argument record: the offset of 56 bytes in the call region, before the buffer of a result. The receiver gives the export the address in the region: the export only writes it, so no copy is needed. The export writes the record when it fails, and only then; it fails when its integer result is negative; an export with a Record result has none. The receiver writes the number of the export in `operation`. The check of a replacement compares the error records (`Difference.Error_Record`).
233. **The storage service gives an error record for each of its 8 answers.** `read` now fails with -7 or -8, in place of an empty text that `size` explained; a wrong name gives -3 in `size` and `read` too, where it gave -7. The client `support/storage` has the status `Failed` and gives the record to its program; `notes` writes the sentence on the log. The answer -4 (more than 32 blocks) cannot come from an isolated caller: the call region (64 KiB) holds less than 32 blocks (128 KiB).
234. **The information exports of `manual` give error records**: `exports`, `imports`, `importers`, `handles`, `slots`, `segments` and `running`, with the kinds Absent, Malformed (the kind of the problem and the byte of the file), No_Grant and Refused. The documentation of each export names its kinds, and `manual` shows it.
235. **The console writes the sentence**: "error: storage.write: the argument name is empty; it must have 1 to 39 bytes (-3)". The number stays at the end: it is the answer of the export.
236. **The load error stays its own record** (`module.Load_Error`, 392 bytes). It holds the names and the hashes of the modules of a load, which the caller of a call host does not have; 56 bytes cannot hold them. It has the same form of answer: values and fixed texts that the caller owns, no pointer, zero for no error.
237. **Each module keeps its frame pointers** (rule 8 of `MODULE_CONTRACT.md`, not enforced before). Odin has no option for it, and with `-o:speed` it kept `rbp` in 76 of 158 functions of `console`. The kernel tool now asks Odin for LLVM IR, and compiles it with `llc` of LLVM 18, the LLVM of the Odin compiler, with `--frame-pointer=all`. The flake has `llvmPackages_18.llvm` for it. Measured: `console` keeps `rbp` in 147 of 153 functions (the 6 others are leaf functions in assembly); its code is 172 773 bytes, 0.45 % more; with `--function-sections`, `fault` lost 27 % of its code, and `kernel`, `manual`, `root` and `storage` lost 0.2 % to 1.7 %. The build of all the modules takes 37 s, as before.
238. **The stack trace of a fault** (`op.Stop_Report`, 880 bytes, 800 more): the kernel walks the frame pointers of the thread that faulted, 16 frames at most. It stops at an `rbp` that is 0, not aligned on 8, not above the one before, or not memory that the program can read. Each frame has the address and the base and hash of the module record that holds it. The console prints one line for each frame of a call host that faulted.
239. **The names of the functions come from a tool.** A module names its exports only; the names of its other functions are debug information, in a separate object (decision 5 of `MODULE_CONTRACT.md`), which the system does not load yet. The kernel tool reads them in `build/modules/<name>/linked.elf`, where the offset of the module is the address: the step `Trace` of the machine test names each frame of the trace of `fault` and checks `fault::three fault::two fault::one entry`.
240. **The program `errors`**: one case for each kind of error, 19 cases, run at its start and again by a key or a click. Each case makes the error for real and shows the sentence of the record. `math.square_root` is a module export with an error record, for the direct call; `fault` now writes to address 0 three calls deep.
241. **Measured** (program `errors`, in the machine test, 200 000 kernel entries, the least of 3 runs, on a computer where other machine tests ran): a kernel operation that succeeds (`Handle_State`) took 540 ns, one that fails with its error record (`Memory_Size` on a budget) 525 ns. The time of a kernel entry hides the cost of the record. A direct call of `square_root` that succeeds took 39 ns, one that fails 3 ns: the failure returns before the work. Before and after this step (the machine test of `master` two times, of this branch one time, on the same loaded computer): a copy of a handle and its close (`origin`, 2 kernel entries) took 1250 and 1100 ns before, 1000 ns after; `clock` made 2 124 942 and 2 015 348 kernel entries in one second before, 1 779 861 after; a call through an import slot (`imports`) took 3.6 and 3.7 ns before, 4.1 ns after; the picture of `processors` with 1 thread took 443 and 446 ms before, 438 ms after. The two measures of the kernel entry move in opposite directions by up to 20 %: these runs do not show a cost of the success path, and they do not prove its absence to better than 20 %. The success path of the kernel has one more comparison of the status. The call through a slot is about 0.4 ns slower: the frame pointer of the called export (a push and a pop).
242. **The wrappers give zero values and the record on a failure** (found by the coordinator at the reading of the branch). The record in the values of a completion made each wrapper give a false result on a failure: `memory_create` gave a handle that was not 0, `memory_size` the kind of the record as a count of pages, and `ring_open` trusted it. Now `system.call` gives zero results when the status is not Ok, and `system.call_with_error` and the last parameter `error` of each wrapper give the record in memory of the caller. The cost on success: none, the branch is on the status that the call already reads. `block`, `frame`, `origin` and the root service read results of `system.call` without the status: they read 0 on a failure, as before the step. The program `errors` uses the wrappers.
243. **The window of `errors` follows `DESIGN.md` where it is cheap**: rectangles only, the colors of section 4 (a name dim, a value and the sentence of a record in the text color, a wrong answer in the color "failed"), each position and size on the grid of 8 pixels, and a caption that names the records (`errors.Error_Record`, `module.Load_Error`, `op.Stop_Report.frames`). Not done: cards with titles and the gradient of a surface; the constants of the design are in `support/bootscreen`, not in `support/graphics`.
244. **The reads of `diagnose` take the locks of the lock split** (choices 210 to 226). Each `read_*` takes the locks that the table of `ARCHITECTURE.md` names, in the lock order, and frees them before it returns; `diagnose` runs with no lock held (the test build checks it), and only on a failure, so a success takes no lock of it. A read through a handle keeps the program lock of the caller while it reads the object: the slot cannot serve another object. The reads that take the control lock: a budget, the log count, the state of another program. `Handle_Slot.ended` is written under the program lock: `handle_close` runs in `command_run`, in `holding_end` and in `program_cleanup`, each under the program lock. The stack trace runs in `program_fault`, under the control lock and the program lock of the program that faulted: `user_range` and `user_copy` run under that program lock, as the rule of the page tables asks. `program.stop` is written there, and read by `Program_State` under the control lock and the program lock of the program. A bug found on the way: after the merge, the log count read the word of the lock split (the second and the count) as a count; it reads the count of this second now.
245. **A case under contention** (program `errors`): for 500 ms, two threads make failures (a map to write of a copy with Read, 64 pages of a budget of 2, the size of a handle that does not exist) on handles that a third thread closes and makes again. The program checks that each record explains its status, as section 8 of `MODULE_CONTRACT.md` states. The lock-order check of the test build is on. The log has "errors: under contention: N failures from 2 threads while a third thread closed and made N handles; 0 records contradict their status".
246. **A refusal takes no global lock that it does not need** (finding E1 of an independent read). After the lock split, each refused `Log` took the control lock and the frame lock to read the count of its budget, and each refused `Memory_Create` the control lock: a program over its log limit that logs in a loop made the page faults of all programs wait for the frame lock. Now the count of `Log` is read under the program lock only: the handle with Read keeps the budget live, `program.budget` is an atomic word that changes only when that budget ends (`budget_release` stores it atomically now), and the limit of a live budget never changes; the table of the locks in `ARCHITECTURE.md` says it. A refused `Memory_Create` reads the pages under the program lock and the frame lock, with no control lock. Measured by `errors` (100 000 refusals each, the counts of `System_Report.locks` of all the processors during the loop, one run each, no other machine test on the host): a refused `Log` took 530 ns with the control lock taken 100 035 times and the frame lock 100 059 times before, 520 ns with 29 and 60 after; a refused `Memory_Create` took 480 ns with the control lock 100 006 times and the frame lock 200 001 times before, 470 ns with 12 and 200 001 after (one frame lock in the operation, one in the record). A refused `Log` needs a program with a log limit of 0: `errors` starts a copy of itself with that budget, which measures and writes the time in a page that the two map.
247. **A kind is written only when its cause holds now** (finding E2): a `Budget` record has a limit above what the budget holds, an `Over_Limit` record of `Log` a count above the limit, and the other kinds that were written with no check (`Overlap`, `Absent` of `Memory_Unmap`, `Bad_Address` of a range or of a word) read their cause again; else the record is `Refused` with the status. The case under contention of `errors` checks the stronger promise. A full table is named only when it is full now and is one cause that the state shows (E3): `Thread_Create` tests the thread areas of the program, its mappings, the memory objects, then the thread table; `Memory_Create`, `Program_Create`, `Budget_Create` and `Device_Create` test their table; a table of another program gives `Refused`. Without Read on a budget, the record depends on the operation alone (E5). The first stop of a program keeps its code, its report and its stack trace: a fault of a program that a stop or another fault already stopped writes nothing (E4; the overwrite was on `master` before this step). A closed handle of the generation 0xFFFF is `Handle_Ended` (its slot has the generation 0).
248. **A table of the machine is named only where it is the one cause.** After the correction of finding E3, `slot_explain` took the control lock on each `No_Slot` failure and told the caller of `Thread_Create` which of two tables of the machine was full (memory objects or threads). The status does not give that fact (P20). Now the record of `Thread_Create` and `Program_Start` names a table of the caller only, else `Refused`; the control lock is taken only for `Program_Create`, `Budget_Create` and `Device_Create`. Found by the read of the coordinator before the merge.
249. **The milliseconds of the time page come from the cycle counter.** Before, the first processor added 1 at each tick: a tick that came late lost time, and under QEMU the time of the system ran slow. Now the tick writes `(cycle counter - cycle_start) / cycle_frequency`, in milliseconds (`milliseconds_now`): a late tick loses nothing, and the value is at most one tick old. The seconds and the rest are divided apart, so no product overflows. The kernel word `ticks` is now `time_milliseconds`; the deadlines of the threads, the second of the log limit and the time of a kernel entry read it. A budget period starts at the first tick 100 ms or more after the period before (`period_time`), not when the count is a multiple of 100: the count can now pass over a multiple.
250. **The time page has the cycle counter at the time 0** (`Time_Record.cycle_start`), beside the frequency. `system.nanoseconds()` reads the cycle counter and these two constants: a time finer than a millisecond, with no kernel entry and no wait for a tick. It assumes what the kernel already assumes for the budgets: the cycle counters of the processors agree. `clock` compares each read of the page with that time for half a second. It fails if the least that the page was behind is more than 1 ms: just after a tick the page is less than one tick behind, so more is lost time. It fails if the page was more than 1 ms ahead. The most that the page was behind is written and not checked: it is how late a tick came, which is the load of the host (13 ms in one run with a host load of 35).
251. **Three corrections in the test of the errors** (found by the read of the coordinator after the merge of step 42). The time of a failed direct call was measured with a loop of 4 to 6 ms on the clock of 1 ms: now `system.nanoseconds()`. The case under contention accepted each record `Refused`: now it counts them, writes the count, and fails when they are half of the failures or more. The LLVM IR that Odin writes in the root of the repository for a moment is in `.gitignore`.
252. **The host encodes, the remote service relays** (`MODULE_CONTRACT.md`, "A call from the host"). The host has the module files, so it has the description of each export: the tool parses the text of a call, checks it against the description and writes the argument record with `isolated.encode`. The remote service reads no description: it copies the bytes at offset 0 of its call region, sends the command record, and sends back the bytes of the places that the request names. The service checks the call region as for any caller. A relay that interprets would need a second reader of the descriptions in the machine, and a way to send names and types to it.
253. **The format `contract/remote`**: a request header of 104 bytes and an answer header of 128 bytes, fixed, little-endian, no text but names of 32 bytes. An answer has parts of 1344 bytes at most, with the total and the offset of each part: a result of the whole call region (64 KiB) is 49 datagrams. The request gives the unit of the integer result in the place of the result (1 for a Text, the size of a record for a Table, 0 for a Record); the remote service sends `min(result, count / unit) * unit` bytes, so a false count of the service sends no byte out of the place. A refusal of the remote service is an error record whose argument is the field of the request (`REQUEST_FIELD_NAMES`). Unit tests: a round trip, each refused field, the bytes of a result, the parts of an answer, and 200 000 random datagrams.
254. **A request number, and the last answer kept.** The tool sends a request again after 300 ms with no complete answer, and gives up after 10 s. The remote service keeps the last answer (64 KiB and its header): a request with its number gets it again, and the call does not run two times, so `storage.write` from the host makes one commit. The first number of the tool comes from the clock, so a new run of `kerneltool call` does not meet the number of the answer that the remote service keeps.
255. **The path through the peer.** The remote service sends to one remote, the host at 10.0.2.2 port 7100, the address of the gateway: the peer of the tool is that host. It learns the port of the endpoint from the first datagram of the remote service (the status Started), receives the call requests of the tool on a UDP socket of the loopback (port 7101 with `kerneltool run`, a port of the system in the test), and gives each datagram of the remote service to the program that sent the last call request. No new device and no second network: the machine has one network device. The peer sends the six malformed call requests once the remote service started, as it sends the malformed frames to the network service.
256. **The grant Connect** of the root service: a ring to the root service that serves Connect and Disconnect only, to the services of `callable_of` (`remote`: `manual`, `monitor`, `storage`). The ring of the console serves each command (`Root_Ring`); a program with Connect cannot start, stop, revoke or replace. The remote service connects at the first call to a service, so a service can start after it; a service that stops ends its connection, and the next call connects again. A call has 5 s: after it, the remote service ends the connection (Disconnect) and answers `Over_Limit` in milliseconds.
257. **The grant acts_for_principal, and its revocation.** The remote service acts for the principal (`isolated.CALLER_ACTS_FOR_PRINCIPAL`): the developer at the host holds the machine, and a smaller grant makes the tool see less than the console. The grant is now a recorded grant of the root service for each program (`Root_Grant.Acts_For_Principal`, "acts_for_principal"): `grants("console")` shows it too, and `revoke(name, "acts_for_principal")` sends `Service_Command.Grant` with the new grant to each service that the program is connected to; a later connection has no such grant either. UDP has no authentication: see the gaps.
258. **The monitor becomes a service** with four exports: `programs()`, `counts()`, `count(name)` and `served()`. Reason: the monitor already holds the machine with the right Inspect and reads these reports for its window, and the owner named `monitor.programs()`; a new program would need the same grant again. The first three give facts of other programs and of the machine, so only to a caller that acts for the principal (P20, line 1); `served()` gives facts of the monitor itself, to each caller. A Record result cannot fail (section 4), so the counts of the machine are a Table of named counts, and `count(name)` gives one of them as an Integer with an error record. The monitor serves the calls between its pictures: it waits on its connections and its window until the next picture.
259. **The sentences compile on the host** (`support/sentence`): the writer of texts, the names of the rights and types, and the sentences of the error records moved out of `support/system`, which keeps each name as an alias (`system.Writer`, `system.error_text`, ...). `support/sentence` calls no kernel operation, so the kernel tool compiles it: the tool prints the sentence of an error record with the code of the machine, not a second copy.
260. **Two units of the error record**: `Connection` ("a connection to this service", for `No_Grant`) and `Milliseconds`.
261. **The window of `remote`** follows `DESIGN.md`: three cards (the calls of the host with the large count, the connections with the color of the hash of each module, the last calls with their state in the color of a program or "failed" and the same fact in words), the grid of points, rectangles only. It draws at most ten times in one second, so a burst of calls does not wait for it. The window names an export by its number: the remote service does not read descriptions. The time of one picture is on the log: 119 us in the last full check ("remote: the window drew one picture in 119 us").
262. **The machine test**: the step `Call` (`expected`, `outcome`, `rows_logged`, `repeat`, `parts`) and `kerneltool test network`, which runs the machine of the network only. The Wait of "root: revoked the grant network of net" is `any_order`: the network service can write its line first (seen one time in this step).
263. **One table of the design rules in `support/graphics`** (`rules.odin`): the grid (8), the line (16), the gap, the margin and the title of a card, the three surfaces, the ten colors of the concepts, the colors of the title bars, and `mix`, `shade`, `block`, `gradient`. The boot display, the compositor, `errors`, `locks`, `net` and `remote` take all their rule colors from it; `log` and `pages` take the background, the text and the dim color. The colors of a program that are not colors of the rules stay in the program: `docs/DESIGN.md`, "State", lists them.
264. **The desktop follows the rules** (`docs/DESIGN.md`, section 9): the background of the rules with its grid of points, computed once; a bar of 32 pixels with the surface of a card; buttons of 24 pixels; the two menus as cards with a title, the caption of their source and a count; the menu of the programs in two columns (31 lines of 22 pixels filled the screen: 32 lines of 24 do not fit); a pointer of squares of 2 pixels; no shadow. The close button is a square of 24 pixels at the right end of the title bar, with the character `x`, not red: red is "failed". Its center is still 14 pixels from the right and 12 from the top, and the title bar is still 24 pixels: the machine test presses the same places. The places of new windows are not changed: the main scenario presses fixed places in them.
265. **The gradient of the title bar** (owner, 2026-11-10; reading of 2026-11-11: "the current blue" is the blue of the title bar, `0x3F7CC4`). The title bar of the window that has the focus goes from `0x3F7CC4` at its left to `0x162C45` (36 %) at its right; the other title bars go from `0x2A3442` to `0x161D27`, with a dim title: the gradient also says which window has the keyboard. The compositor computes the colors of one line once, for the columns that the frame shows, with a fixed-point step for each channel (no division for each pixel), then copies the line to the 24 lines. At its start it measures three forms of a title bar of 1024 pixels, 64 times each, and writes them on the log: one color 1.6 to 2.7 us, a mix for each pixel 65 to 87 us, one row of colors then copies 5.7 to 6.8 us (10 machines of one check). The gradient costs about 4 us for each window that a frame draws. At 8 bits for each channel the steps are 41 levels of red, 80 of green and 126 of blue over the width of a window: the picture shows no band, so there is no dithering.
266. **`graphics.copy` does not call `memcpy`.** The first measures showed a full frame of 9.1 ms with no window and 26.6 ms with 15 to 17 windows, and 21.5 ms for each frame of the move of a large window (`boot`, `log`, `manual`, `paint`), and one row of colors copied to 24 lines slower than a mix for each pixel (109 us against 65 us). The cause: in a program of this system, `memcpy` is the one of the freestanding Odin runtime (`base:runtime`), a loop of one byte, compiled with no optimization (each step reads and writes its counter in memory). `graphics.copy` copied each line of each window and of the background with it. `copy_pixels` now copies 16 bytes at a time; its store is volatile, so that the compiler does not make the loop a call of `memcpy` again. After: a full frame with one window 1.0 to 1.2 ms, with 15 to 17 windows 1.5 to 3.2 ms (mean 2.1 ms), a frame of the move of a large window 1.4 to 2.1 ms (mean 1.6 ms); the move of `life` 0.9 ms for each frame (3.4 ms before). The compositor writes these numbers on the log: "compositor: a full frame of 1024x768 with N windows in N us" (the first frame, and the frame after a window is put down) and "compositor: the move of NAME: N frames, N us per frame, N us the longest".
267. **The launcher** (owner, 2026-11-10, "as an experiment"; reading of 2026-11-11): a panel in the middle of the screen that the key super opens. The person types a part of a name; the list narrows at each key: a name matches if it contains the part, without the case of the letters, and the names that start with it come first (`bar.filter`, with five host tests). The arrows move the choice, Enter starts the program, Escape, the key super or a press of the pointer close it. With no match the list says so and Enter starts nothing. The key super is the left key with the logo (scan code E0 5B, `display.KEY_SUPER`): no program uses it, and the compositor sees each key before the program that has the focus, so it keeps this key, and each key while the launcher is open. The launcher lists `bar.names`, the table of the menu of the programs, and starts a program as the menu does: the record `Start` with the name to the root service. It has no other power. The menu of the programs stays. The log has "compositor: the launcher is open", "compositor: the launcher lists N of M for "p": ...", "compositor: the launcher chooses NAME", "compositor: the launcher starts NAME", "compositor: the launcher is closed by ...". The machine test has a step `Keys`: keys with no Enter.
268. **`support/launcher` is now `support/bar`** (one term for one concept: "launcher" names the panel of 267 now). The package has the names of the programs, the places of the bar and of its two menus, and the filter of the launcher. The picture of the menu of the programs is `menu`; `launcher` is the picture of the launcher. The picture `desktop` comes from the machine of the network, with four windows that the tool places; the main scenario keeps its picture as `desktop_moved`.

269. **A strategy is a table of six functions over the data of a run queue** (`kernel/strategy.odin`; owner, 2026-11-10: "study CFS, EEVDF and `sched_ext`", "change between the strategies"; the research note is `docs/SCHEDULING.md`).
