It was removed. AVX2 comes back only after the kernel saves the extended state, with a form that wins by measure on the machine and the cause of the wrong bytes understood.
472. **A vector form gives the bytes of its scalar form for every input**: the same integer arithmetic in lanes, no other rounding, no load or store past the slice of the caller (the last values of a row go through the scalar form). Host tests compare the SSE2 form with the scalar form on inputs from a fixed seed: every length from 0 to 67, every place against 16 bytes, the edge values; a failure gives the first wrong pixel, both values, and the pixels changed outside the drawn columns.
473. **The blend of a glyph in SSE2** (`support/graphics/blend_vectors_amd64.odin`): 4 pixels at a time, one lane of 16 bits for each byte; the squares, the products, the index and the choice of each channel and pixel are vector code. The table of square roots stays, read one byte at a time (SSE2 has no gather). The comparison found a fault of the scalar form: a first pixel `0xFFFFFFFF` was blended as black (no canvas holds it); it is corrected.
474. **The blend of the pointer picture in SSE2**; the fill, the copy and the swap of red and blue are not written again: the copy is `runtime/memory.asm`, and the compiler already makes the fill and the swap vector loops (seen in the code of the compositor).
475. **BLAKE3 with four chunks side by side** (`contract/blake3/chunks_side_by_side_amd64.odin`): a subtree of 4 chunks has its chunks compressed in the lanes of 16 SSE2 vectors, with a transpose of the words of each block; the parents and every other part of the tree stay scalar.
476. **No SSSE3, SSE4.1 or AVX-512 form**: their gain is a few instructions in loops whose cost is the reads of the table or the rounds, and each needs a test of the processor (`docs/SIMD.md` says what AVX-512 needs in the kernel). The arithmetic is `base:intrinsics` on `#simd` types, and a module stays compiled for the baseline. A lesson recorded for a later AVX2 form: a vector of more than 128 bits passes between a procedure with the target feature and one without it only through a pointer.
477. **The line editor is a package with no state** (`support/line_editor`): a `Line` record (200 bytes, a count, a caret) in memory of the program, and procedures that take one key and change the record. Each procedure brings the count and the caret back inside the bytes first: a record that the caller wrote wrong cannot make it write past an array. The package has no kernel call and does not know the codes of a keyboard (`Edit_Key`), so host tests run each key. The console keeps the line, its history and its list; the terminal still has no line editor (choice 90).
478. **The completion list is a fuzzy match with a score** (`docs/CONSOLE.md`, section 3): the characters of the query in order, gaps allowed; +16 a character, +32 at the start of the name, +24 after `.` or `_`, +16 side by side, -3 and -1 a byte for a gap; a tie goes to the tie order (the age of a history line), the shorter name, the order of the bytes. One pass forward and one back give the shortest window that ends at the first full match: bounded work (320 steps for a name), not the best window. The test pins the order of five queries over the exports of six programs, so a change of a rule shows.
479. **The list opens while the person types**, at the first character of a name, at once after a `.`, and inside the quotes of the first argument of `load`, `isolate`, `start`, `replace`, `connect`, `stop`, `grants`, `revoke`, `unload`. Tab or Enter takes the chosen name (a command gets its `(`, a name in quotes its closing quote), Up and Down move the choice, Escape closes the list until the next character. One name equal to the query closes the list. Enter on a name that is already the name at the caret runs the line: a line that ends with a variable (`t`, then Enter) runs as before. The common start of several names is not completed: the list replaces the second Tab.
480. **The console sends the rows; the terminal lays them out** (records `List_Row` and `List`, and `Hint` for the line under the edit line). The console knows the names from the descriptions; the terminal owns the cells, the width and the place of the edit line. A list record names a byte of the edit line, not a place on the screen (choice 90). The rows go through the text region of the console and its terminal, which no third program reads. The terminal draws each byte under 32 or over 126 of a list or a hint as `?`.
481. **A cell of a text grid has a fill color** (`filled`, `background`; draw record version 5): the list needs the card color under its text and the blue of the title bar under the chosen row, with matched characters in another color, which `reverse` cannot give. A cell is 12 bytes in place of 8: the view of the terminal (32 x 104 cells) and its two copies grow from 26 624 to 39 936 bytes each, and the difference reads 50 % more bytes for each frame. The border of the list is four records of one pixel after the others.
482. **The bounds of one key**: a name is read for 128 bytes at most, a query of more than 64 bytes matches nothing, one ranking reads at most 4096 names and keeps the best 64; the console reads at most 512 names (its tables are smaller). One key costs at most about 1.3 million steps. The console measures each ranking with the cycle counter and writes the longest one when Tab opens a list.
483. **The line history is a ring of 16 384 bytes for at most 256 lines** (`line_editor.History`): a line takes its own length, and never wraps at the end of the ring, so each line is one slice. The same line two times in a row is kept one time. Up and Down walk it, and the line that the person typed comes back after the newest. The history search is the completion list over the history: Control-R opens it and moves down (older), Control-S moves up (newer); Control-S with no list does nothing; Enter puts the chosen line in the input and does not run it; Escape keeps the line as it was. A line that the history holds two times shows one time in the search (the newest).
484. **The modifiers reach the program of a terminal**: the input service, the compositor and `display.next_event` carried them, and the terminal dropped them. The key record of the terminal has them in `values[1]` (`terminal.Key.modifiers`). The console types no character for Control with a key.
485. **The completion writes on the log only when the person asks** (Tab that opens a list, a take of the list or of the search): the main scenario has about 13 log records of room before the program `log` reads the oldest ones, and each line that it types opens lists.
486. **The completion of a name in quotes uses what the console holds**: the boot image for the modules, `Inspect` on the machine for the programs that run. The root service has no command that lists its services, and the console asks for no new grant: `connect("` lists the programs that run, and the root service still decides. With no grant to inspect, the list names the services that the console is connected to.
487. **The line history across a stop is designed, not built** (`docs/CONSOLE.md`, section 6): one object of the private store of the console (it holds the grant `Store`), written at most each 10 s and at `shutdown()`, read at the start as data that the console did not write; nothing runs on load; a command empties it.

488. **The fast blend of text over a flat color** (`graphics.blend_glyph_rows`, `docs/FONTS.md` section 5): blend tables of 3 x 256 results for each pair of a text color and a pixel color, 8 tables in 24 KiB of the program, each value computed on first use by the formula of the reference; a row of a glyph whose two ends have one color uses its table. Measured on the host (`taskset`, the least of 3 000 batches, the least of four cores): 43.4 of the 128 words of a cell are partial; one glyph at scale 1 over a flat color 109 ns against 181 ns for the reference blend and 33.8 ns for Spleen (3.2 times, 5.4 before); over a gradient across 178 ns. A host test compares each pixel with the reference: 0 differ.
489. **The settings survive a stop**: one record of 48 bytes (`settings.Kept_Settings`) in one object of the private store of `settings`, written before the page; a boot pass of `settings` (no window) reads it and writes the page. The storage service now starts at boot. The boot display uses the defaults: the root service draws it before the storage service runs.
490. **The loss of power for the settings** (`kerneltool test power`): 1000 records, one commit each; 306 images, 72 torn, each gives the durable record (227) or the record in flight (79), never a mix; the next boot pass reads the durable record.
491. **Two more settings, one row of a table each** (`settings.descriptions`): the pointer theme (the compositor receives both theme files and chooses live) and the grid fit (two glyph caches in the boot image, +397 344 bytes). The scheduling strategy is not a setting: the right `Schedule` stays with `scheduler` (P20); the person changes it there.
492. **A bold weight for the titles of the cards**: the ASCII subset of JetBrains Mono Bold (14 212 bytes), a glyph cache at scale 1 only (30 720 bytes); the draw record version 6: byte 3 is `kind_detail`, the thickness of a line (choice 458) and the weight of a text.
493. **The window of `settings` is not covered** in `font_vector`: the scenario moves `monitor` to the left and `settings` to the right.
494. **The context values of the settings**: the boot pass of `settings` is the value 17, and the second pointer theme of the compositor the values 18 and 19 (`CONTEXT_VALUE_SETTINGS_BOOT_PASS`, `CONTEXT_VALUE_SECOND_POINTER_THEME_ADDRESS`, `CONTEXT_VALUE_SECOND_POINTER_THEME_SIZE` in `contract/operation`): the values 9 to 16 are the random seed, the settings page, the zero address of `fault`, the date page and the strength of the seed.
495. **Paper Mono is the font of the system** (decision of the owner: "Paper mono très bien pour monospace"; `docs/FONTS.md`, section 6): the default vector font (`DEFAULT_VECTOR_FONT` `paper_mono`) and its SemiBold for the titles (`EMPHASIS_VECTOR_FONT`), fetched by the flake from its repository at one commit with the hash of each file (it is not in nixpkgs). Its advance is 606 of 1000 units: an em of 13.2 pixels on the grid of 8 x 16; the baseline is row 12, its capitals 9 rows. `i` and `j` are glyphs made of glyphs, which the subset makes simple. It has the box-drawing characters; the glyph cache keeps ASCII only in this step. JetBrains Mono stays a candidate and the font of the host tests of the renderer.
496. **One table of the fixed ranges** (`contract/fixed_addresses`): the address and the span of each mapping that the system gives a program (the files of a module, the pointer themes, the settings page, the three glyph caches, the date page, the storage file, the tables of an export), one slot of 1 TiB each from 0x3000_0000_0000; the compiler checks that no two overlap. Each owner takes its address from it. The storage file moves from 0x3100_0000_0000, where the pointer themes also were, to 0x3700_0000_0000. Two pages that a program places by its own choice move out of the band: the measure page of `errors` (it was at the address of the settings page) and the objects of a locker (at the address of the module files).
497. **B612, the interface font, as subsets** (decision of the owner: "Go b612"; `docs/FONTS.md`, section 12): Regular and Bold from its repository at one commit, with the hash of each file; the subsets keep the 232 and 239 pair adjustments of `kern` between ASCII characters. The reader of `kern` (version 0, a first subtable of format 0) checks each count and offset, the order of the pairs, and each glyph; the reader of `hmtx` now checks its length for every glyph. Host tests: the advances of 12 characters and 7 pairs against the values read by hand, a damaged `kern` table.
498. **The glyph cache version 2**, one format for monospace and proportional fonts: positions for each scale, a box for each glyph, the advances and the pairs in 1/64 of a pixel; B612 Regular with 3 positions at scale 1 and 1 at the scales 2 and 3 (408 524 bytes), B612 Bold at scale 1 with 3 positions (100 816 bytes), measured before the cap height came from `OS/2`. The size on the grid: the cap height that the font states in `OS/2` (1 500 units) is 10 rows at scale 1, not the top of the outline of `H` (1 520, the angled tops of its stems). Each advance and each pair is rounded to 1/64 of a pixel on its own, to the nearest, a half toward plus infinity; the shaper adds the integers. `validate` checks each new part; host tests damage each one.
499. **The text shaping module** (decision of the owner: "Il nous faudra un module de text shaping/kerning"): `support/text_shaping` and `modules/text_shaping`, exports `shape_text` and `measure_texts` over tables of the caller; the spacing is two tables of the caller; each value read once and kept in its limits; a character outside the cache is placed as `?`.
500. **No program chooses where another program reads its glyph caches** (`review_fonts_hostile.md`, Fault 1, older than the fonts branch). The settings page loses the addresses, the sizes and the hash of the glyph caches: the program `settings` writes that page, and could point each later program at memory that it controls and change it after the validation. The address of each cache is its fixed range; its size is a value of the context that the root service writes (20, 21, 22), bounded by the span of the range (`settings.glyph_cache_place`). Each cache mapping is read-only for every program, and the one handle of the boot image has no right Write: no program writes a cache. A reader of the page gives up after 64 reads of a page left in a change and takes the defaults. The window of `settings` shows the hash of the font from the header of its cache.
501. **A budget of 64 parts for a glyph made of glyphs**, in all its levels (`COMPOSITE_PART_LIMIT`; Weak point 1): a glyph of 21 000 empty parts in four levels ends at once. A face number past a collection is the problem `No_Face`.
502. **A monospace glyph cache keeps each glyph in its cell** (Weak point 2): the header has `FLAG_PROPORTIONAL`; with no such flag, `validate` requires one position, no pair, `left` 0, `width` at most the cell, and the advance of the cell. `validate` also refuses an unknown flag or filter and a font name that is not printable text with an end. The kernel tool validates every cache that it writes.
503. **The text over a gradient or a picture takes the SSE2 form** (Weak point 3): the fallback of the table blend is the form of the target. A host test compares the table blend, the SSE2 form and the scalar form byte for byte with Paper Mono, its SemiBold and B612; a host speed test gives the three over a flat color, a gradient across and noise, for the choice of one path.
504. **The fixed ranges have one place**: the enumeration `Fixed_Range`; each address is its slot. The loader refuses a module that passes `BAND_START`. The places of `address_space` are checked against `OWN_CHOICE_START` at compile time. Paper Mono states the cap height of its outlines (710, x-height 509): its path stays.
505. **The mark of the system is data in `support/mark`** (decision of the owner: "le heron A n°1 est le meilleur, nickel cest simple ! + le logo \"modus\" comme t'as fait super cool"): the 24 rows of the heron, the table of its parts with their colors on the dark and on the light background and their boxes, the letters of the name of the mark, and pure procedures of one pixel. Each part takes one linear gradient from 30 % toward white at the top left of its box to 30 % toward black at its bottom right, taken at the centre of each pixel, in integers rounded to the nearest value: at most one unit from the gradient in floats of the proposal. `kerneltool logo` writes `resources/logo` and `.project/image.png` with the PNG writer of the Odin standard library (compressed, with alpha); `kerneltool check` compares the pixels of each file with the data, not its bytes, because the bytes of the compressed stream depend on the Odin release. `.project` is in the files of the check of the flake. Host tests: the box of each part against the rows, pinned pixels, the cells at cell size 1, two calls with the same pixels, the cells of the name.
506. **A listen grant of UDP** (`docs/NETWORK.md`, "The listen grant of UDP"). The datagram contract had a listen grant for TCP only. A datagram endpoint can now listen on one local UDP port that the root service names, and answer the remotes that came to it, with the table of admitted remotes of TCP unchanged. A new remote is admitted on a datagram of 1200 bytes at least (`LISTEN_FIRST_LEAST`, the least first datagram of a QUIC client), from an address that can answer, when a Receive waits. No kernel change.
507. **A QUIC server of several connections** (`docs/QUIC.md`, section 6; `support/quic/server.odin`). A table of 16 connection records in a mapping of the program. A datagram goes to the record of its Destination Connection ID and its remote; an Initial with a new identifier takes a record only when a copy of it opens, and under the limits: 8 connections and 2 handshakes for one remote address, 8 handshakes at once. A datagram over a limit gets no answer. A long header of another version gets one Version Negotiation packet, 16 in one second at most. The server sends `disable_active_migration`. Not done: Retry, the stateless reset.
508. **`quic_echo`, and the key of a server** (`programs/quic_echo`). An echo server over the listen grant of UDP 4433, with the module `cryptography` through its imports. It makes its Ed25519 key at its start from its own generator (strength "processor" at least), writes its public key on the log and in its window for a client to pin, and keeps the private key in its memory; a new start makes a new key. A weak seed makes no key and no endpoint, and the window says so.
509. **The QUIC client of the peer, and `kerneltool test quic`** (`tools/kerneltool/peer_quic.odin`, `quic_scenario.odin`). The peer links `support/quic` and the module compiled for the host, pins the key that the machine printed (step Quic_Key), and runs cases (step Peer_Quic): 1 MiB each way, with loss, reorder and duplicates, 8 clients, a wrong pinned key, a flood of 1000 forged Initials from 198.18.0.0/15 with a datagram of another version, and a client that vanishes. A stack of another project (OpenSSL 3.6 `s_client -quic -enable_server_rpk`) and `remote` over QUIC are designed, not built (`docs/QUIC.md`, sections 6 and 7).
510. **Retry: a server keeps nothing before a client proved its address** (`review_quic_fourth.md`, F7; `docs/QUIC.md`, section 6; `support/quic/retry.odin`). An Initial with no token gets a Retry, and no record, no key, no open of the Initial. The token is ChaCha20-Poly1305 under a key that the server makes at its start, over the original identifier of the client, the identifier of the Retry and a time, with the address and port of the client as additional data; it lives 10 s. A record is taken only for an Initial whose token opens for its remote and names its identifier. A client takes one Retry, checks its tag, and checks `original_destination_connection_id` and `retry_source_connection_id`. A replay of a token works only from the address and port of its client, for 10 s.
511. **Budgets of the work of new identifiers** (F8). In each second: 2000 Retries (2 exports each), 2000 checks of a token (1 export), 500 checks of an Initial with a valid token (11 exports): about 41 ms of one processor in the worst second. Over a budget, a datagram is dropped before any cryptography. The server copies each datagram once and decides on that copy (W23); `quic_echo` gives the datagrams of known records first in each pass.
512. **The network service holds the limit of 3 times** (W21; `contract/datagram`, `programs/network`). A UDP listen grant sends to a remote at most 3 times the bytes that came from it, until the program marks it with the new command `Validate` (status `Amplification` else); a send no longer keeps a place alive. A program that validates every remote lifts the limit for the addresses that sent it a datagram, for 120 s after their last datagram.
513. **The other points of the fourth read**: a NAT that changes the address or the port of a client ends its connection (W22, written in `docs/QUIC.md`); a record has a generation number for the state of its program (N23); the closing state answers the 1st, 2nd, 4th, 8th... datagram (N25); the tests send the flood of F7 (N28). `kerneltool test quic-real` runs `openssl s_client -quic` of the host against `quic_echo` through QEMU user networking, written and not run.
514. **The UDP relay of the peer for `kerneltool test quic-real`** (`docs/QUIC.md`, section 6; `tools/kerneltool/peer_relay.odin`). QEMU user networking with `restrict=on` drops each UDP datagram of the machine (libslirp, `udp_input`), and `restrict=on` stays. The machine of `test quic-real` has the network device of the peer, and the peer relays UDP between a socket of the loopback of the host and the UDP port 4433 of the machine, from 10.0.2.2 and a port for each client of the host (a table of 16). `peer_run` reads every frame that waits before the next sends of the cases. `quic_echo` logs the error code, the frame type and the reason phrase of each close. The test needs only the loopback of the host: it can join the whole test after a first pass.
515. **One address cannot spend the shared budget of Initial checks** (`review_quic_fifth.md`, F9; `docs/QUIC.md`, section 6; `support/quic/server_budgets.odin`). An Initial with a valid token passes, in this order, the check that its token took no record before, the limits of its address, of the handshakes and of the table, then a budget of its own address (4 Initial checks at once, 4 in each second, in a table of 32 addresses whose token opened), then the shared budget, and only then the open. Each token takes a record once: a table of 64 counters of tokens that took a record, kept for the lifetime of 10 s; full, it forgets the token issued first, which can then take a record again from its own address and port until it expires. A table of used counters, and not a window of bits over the counter: it keeps 1.5 KiB only for tokens that took a record, where a window must cover every Retry of 10 s (25 KiB) and a flood of Retries would push the tokens of real clients out of it.
516. **The shared budgets are token buckets** (W24). A rate and a depth of a tenth of a second: 20 000 Retries a second (2000 at once), 30 000 token checks (3000), 2000 Initial checks (200). By the computed costs (10, 3 and 30 us), every budget spent takes about 35 % of one processor; a flood must bring 24 MB/s with no token, or 36 MB/s with tokens, to spend one. Above that, new clients stop for the duration of the flood, and connected clients go on; `docs/QUIC.md` says so, and designs a budget for each source prefix that a deployment adds. The host test `the_cost_of_the_work_of_new_connections` prints the real costs on the host, for the coordinator to set the rates.
517. **The other points of the fifth read.** W25: `quic_echo` decides the order of a batch once (`quic.server_batch_order`, known records first), so the second datagram of a new client in one batch is given, to the record that the first one took. N32: that order reads the header in place, with no copy, because it is a priority and not a decision of security; each datagram is copied once. W26: a record asks for the validation of its remote at each datagram until a `Validate` for its generation gives 1 (`quic.server_validation_confirmed`; the tag of a `Validate` names the place of the record). N30: the tool requires at most one Retry to each forged address and port of the flood. N29: `docs/QUIC.md`, section 6, no longer says "not run yet". C1: no comment names a review.
518. **The mark in the system** (decision of the owner: "The mark in the system: the heron in the bar of the desktop and at the boot display"). The bar is 32 pixels high, so the heron fits at a cell size of 1 (24 pixels), at (16, 4), the height of the buttons; it takes the place of the word "kernel" and the buttons do not move. The compositor computes its gradients once, into a picture of 24 x 24 pixels that first gets the pixels of the bar from the same records, and shows it with a Picture record of a fixed generation: a frame pays a copy of 24 rows. The boot display draws the heron with cells of 2 pixels and the name of the mark with cells of 3 in its head, in the place of the large word "kernel": no card moves. `support/mark` draws into a canvas (`mark_draw`, `name_draw`, the covered pixels only, cut to the canvas, in the order of the channels of the screen) and refuses a cell size above `CELL_SIZE_LIMIT` (1 << 16), which kept a division from wrapping to 0 at a cell size near 1 << 52 (Weak point 2 of the second read of the fonts).
519. **The compositor tells the windows of a change of the settings only when the values differ** (Weak point 1 of the second read of the fonts). It keeps the values that it told last and compares them with `settings.values_differ`; the count of changes alone no longer sends a record into the ring of each window, which also carries its keys. The first change after the start is always told, as before.
520. **The status line of the terminal names the lines of the text that the view shows** ("lines 709 to 736 of 737", or "none in view"). It named the first row of the view as a line: after `clear()` the terminal forgets its lines and the first row is the edit line, so it said "line 738 of 737". The cause was not the hint line: it showed with or without one. The view counts the rows that show lines of the text (`View.text_rows`), and the status uses that count.
521. **One free row under the edit line at all times**: the hint line is drawn in it, so a hint that comes or goes does not move the text by one row. The cost: the view of a terminal with an edit line shows one line of text less. The list opens under the free row, or above the edit line when the rows below do not hold it, over the text.
522. **A hint line has a long and a short form; the terminal chooses** (`terminal.hint_form_choose`, a pure procedure with a host test): the long form when it fits in the columns, if not the short one, cut to the columns. The terminal knows its columns; the console does not ask for them, and a resize of a terminal needs no new record.
523. **The measure of one key is a command of the console** (`measure_completion()`), in the second thread with its own table of names: the collect, the rank and the rows, the least of 20 batches of 10 keys, for the names of the console and for 512 names of 128 bytes whose matches are far apart.
524. **The names of the programs that run are read once for each quote**: when the caret enters the quote of `connect`, `stop`, `grants` or `revoke`, and kept until it leaves the quote or a line runs. A key in the quote makes no kernel call.
525. **The line history file** (`support/line_editor/history_file.odin`): version 1, the lines oldest first with a byte of length each, a BLAKE3 hash at the end; the newest lines that fit in one block of 4096 bytes. The read checks each field and each byte before it adds a line; a file that fails gives an empty history and the name of the check. Nothing runs on load.
526. **The console does not write the line history file yet** (`docs/CONSOLE.md`, section 6): it holds the grant `Store`, but its store is the store of 4 blocks that shows the limit in the main machine, the storage service starts on demand, and each commit moves the generation that many checks name. Two forms keep the properties (a second store for the history, which is a new grant and a decision of the owner; or a larger limit for the console and the check of the limit moved to another program); each one changes those checks one time.
527. **The console tests the failure of an isolated call first** (review of steps 71 and 73, F1): on a failure it copies back only the table of error details (`isolated.error_details_return`), as `system.call_isolated` does; the rows that a failed export wrote in a Change table stay out of the variables of the console. Shown by the export `scale_values_up_to` of `sample` (it changes the readings in order and refuses the first one that passes a largest value): after its failure in a call host, `sum_values(t)` gives the sum of before (300, not 480).
528. **The order of two names reads at most 256 bytes of them** (`NAME_COMPARE_LIMIT`, W1), and the place of a name in the kept ones is a binary search (7 orders): one key costs at most about 8.9 million steps for 4096 names, whatever their length; before, the order read whole names, up to 64 times for each name. 256 and not 128: a line of the history (200 bytes) compares whole, so two lines that differ at their end stay two lines in the search.
529. **The rows of a completion list go to one List record** (W2): `support/terminal` keeps them (`List_Keeping`, `list_keep_row`, `list_keep_show`, `list_keep_forget`, pure procedures with host tests); the rows given are forgotten after each List and when the program stops, so a List with no rows shows empty rows. A terminal serves one program: the root service starts a new terminal with each start of a program.
530. **A wrong table of error details keeps the error record of the export** (W3): `system.call_isolated` gives the new status `Failed_With_Wrong_Details`, with the record that says why the export failed, and the count of the table of the caller from before the call. Before, the record was replaced by one about the table, and the reason of the failure was lost.
531. **The hint line says when names were not read** (note 4 of the review): more than 512 names gives "and more names than the list reads: type more characters".
532. **Control-R is a character with Control, not a key code**: the input service gives the character of the layout with Control held, so the search follows the letter R in any layout (`docs/CONSOLE.md`, section 1).
533. **The zero of the system is the slashed zero of Paper Mono** (decision of the owner, 2026-11-12: "yes" to "Take the slashed zero"). The feature `zero` of the table `GSUB` of both pinned files is a single substitution of the glyph 294 by the glyph 304 (`zero.zero`, 3 contours and 32 points against 2 and 24 or 26). The tool reads it with a reader of `GSUB` that takes each offset as hostile input, and draws it for '0' in the subsets of Regular and SemiBold; a font with no such feature keeps its zero, and the tool says so. Every picture with a 0 changes.
534. **The text of a proportional font is drawn** (`graphics.text_proportional`, `docs/FONTS.md` section 12.8, step 4): each placed glyph at the nearest third of a pixel, through the blend of the text of cells; the ink of a text stays within its measured box and 2 pixels on each side (200 texts); the three blends give the same bytes. The program `interface_font` shows B612 Regular and Bold, three pairs with and without their adjustment, one word at the three positions, the digits in columns, and the time of one line; it builds its two caches at start. The caches as resources of the boot image wait for a decision: the context has one free value of the system (23), and two sizes need two.
535. **The text of cells refuses a proportional cache** (`graphics.cell_cache_problem`, the problem `Proportional` of `contract/glyph_cache`): `text_cache_use`, `text_bold_cache_use` and `display` refuse it; the text falls back as for any refused cache.
536. **The limit of the rule of the glyph caches is written**: a program trusts its starter, which writes its context and chooses its mappings; under the root service, the starter of each program with a surface, no program writes a cache or points a program at other bytes (`docs/FONTS.md`, section 5).
537. **The loader stops at an invalid header, and its band rule is tested**: `place` returns the error of `module.header` at once; `module_fits_below_band` is the rule (a module and the page after it end at `MODULES_END`, a new name of `contract/fixed_addresses`); a load whose module would end at the band is refused with the stage `Room` before any kernel call. `kerneltool check` refuses a file of `contract`, `support`, `programs` or `modules`, outside `contract/fixed_addresses`, that names `BAND_START` or `SLOT`: an address of the band has no other source than a member of `Fixed_Range`.
538. **No size of a glyph cache goes through the context** (decision of the coordinator, 2026-11-12; the owner can reverse it). The value 20 is a set of bits, one for each member of `Fixed_Range`: the ranges that the starter mapped (`CONTEXT_VALUE_FIXED_RANGES_MAPPED`, `fixed_addresses.ranges_mapped`). A program reads nothing at a range whose bit is clear. A cache states its own size in its header: `glyph_cache.validate_at` bounds it by the span of the range and by the header, then validates the whole. The root service gives a cache only when `validate` accepts the resource, so the header is mapped and states the size of the resource. The values 21, 22 and 23 have no role.
539. **The settings page and the date page are stated by their bits**: the values 13 and 15 held only their fixed addresses, so their bits remove two values and change nothing else. The pointer themes, the storage file and the files of a module keep their values, which hold sizes; the tables of an export are mapped by the receiver at each call.
540. **The two caches of B612 are resources of the boot image** (`interface_glyph_cache`, `interface_glyph_cache_bold`), at the new fixed ranges `Interface_Glyph_Cache` and `Interface_Glyph_Cache_Bold`, mapped read-only for each program with a surface, each terminal and the compositor; `display.interface_font_open` validates them; `interface_font` uses them and builds no cache. The tables of mappings of the root service grow to 12 for a program and the compositor and to 8 for a terminal.
541. **The table of address budgets gives at most the sum of its buckets** (`review_quic_sixth.md`, F10; `docs/QUIC.md`, section 6; `support/quic/server_budgets.odin`). A place of the table moves to a new address only when it is free or its bucket refilled to its depth; when no place can move, the new address gets no Initial check (counter `address_table_full`). The table has 1024 places of 32 bytes (32 KiB), and each address 4 checks at once and 1 in each second: addresses that rotate through it get at most 4096 checks at once and then 1024 in each second, half of the shared budget of 2000. Before, 33 real addresses in turn spent the shared budget. What remains: more than 1024 real addresses that keep asking hold the table. A place frees only when its address asks less often than the rate (1 s after a client that needed one check), and the first new address to ask takes it. A new client then has no bound on its wait while such an attack lasts, and gets a place within 4 s after it stops. The budget for each source prefix (designed) is the next defence.
542. **The smaller points of the sixth read.** `server_batch_order` reads the header of each datagram one time (the known datagrams fill the order from the front, the others from the back). The tag of a `Validate` of `quic_echo` carries the place and the low 27 bits of the generation of the record, so a result for an older record of a place confirms nothing. The cost of the work of new connections is computed; the speed test exists, and its numbers of a quiet host are not yet in `docs/QUIC.md`.
543. **Several streams in a QUIC connection** (`docs/QUIC.md`, section 5; `support/quic/streams.odin`). The caller gives a table of stream records with each connection; their count bounds the streams open at one time. Only the client opens streams, all bidirectional. A server gives the client a limit of streams equal to its records, and one more each time a stream ended on both sides (MAX_STREAMS), so the limit never promises a stream that has no record; a client at the limit sends STREAMS_BLOCKED. A frame that names a stream of the client opens it and each lower one, within the limit. Flow control for each stream (64 KiB) and for the connection (the buffers of every record). RESET_STREAM and STOP_SENDING both ways, with an API for the application (`stream_reset`, `stream_stop_sending`). Each packet carries one STREAM frame, of the streams in turn. `quic_echo` gives each client 4 streams and echoes each one; its budget grows to 4096 pages. The tool has the case "streams": one client, 4 streams of 256 KiB at once.
544. **`remote` over QUIC: one call, one stream** (`docs/QUIC.md`, section 7; designed, not built). Each call is a bidirectional stream of its own: the request header and the call region, then the end; the answer header and the result, then the end. The request number, the answer parts and the limit of one datagram leave the contract. 8 calls at once for one host; `remote` runs one call at a time in the order in which the requests completed. An error of one call is a reset of its stream with a code of a new enumeration `Stream_Error`, never a close of the connection.
545. **A reset states the bytes sent** (`review_quic_seventh.md`, F11; `docs/QUIC.md`, section 5; `support/quic/streams.odin`). The final size of a RESET_STREAM of this side is the largest offset that it sent, not the bytes that the application wrote (RFC 9000, section 4.5): at the reset, the stream forgets the bytes that flow control held back. Before, a stream reset after flow control held bytes back gave a final size over the limit of the peer, and a correct peer (this library too) closed the connection with FLOW_CONTROL_ERROR; the two sides also counted different bytes for the connection. The receive side was right: a final size over the limit of the stream or of the connection is FLOW_CONTROL_ERROR. A stream record writes zeros over the part of its rings that it used when it is freed, and when a new connection takes a record that an ended connection left open (N38). The rule that a record is freed only when the application read the end of the stream is written where `stream_read` is and where `quic_echo` takes its streams (N40). `docs/QUIC.md` says that exactly 1024 real addresses hold the table of address budgets, at about 1.2 MB/s; choice 541 said "more than 1024".
546. **The kernel saves the extended state with `xsave`, and programs can use AVX and AVX-512** (owner, 2026-11-11: "Nah évidemment qu'il faut donc ajouter le support mdrr. On va pas se faire artificiellement limiter par fxsave"; `ARCHITECTURE.md`, "The extended state of the processor"). The kernel sets `CR4.OSFXSR`, `CR4.OSXMMEXCPT`, `CR4.OSXSAVE` and `XCR0` itself on each processor; a bit of `XCR0` is set only for a component that the kernel saves: x87, SSE, AVX, and the three components of AVX-512 when the processor has them; not MPX, PKRU or AMX. The area of each thread is a fixed field of 2688 bytes in the thread table of the kernel (not pages of the budget: the slot of a thread already pays for its frame). `xsaveopt64` at each entry from a program and `xrstor64` at each resume, with all components: a program that never uses AVX pays no write and no read of the ymm high halves. The trap of the modified optimization of `xsaveopt` (an area changed by another writer between the restore and the save of one processor) cannot happen: a processor saves only into the area that it restored last, and the kernel writes an area only before the first resume of its thread. With no `xsave` or no `xsaveopt`, `fxsave64` and `fxrstor64`, chosen once at start and logged: one compare at each entry and resume. A new thread gets the start state: x87 control `0x037F`, `MXCSR` `0x1F80`, `XSTATE_BV` 3, everything else zero; the first processor restores it and saves it again at boot. A program reads its usable instruction sets with `support/instruction_sets` (`cpuid` and `xgetbv` as `asm` templates), with no kernel operation. The processors of the test machines: `Processor_Model` in the tool replaces the flag of the random test.
547. **The corrections of the independent read of the extended state** (`review_xsave.md`). W1: the tool reads whether the host gives the machine AVX-512 (`avx512f`, `avx512bw`, `/dev/kvm`) and waits for the exact line of that machine; the host of the tests has none, so the AVX-512 path is not tested there (a gap). W2: in each pass, one round changes only `MXCSR` and one only the x87 control word, between two entries; with AVX-512 BW the opmasks are moved and checked whole (`kmovq`). W3: the boot proof restores values that are not the init state and reads them back (`fnstcw`, `stmxcsr`, xmm0, and the high half of ymm0 with AVX), so it proves the offsets; the proof of the start state says that it only shows that the start state does not fault. N1: `vzeroupper` after the save at an entry when AVX is enabled, chosen by the form byte (`Xsaveopt_And_Vzeroupper`), with no other read. N3: the form byte is in the processor record at offset 32, in the cache line of the area pointer that the entry reads already, written once by its own processor; the byte in the data of the kernel is gone. N4: the log line says what one save writes at most and the area of 2688 bytes of each thread entry. N5: `thread_create` writes the thread entry in place (a zero fill, then each field), with no temporary of the size of the entry on the kernel stack. N7: the test takes up to 31 threads (the most for a program); a machine with more than 29 processors gets fewer threads than processors plus two, and the log says it.
548. **The kernel saves and restores the extended state only at a switch of threads** (`ARCHITECTURE.md`, "The extended state of the processor", section 3). It replaces, in 505, "`xsaveopt64` at each entry from a program and `xrstor64` at each resume", and in 506 the parts N1 (`vzeroupper` after the save at an entry, the form `Xsaveopt_And_Vzeroupper`) and N3 (the form byte in the processor record). The reason is measured: one kernel entry of `locks` took 177 ns on `master` (`fxsave64` and `fxrstor64` at each entry and resume) and 229 and 249 ns with the save at each entry by `xsaveopt64` and `xrstor64`; on the host the pair is 227 cycles with `fxsave64` and 430 with `xsaveopt64` (`xrstor64` alone 327). Now `schedule` saves the thread that a processor leaves, before the thread can reach a run queue, and restores the thread that it takes, just before `run`; an entry, an interrupt and the return to the same thread touch nothing. The entry and the resume of `kernel/processor.asm` lose the save, the restore and the read of the form; the processor record loses `extended_state_area` and `extended_state_form` (`self` is now at offset 16 of `gs`); the scheduler passes the form, `Fxsave` or `Xsaveopt`, to the two procedures. A thread taken again just after it left is restored again: an owner of the registers is a later step. The program `extended_state` measures one hand-over between two threads with x87 and SSE in use and with the ymm high halves in use.
549. **The kernel has no x87, MMX, SSE or AVX instruction, and the build proves it** (`ARCHITECTURE.md`, "The extended state of the processor", section 3a). A rule of the kernel: no code that runs in the kernel, an interrupt handler included, changes a register of the extended state, except the save and the restore. The kernel object is made by `llc` with `-mattr=-x87,-mmx,-sse,-sse2,-sse3,-ssse3,-sse4.1,-sse4.2,-avx,-avx2,-avx512f,+soft-float` (the IR of Odin has no target features on its functions, so these hold for all of it; the kernel has no floating-point value, and the four `f16` conversions of the Odin runtime that the IR holds are not called and are dropped by the link). `runtime/memory.asm` has a kernel form with general registers and `rep movsb`/`rep stosb` (`WITHOUT_VECTOR_REGISTERS`), with its host tests in `runtime/without_vector_registers`. After the link, the tool disassembles the kernel with `llvm-objdump` and refuses any instruction that names a vector, x87, MMX or opmask register or uses that state, outside `extended_state_save`, `extended_state_restore` and `extended_state_restore_and_read` (`tools/kerneltool/vector_check.odin`, host tests in `vector_check_test.odin`). Built by the coordinator with the first form of the features: the linked kernel has no instruction that names a vector register outside the three procedures (635 on `master`). After the third independent read, the check cannot pass on a listing that it does not read: it fails with fewer than 10 000 instructions read (the kernel has 31 145), when one of the three procedures is missing, when it sees no vector instruction in one of them, or when a procedure is not followed by its end label (`<name>_end`, in `kernel/processor.asm`); it refuses `wait` (the name of `fwait` in the listing) and the instructions of AMX; and the build refuses kernel IR whose functions carry target features of their own.
550. **The elements of the interface draw in two fonts** (`docs/FONTS.md` section 12.8, step 6; `docs/INTERFACE.md` section 5): names, titles, labels, buttons, lists and column titles in the interface font (B612); values (numbers, typed text, the count of a card, the cells of a table, the text grid) in the font of the cells (Paper Mono). The draw record is version 7: the kind `Interface_Text`, its rectangle grown by its ink margin, its origin at a third of a pixel (`text_origin_thirds`, which was `text_subpixel_x`). Each element measures a text in the font that draws it and cuts it to its box. A program takes it with no change of its code; `interface_elements` and `interface_through_module` needed one line each.
551. **The rule of the two fonts in `DESIGN.md`, section 5** replaces "one font at a time, on the grid of cells", as a proposal that the owner accepted in substance on 2026-11-11 ("Go b612", "Paper mono très bien pour monospace").
552. **The module `interface_module` takes the interface font of the program that runs it**: in a direct call, the caches at the fixed ranges of the caller; in an isolated call none, so the call host draws in the font of the cells. Hostile `Interface_Text` records draw nothing.
556. **The line history has a store of its own: a decision of the coordinator** (2026-11-12; the owner put the item in his list and did not choose; he can reverse it). The history is the state of the console, not a file of the person; the store of the person and its checks stay as they are. The root service gives a second connection to the storage service (`Root_Command.Connect_History`, the role `History_Store`, the grant `history`): the store `console.history`, no grant to act for the principal, so that store and no other one.
557. **The history store has 4 blocks, not 2**: one object of one block takes 3 blocks of a store, and a change needs one more in the commit that thins the version before (the store of the person shows both). A hostile console fills 4 blocks at most.
558. **The connection comes through the root ring at the start of the console**, not in its context: the places of the store connection in a context are those of the terminal, and the storage service starts at boot after the console (for the boot pass of `settings`); the root service answers the ring after that start, so the order of the boot does not change. The console waits 30 s at most for it.
559. **The root service keeps the history connection apart**: `Disconnect` by the name of the service does not end it; a revocation of `store` does not touch it; a revocation of `person` gives it its limit only; `revoke("console", "history")` ends it. One connection of each kind for one program and one service.
560. **One commit after each line that the history added**, in the second thread while the line still runs; `shutdown()` and `stop("console")` save before they act. A held Enter adds one line at most. A rule of time (one write in a second) makes the count of commits a fact of the host, and the generations count the commits of the whole disk; a rule of lines keeps it a fact of the scenario.
561. **The load checks the file as data**: absent, damaged, unreadable, larger than 4096 bytes, or refused by `history_file_read` gives an empty history and one log line; a line keeps the bounds of a typed line; nothing runs.
562. **The machine tests count generations from a base** (the step `Generation` and `{generation+N}` in the text and the expected text of a step, `generation_expand` with a host test): the main machine reads the generation of the first write of the person, and names the 9 generations after it from that base; the persistence machine names 1027 in place of 1023 (4 commits of the history) and 3 stores, 4 objects in place of 2 and 3.
563. **`history_clear()`** empties the history in memory and removes its object and its versions from the store; the line of the command goes too. `support/storage` has `remove`.
564. **The console prints a result whole, at any length.** `print` formatted all its parts in a buffer of 256 bytes, and kept 192 bytes of a line for the log; the writer and `copy` dropped the rest with no sign. So a Text result longer than 256 bytes was cut in the terminal and in the log, and a line longer than 192 bytes in the log. The answer of `storage.stores()` passed 256 bytes with the store of the line history, and the machine test saw it cut in the middle of a store. Now a Text part goes to the terminal as it is (the terminal makes as many records as it needs), a number is formatted on its own, and the log line holds `system.LOG_CALL_LIMIT` (4096) bytes, the text of one Log call of the kernel, which writes it in as many log records as it needs; a longer line goes to the log in parts of 4096 bytes. `system.log_text` writes a text with no format and no buffer. The machine test prints a result of 324 bytes on one line.
565. **A text is never cut in silence** (`docs/ARCHITECTURE.md`, "A text is never cut in silence"): a text that does not fit is written whole in parts, cut with the cut mark, or refused with an error. The writer of `support/sentence` counts the bytes that it drops, and `written` ends its text with the mark; `log_in` writes a text whole, in lines of 512 bytes; the kernel ends a record of its own that passes 352 bytes with the mark; the Text answers of the storage service and of `settings.get` give the whole count; the console refuses a root ring name of more than 31 bytes.
566. **One mark for the whole system: "... and N bytes more"**, after a whole UTF-8 character, in `contract:cut_mark`, which the kernel and the programs share. N counts each byte that the place does not hold, those that the mark takes back too. `information.text_put` used "..." with no count: it now uses the mark. A buffer smaller than the mark holds as much of it as fits.
567. **A byte written after the mark is a missing byte**: the writer writes the mark again with the new count at the next `written`; `writer_reset` starts a new text in the same buffer.
568. **A partial check of the sources** (`text_cut_check`): outside `support/sentence` and the tests, no source sets `writer.count` or reads `writer.buffer[`. It reads text, not types: a writer of another name escapes it.
569. **The scenario**: after the check of the log of the main machine, `probe.log_long(1500)` logs 1500 bytes in three lines of the log (9 records), and the tool waits for the end of the text; `start` with a name of 39 bytes is refused by the console with the count.
570. **The idle timeout bounds a connection whose peer went silent** (`docs/QUIC.md`, section 5; `support/quic/connection.odin`, `recovery.odin`). The three probe timeouts of the idle bound, and of the closing and draining states, are computed with no backoff (`probe_timeout_base`): a connection that receives nothing ends at most `max(idle timeout, 3 × base probe timeout)` after its last activity, whatever its probes. With the backoff, each unanswered probe doubled the bound and the bound moved past each next probe: a server kept the record of a vanished client for about 2 hours. Found by a machine step of the case "loss" that failed once under load, after a lost close. The peer of the tool sends the close of a client at the end of a case 3 times, outside the loss of the case; a new machine case "silent" checks the end by the idle timeout.
571. **What moves the idle bound, a server always has one, and the machine proves it in its own time** (`review_quic_eighth.md`, N43, W27, W28; `docs/QUIC.md`, sections 5 and 6). The last activity moves only for a packet that is recorded: a packet whose data had no place is processed but not recorded, and a replay of it no longer keeps a silent connection alive. `server_init` refuses an idle timeout of 0 (`Empty`, milliseconds, argument 5); a client may give 0, and its connection then has the idle timeout of the server. At an end by the idle timeout, `quic_echo` logs the silence in time of the machine, its bound, and a verdict ("held its bound": at least the idle timeout, at most the bound and a late wake of 5 s); the step "silent" reads the verdict, runs under KVM and under emulation alike, and is never skipped (the test of `/dev/kvm` is gone).
572. **The compositor draws its names in B612** (`docs/FONTS.md` section 12.8, step 7): the buttons of the bar, the lines of the menus, the titles of the windows, the launcher; its values (clock, date, typed text, counts) stay in Paper Mono. The places of the bar come from the bar layout, measured in the font that draws it; `bar.button_at`, `bar.line_at` and `bar.windows_line_at` map a press to the entry that the eye sees. The compositor writes the layout in its log; the kernel tool measures the same layout and checks it. Two clicks of the scenario that used typed places of the buttons of the bar ask the layout now.
573. **The boot display in two steps, as a rule** (`docs/DESIGN.md`, section 5): the boot program draws its first screen in Spleen 8x16 (it holds no glyph cache); the root service draws it again with B612 for the titles and the names of the facts, and Paper Mono for the values, at no added byte.
574. **A measure of the bar**: one whole frame of the bar with the menu of the programs open, built and drawn, in each font, the least of 20, once in the log.
575. **The PNG files of the tool are compressed, with one encoder** (decision of the owner, 2026-11-12: "oui compresse les images parce que ça fait beaucoup"). Before, `png_encode` wrote stored blocks of deflate: each picture of the screen was 2.36 MB, and `docs/screenshots` held 85 files and 192 MB. Now `png_encode` (`tools/kerneltool/picture_files.odin`) calls the encoder of the Odin standard library (`core:image/png`, `save_to_buffer`, its default level 5 and its adaptive filter of each row), which `kerneltool logo` used already: the pictures of the screen and the picture of the font candidates are RGB with 8 bits for each channel and no alpha, the files of the mark keep their alpha. The tool has one PNG encoder: the writer of stored blocks and its chunk and checksum code are gone. The same pixels give the same bytes: the encoder and `core:compress/deflate` read no clock, no random number and no map (read in the source of `dev-2026-11a`), so a picture with no change of pixels is no change of the repository; the pictures are not outputs of the check of the same bytes after a second build. A host test encodes one picture twice and compares the bytes, and decodes it and compares each pixel. `kerneltool screenshots compress` converts the files that exist, with no machine: each PNG file of `docs/screenshots` and `resources` whose first block of deflate is stored is decoded, encoded, decoded again and compared pixel by pixel, then written; a file that is compressed already keeps its bytes; the first file that fails stops the command with its name. Not measured yet: the size of the 85 pictures after the conversion, and the time to encode one picture of the screen in the test.
576. **The site is the documentation of the system, generated by `kerneltool site`** (`docs/SITE.md`; request of the owner, `DECISIONS.md`, D-163; started on 2026-11-12, then made "doc like" by the owner the same day). `build/site/` holds several pages: the home, which is the one page that shows the system; the guides, one for each document of `docs/` (not `HANDOFF.md` and `REVIEWS.md`, working records); the reference tables (the glossary, the commands of the tool, the measured numbers, the licenses); the releases. No page holds a text that a person must remember to update: the text comes from `docs/`, `README.md`, `CHANGELOG.md` and the other files that each page names in its foot, with the commit, the release and the time of the build. One style sheet, no script, no request to another server; each link inside the site is relative, so the site works from a file, a folder of a host or a domain. A Markdown renderer in the tool knows the Markdown of `docs/` and lists what it does not understand. Each number is a line of a log of the last machine tests, quoted whole. The command checks every page before it writes one: one `h1`, a title and a description that no other page has, an alt text, a width and a height for each picture, and each link and anchor inside the site. `kerneltool check` writes `build/reproducible_build.txt` for the site, and checks that each picture that `README.md` names exists. The address of the site is one setting, empty: the owner has not chosen a host or a domain. The site reads `gui_toolkit/src/site` for ideas only and uses none of its code. The status page, the reference tables of the code and the module pages: a choice of `docs/FIRST_MILESTONE.md`. Not done: the news, the search, the metadata for other sites, the feeds (`docs/SITE.md`, "Not done yet").
577. **The licenses are tables, each cell from a file** (owner, 2026-11-12: "be more table oriented ;)"). One row for each thing that has a license, and a second table for what builds and tests the system; each cell comes from `LICENSE`, the `.license` files of `resources/fonts`, `flake.nix` and `flake.lock` (small readers of the exact forms of those files, which fail with the line when the form changes), the pointer manifests, the license files of `support/cryptography` and of the portrait. A cell that no file records says "not recorded", and the command lists it: the license of Spleen and of each tool of the build. `support/cryptography/LICENSE.odin.txt` says the code was taken at the tag `dev-2026-05`, and the origin check compares with `dev-2026-11a`: the table shows both.
578. **The mark on a transparent background** (owner, 2026-11-12). `kerneltool logo` also writes `resources/logo/modus_transparent.png` and `modus_light_transparent.png`, in its check, and the README shows them in a `<picture>` for the two color schemes. The site draws its own pictures of the mark from `support/mark`, transparent, at whole cell sizes (8 for the head of the home, 1 for the bar), and shows each at its own size; its picture for other sites keeps the dark background.
579. **The reference tables are read from the files that define each thing** (owner, 2026-11-12: "une doc riche et auto générée"). The kernel operations and the status of a completion (`contract/operation`), the roles of the context (`contract/operation`, `support/system/grants.odin`), the error kinds and units (`contract/errors`), the grants (`programs/root/main.odin`, `support/system/grants.odin`), the fixed ranges (`contract/fixed_addresses`), the built-in commands of the console (`programs/console`). Small readers of the text of each file (`tools/kerneltool/site_source_readers.odin`) take the names, the numbers and the comments, because the comments of a contract are its documentation and the compiler drops them; each reader knows one form and stops the command with the file and the line when the form changes. The host tests compare each enumeration that a reader finds with the enumeration that the compiler knows: the same names, values and order. The parameters and the result of a kernel operation exist only as the words of its comment, "(parameters) -> result": the table shows those words. A comment line inside an enumeration continues the comment of the member above it: a heading comment before a member (as "The word wait" before `Word_Wait`) shows under the member before it. The roles 13 and 15 of the context have no constant in `contract/operation`, only a comment: the table does not show them. The address of a fixed range comes from `fixed_addresses.range_of`, not from a copy of the constants.
580. **A module page for each module of `module_builds`** (`tools/kerneltool/site_module_pages.odin`). The page gives the documentation of the module (the comment before `package`, the text that the build writes into the module), its exports with their signatures as the console prints them (`write(name: Text, data: Text) -> Integer`), its imports and the modules that import it, its size in the boot image of the last build, and for a program (a source in `programs/`) its grants and its budget from `grants_of` and `things_of` of the root service. The signatures come from `describe_package`, the reader that the build uses: no module needs to be built for its page.
581. **The status page** (`tools/kerneltool/site_status.odin`; owner, 2026-11-12, of a part "What runs today": "watch out cause it'll be a nightmare to udate the description"). Each property with its status, the column of the summary of `PROPERTIES.md`; the evidence items and the gaps of this file that name it ("property <n>" or "P<n>", one whole word); then each gap with its group, and the next steps. The page has no sentence of its own about the state: each cell is a part of the two documents. The ids of the evidence items are those of the guide, because the page renders this file again with the same renderer.
582. **The tiles of the boot image on the home, and links from the guides to the reference**. The home shows one tile for each module of the boot image of the last build, as the boot display draws it: its width from its size, its color from the first byte of its hash. The rule of the color, `hue_color`, moves from `support/bootscreen` to `support/graphics`, so that the boot display and the site use one rule. A code span of a guide that is the whole name of a kernel operation, an error kind or a module, or a call of a built-in command of the console (`load("math")`), links to its row; a name that two tables have (`Bad_Address` is a status and an error kind) links nowhere.
583. **The news: a second record beside the changelog** (`tools/kerneltool/news.odin`, `site_news.odin`, `news/README.md`; owner, 2026-11-12: "we could have a \"parallel changelog\" (changelog is very technical/list-like) which would be like a \"status report\" or like \"News\""). A news entry is one file of `news/`, named by its date and its subject, never moved or merged, as a fragment of the changelog is one file: a head of six keys (title, date, release, picture, kind, guide) and a text of at most 1200 bytes. Four kinds, each the colored mark of a card: Shown, Measured, Decided, Fixed. `kerneltool news new` writes an entry with no title and no text, so that the check fails until a person writes them; `kerneltool news check` is part of `kerneltool check`. The site shows the entries on `news.html`, the three newest on the home, a link from each release to its entries, and the feed `news.xml` in Atom, whose ids are the addresses of the files in the repository, so that they do not change when the site gets an address. The home has no prose of its own about what runs: the newest news, the tiles and the numbers. The first eleven entries are written from the dates of `CHANGELOG.md`, the evidence items of this file and the steps of `REVIEWS.md`. A step adds an entry only when a person can see or use something new.
584. **The search of the site: one index, one small script, the match of the console** (`tools/kerneltool/site_search.odin`; owner, 2026-11-12: "une doc riche et auto générée, avec recherche, modules, etc."). `kerneltool site` writes `search.json`, an entry for each page and for each row or section that has an id (modules, exports, operations, error kinds, commands of the console, terms, sections of the guides, properties, news, releases, gaps, rows of the licenses), checked against the written pages: each page has an entry, each entry names an id of its page. Bounds: 4096 entries (the names that one ranking of the console reads), 1 MiB, a title of 128 bytes, a text of 120 bytes. `search.js` is plain JavaScript written by the tool, loaded with `defer`; it fetches the index on the first key and ranks it with the fuzzy match and the order of `support/line_editor/fuzzy_match.odin`, ported rule for rule; the tie order is the kind. The tool writes the constants of the match into the script, and one table of queries, names, scores and orders is run by a host test over the matcher of Odin and by the script with `?selftest=1`: a change of the rule fails the host test, then the self test of the script. The look is the completion list of the console: a box at the right of the bar, at most 8 rows, the matched characters in the accent, the kind at the right, Up, Down, Enter, Escape, `/` to focus, the query in the address as `?q=`; the roles of a combobox and a listbox. With no script, the box stays hidden and every page is reachable by its links. The check of the pages now refuses inline code and inline style. Refused: a library of search, a build step of JavaScript, an index made in the browser from the pages. Not done: the self test of the script runs in a browser only, since no program of the repository runs JavaScript; a page opened from a file cannot fetch the index.
585. **What other sites read: written and checked only with a site address** (`tools/kerneltool/site_metadata.odin`; owner, 2026-11-12: "we'll want a sitemap, json-ld, good seo (graph) etc"). A canonical address, Open Graph and the `@id`s of JSON-LD are absolute, and a relative one is wrong for the program that receives it: so `kerneltool site` writes them, with `sitemap.xml` and `robots.txt`, only with a site address (`--address https://<host>/<path>`, a path works), and says which parts it did not write with none. Each page has one page graph, built from the data that the page shows: the site with its search, the system as a `SoftwareApplication` (an operating system on x86-64, tested under QEMU), its source code, the picture for other sites, the page, its breadcrumbs, and a `TechArticle`, a `DefinedTermSet`, an `ItemList` or a `Blog` with a `BlogPosting` for each news entry. The JSON is escaped for a script element, and the check of inline code allows this one script of data. A date is written only when it is true: the date of a news entry, of a release, and the `lastmod` of the news and of the releases in the sitemap; a guide has no date. The checks: one canonical address for each page, its own and unique; the Open Graph picture exists with its stated size; each graph parses and each id that it names is a node or a page; the sitemap lists exactly the written pages. Also written in both cases: `releases.xml`, the feed of the releases with stable ids; `site.webmanifest`; `llms.txt`; `rel=prev` and `rel=next` between guides; each picture of the repository written again compressed, with the same pixels (`site_png_compress`). Not done: `author` and `publisher` (the owner decides whether the graph names a person); a validation against schema.org or a search engine, since the work runs with no network.
586. **The site menu, the author, and the publication by a workflow** (owner, 2026-11-12: "il manque un menu burger sur la version mobile du site", "pour l'auteur du site évidemment tu peux mettre", "pour la publication du site je préfère un workflow GitHub évidemment", and the news cards of the home that do not align: "ça fait dommage"). (1) The sections and the search of the bar are in one `details` element: one list of links at every width, so a screen reader reads each section once. Under 720 pixels its `summary` is a square button with three lines drawn by the style sheet and the hidden word "Menu"; on a wide screen the menu is open and the button hidden. A closed `details` cannot be opened by a style sheet in every browser: a browser with `::details-content` shows its content with no script, and `search.js`, already loaded on each page, opens it on a wide screen; with no script and no `::details-content`, the button shows and works. The menu is closed in the file, so that a narrow page does not move after its first paint. Refused: two copies of the links, a checkbox as a button, an inline script. (2) The page graph names a `Person` from the line `Copyright` of `LICENSE` (its holder, nothing else) with the address of the account that owns the repository: the `author` of the program, of its source code, of each guide and each news entry, and the `publisher` of the site; `<meta name="author">` on each page and `<author>` in the two feeds. (3) `.github/workflows/site.yml` builds the boot image and the site with the tool, and deploys `build/site` as an artifact of GitHub Pages; the domain is a setting of the repository, with no `CNAME` file; the headers come from the proxy of Cloudflare, since GitHub Pages sends none of its own. A clean checkout has no log of a machine test: the numbers say "Not measured in this build.", and the owner decides whether the workflow runs the tests. (4) The news cards of the home share the rows of their grid (`subgrid`): the title, the line, the picture and the text of each card of a row start at one height; with no subgrid, a title keeps the room of two lines.
587. **The tagline of the home, and the icons drawn from the table of the heron** (owner, 2026-11-12: "la tagline est trop longue", "la favicon faut qu'elle soit bien configurée"). (1) The head of the home holds the mark, the name, one line (the tagline: the first sentence of the first paragraph of `README.md`) and the links; the rest of the paragraph is a section "What it is" after the news, so that the first screen of a phone holds no explanation. The description of the home stays the whole paragraph: the tagline alone is too short to describe the system. (2) Each raster icon is the heron at a whole cell size with an even margin: `favicon.ico` at the root with 32 and 48, written by the tool as an ICO container of PNG files; 180 for a phone and a maskable 512, opaque on the dark background; 192 and 512 transparent in the light form, whose neck reads on a light and a dark tab. An SVG icon draws the cells as rectangles with the gradients of `support/mark` and a rule for the dark scheme. No icon of 16: 16 pixels cannot hold 24 cells, and a browser scales the 32. The links are relative, in the order ICO, SVG, phone. The command checks the ICO, the sizes of each named icon and the opacity of the icons of a phone.
588. **The columns of a table have kinds, and a wide table scrolls in its own box** (owner, 2026-11-12: "tableau pas ouf, si possible de faire un tableau scrollable ? pck la colonne compressée"). The writer of each generated table states the kind of each column (name, short, prose, line, date, number); the tool writes the class of the kind on each cell, and the style sheet gives each kind a least width (a width on the header cell, which the automatic layout of a table reads as a least width). A date is a `time` element and never breaks; a number never breaks and is aligned right. A table of a document has the general kind, with a cell of only a number or a date of that kind. The box of each table is a region with a name and `tabindex="0"`, scrolls sideways at every width when the table is wider, keeps the first column in view, and shows a line at an edge where more is, tied to the scroll in a browser that has scroll timelines. Refused: a head row that stays at the top, because inside a box that scrolls sideways it sticks to the box and does nothing.
589. **The animations of the mark** (owner, 2026-11-12: "on pourrait même en faire une version animée, en mode idle quoi [...] plusieurs animations parmi lesquelles on cyclerait [...] le favicone soit animé [...] respecte les choix de l'utilisateur au niveau des animations [...] seulement le gros logo sur la page d'accueil"). Five animations as data in `support/mark/animation.odin`: blink, look, leg, ripple, dip. Each frame is a list of changes of cells of the still heron, or of an earlier frame of the same animation, with a duration; the frame 0 is the still heron, and after the last frame the heron is still again. A fixed order of play of 15 steps gives the still time and the next animation: no random number. The site writes one sprite sheet for each color scheme of the head of the home, one sheet of the icon at 32 pixels (one request, cut by the script on a canvas), and `mark_animation.js`, with the table written into it, loaded with `defer` on the home only. The script does nothing for a person who asks for reduced motion and stops in a hidden tab. It puts a canvas of the same size in the place of the still picture only while it plays, and sets the icon of the tab as a `data:` address during an animation only. The Content-Security-Policy does not change: `script-src 'self'` and `img-src 'self' data:` allow it. Refused: an animation in CSS alone, because a cycle of five animations with still times of varied length needs one long list of key frames, and the icon of the tab needs a script anyway. Not done: the desktop of the system does not play them; Safari does not show a changed icon of the tab.
590. **A refused return is a fault of the thread's program** (`ARCHITECTURE.md`, "The return to a program"; the owner's list of next work, 2026-11-12). Two causes let a program stop the machine: an instruction that ends at the last byte below `USER_END` gives the return address 2^47, which `iretq` refuses in the kernel (a `syscall` there every time, or an interrupt after any instruction there), and the flag nested task, which a program sets with `popfq` and which `syscall` kept in the flags of the kernel, so that its `iretq` faulted. Two layers. First, `run` calls `return_frame_prepare` before each return: the selectors are forced to those of programs, the flags to the bits that a program may keep with interrupts on, and an instruction address at or above `USER_END` or a stack address that is not canonical stops the program (`STOP_CODE_RETURN`, the field and its value in the stop report and the log); `MSR_SFMASK` also clears nested task. Second, `resume` returns from a copy of the frame at the top of the kernel stack, so a fault of its `iretq` pushes its frame on the kernel stack and not over the thread table; `interrupt_common` gives the kernel its gs back when the saved `rip` is the `iretq` of `resume` (`resume_return`) and the vector is 11, 12 or 13, and `kernel_interrupt` stops the program of the current thread for it. A program that keeps an address that is not canonical in `rsp` is stopped at its next return: the kernel does not depend on whether a processor refuses it.
591. **The program `return_fault`** (`programs/return_fault`): one child for each cause that a program can make, started from the boot image with a role in its context, and its stop code and stop report read by the parent; the machine runs `extended_state` at the same time, so the stop of a child that holds ymm values is also a test of the save at a switch of threads. A NASM file of the program holds the three hostile sequences: they need `rsp`, the flags and a jump that compiled code cannot give.
592. **A kernel of the test for one machine, by a setting of one module** (`ARCHITECTURE.md`, "The return to a program", section 3). The module table gives a module its own settings of the compiler (`defines`); `kernel_without_return_check` is the kernel built with `TEST_BUILD_SKIPS_THE_INSTRUCTION_ADDRESS_CHECK`, the boot program takes its kernel module by a setting (`KERNEL_MODULE`), and one boot volume holds it, which only the second machine of `kerneltool test return_fault` boots. Why: the second layer of the return is code on a path of exceptions that no program can reach on the kernel of the system; a mistake in it would show only on the day that it matters. Why not a setting of the whole build: every other machine would run a kernel with a check removed. The guard: the kernel of the test writes its own line at boot, and `machine_test` fails each other machine whose log has it. Also: after a refused return, the processor continues in `schedule` at the top of its kernel stack, so a chain of refusals uses a stack of a fixed size.
593. **The small instructions of the kernel are instruction templates** (`ARCHITECTURE.md`, "The instructions of the kernel"; `DECISIONS.md`, D-161). The ports (`in`, `out`), `rdmsr`, `wrmsr`, `xsetbv`, the reads and writes of `cr0`, `cr2`, `cr3` and `cr4`, `invlpg`, `lidt`, `ltr`, the read of the processor record through gs (`processor_self`) and the copy of `user_copy` (`stac`, `rep movsb`, `clac`) are `asm` templates in `kernel/instructions.odin`; their procedures and `foreign` declarations in `kernel/processor.asm` are gone. Each is `#volatile` with `#clobber memory`, the meaning of the call that it replaces. The abbreviated names became complete words (`model_specific_register_write`, `control_register_3_write`, `interrupt_table_load`, `task_register_load`). What stays in assembler, and why: the entries, `resume`, the stubs, `stack_switch` and `gdt_load` own the stack or the frame; the save and the restore of the extended state are the only code allowed to name vector registers, between their start and their end label; `idle_wait` and `processor_stop` have `hlt`, which the compiler takes as an end of the flow. `runtime/memory.asm` stays: LLVM calls `memcpy`, `memmove` and `memset` by name, and a template cannot be the target of a call. The cycle counter, `cpuid` and `pause` were already intrinsics of the compiler, in the code of their callers.
594. **The fault ring is in the thread record of the handler** (`ARCHITECTURE.md`, "The fault ring"; it replaces the ring of its own of choice 437). `op.Thread_Record` gets `fault_written`, `fault_read`, `fault_full` and 16 records of 64 bytes (`op.Fault_Record`): the kernel writes that page already (the event word), the budget paid for it with the thread, and the ring lives as long as the handler, so it needs no memory object, no mapping and no count of references. The kernel indexes with its own count; `fault_read`, which the program writes, can only make the ring full.
595. **Two operations: `Fault_Handler_Set` (30) and `Fault_Answer` (31)**: the kernel has 31 operations; property 1 names a first target of 32 or fewer. The program names one of its own threads (a pager for another program is a later step); any thread of the program answers, by the number and the serial of the record: `Resume` runs the instruction again through `run` and its check of the frame, `Stop` writes the report of today (`program_fault_report`, now shared with the stop of a fault with no handler).
596. **Which faults go to the ring**: every page fault that `page_give` does not serve, at an address below `THREAD_AREA_BASE`, of a thread that is not the handler. No flag of a mapping (choice 437 named `Faults_To_Ring`): a program answers `Stop` for a range that it does not serve. Never delivered: the handler's own fault (no fault of a fault), the thread areas, the time page and the kernel half, a fault of the kernel on behalf of a program (`user_copy` checks the range first: the operation fails as before), and the faults of other kinds.
