597. **No wait for ever on a place, and no resource held by a waiter**: a full ring stops the program as with no handler, and the kernel counts it (`fault_full`, a line of the log). A thread that waits holds only its thread; it ends with its program. When the handler ends while a thread waits, no thread can read its record: the program stops with the new stop code `STOP_CODE_FAULT_UNANSWERED` (0x500). No time bound on the wait: the handler of the program decides, and the program or the person stops it.
598. **The states of a waiting thread are the states of a word wait**: `Waiting` with the deadline `WAIT_FOREVER` and the new field `fault_waits`, which only `Fault_Answer` clears; it starts under the program lock and the run lock, before the record is published, and it ends through `thread_wake`, as a word wait does. `schedule` saves the extended state of the thread when it leaves its processor, as for any thread. The program `fault_ring` (thirteen cases in children, as `return_fault`). The thread also leaves the table of words when it starts to wait: the entries of its last word wait stay there until its next kernel entry, and a wake through them (`Word_Wake`, a decommit, a revocation) would end the wait with no answer; `thread_words_wake` skips a thread that waits for an answer.
599. **A ring that ends while a thread waits stops the program, whatever ends it** (`ARCHITECTURE.md`, "The fault ring"): the end of the handler thread, `Fault_Handler_Set` with another handler or the same one, and `Fault_Handler_Set` with `end` 1 all stop the program with `STOP_CODE_FAULT_UNANSWERED` when a thread waits. One rule for the three: the record of the waiting thread is in a ring that no thread will read. To refuse the operation with `Bad_State` would be a second rule for the same loss, and the end of a thread cannot be refused. A program answers its waiting threads before it changes its handler.
600. **The serial of a fault record is a count of the program** (`Program.fault_serial`, 1 for the first fault that goes to a ring), not the serial of the kernel's slot of the thread: all programs share the slots, and no operation shows the serial of a slot to a program, so a record that held it would tell a program how many threads others made. The serial names the fault, not only the thread: an answer for an earlier fault of a thread that waits again is refused too.
601. **A page fault with the reserved bit of the error code stops the machine**: only the kernel writes the entries of page tables, so the fault is a mistake of the kernel, as an exception in the kernel is; it is never delivered to a fault handler, which could repair the page and loop on the same fault. The address of a page fault is read from `cr2` once, at the start of `kernel_interrupt`, and each path takes that value.
602. **The instruction templates that change a flag declare it** (`kernel/instructions.odin`): the moves to and from the control registers (the arithmetic flags become undefined), the copy with `cld` (the direction flag), and the copy with `stac` and `clac` (AC) have `#clobber %flags`. The compiler adds that clobber by itself only for an instruction that its table lists as writing an arithmetic flag, and its table lists none of these.
603. **Kernel events: four fixed event points, a table of rules, a ring for each processor** (`ARCHITECTURE.md`, "Kernel events"; `DECISIONS.md`, D-079). The event points: each command of a kernel entry (its operation and its cycles), a processor that takes a thread from a run queue, an exception of a program after the kernel acted on it (first use, fault ring, or stop), a lock of the kernel taken after a wait (its kind and its cycles). One operation, `Events_Set` (32): the kernel has 32 operations, the first target of property 1. The event of a kernel entry is one command, not one entry: an entry runs up to 32 commands, and the decision asks for the operation number.
604. **The rule is a record that the kernel compares, with a bound** (`op.Event_Rule`, 64 bytes): a kind, a program (a handle with Read, or 0 for all), a detail with `Match_Detail`, a least duration (at most 1 s, in cycles once at the give), "one in N". At most 16 rules: a seventeenth is `No_Slot`. An event that one rule or more accept is one record. "One in N" is a count for each rule with an atomic addition, so it is exact across processors; it is the one line that two processors can write at an event point, only with such a rule.
605. **The right `Events` on the machine**, not `Inspect`: the records give the order and the duration of the work of each program to the cycle, a precise clock over other programs (property 20, line 17). The root service gives it by name; only `kernel_events` has it, with no `Give`: no program of the system passes `Events` on.
606. **The rings are in a contiguous memory object of the caller, written through the direct map** (property 14). The kernel holds one reference to the object while the table exists, so a close, a revocation or a decommit of the program does not free the frames under an event point; the kernel never uses an address of a program at an event point. The kernel reads one word of a ring (`read`) and trusts it for nothing: it indexes with its own counts. The form that the coordinator named first (pages that the kernel owns, mapped read-only) still needs a word that the program writes, and adds a mapping and a lifetime rule of its own: not taken. Device memory is refused.
607. **A full ring drops the new record and counts it** (`lost` in the head of the ring): never a wait, never a write over a record that the program did not read.
608. **The table changes only while every event point is off and every processor answered a `processor_sync`**: store 0 in `observed_kinds` of each processor, synchronize, change or release, then store the new kinds last. An event point contains no point where a processor answers `processor_sync`, so after the synchronization no processor reads the old table or writes the old rings. No generation word (a reader can have written into memory that the change released), no stop of the processors.
609. **One table on the machine, owned by the program that gave it**: a table of another program is not replaced (`Bad_State`); the owner replaces it or takes it away (`Events_Set` with memory 0); the kernel ends it when its owner ends (`program_cleanup`, before the handles close), and logs the records written and lost.
610. **The performance counters are detected, not used**: the host of the tests is an AMD Zen 3 under KVM, where `CPUID` leaf `0xA` is not given and the virtual PMU depends on the settings of KVM and QEMU; TCG has none. The kernel writes one line at boot; the records carry the cycle counter only; no code for the registers of the counters.
611. **The program `kernel_events`** (`kerneltool test kernel_events`): ten cases, the children on the model of `fault_ring`. Exact counts: 1000 operations of one child are 1000 records, and 100 with "one in 10"; a child that faults once has one record of a fault that stopped it; with a ring of 16 records, written plus lost is 1000. Also: a copy of the machine handle with no right is refused (`No_Right`), a seventeenth rule is refused, a table taken away writes no more records, the address of a fault is 0 for a rule for every program and given for a rule that names the child, 1000 gives and ends of a table while loaders of a child enter the kernel on each processor leave a pattern over the whole memory object whole after each end, and the right revoked in the console ends the table (no record after it, `Events_Set` refused). The end of a table with its owner is a step of the scenario: the tool stops `kernel_events` while its table is given, the kernel writes its line, and the second run gives its first table with `Ok`. The cost of an entry is measured three times by the load child, with its own count of operations and its own time; with a rule that accepts each of its operations, the records written plus lost in all the rings must equal its count, else the case fails. Each child asks only what it uses (its threads, no log record), and the budget of `kernel_events` in the root service is its arithmetic (1831 pages, 7 threads).
612. **A table lives while the right that made it lives** (`ARCHITECTURE.md`, "Kernel events", section 4): the close or the revocation of the last machine handle of the owner with `Events` ends the table, in `handle_close`, the one place that a close, a revocation and the cleanup of a program reach. `Handle_Close` of such a handle asks for the control lock. The kernel writes a line for an end that the owner did not ask for, and the line of a given table once for each program.
613. **The address of a fault only for a rule that names the program** (section 5a): the order of the pages that a program touches can show its data. Naming a program needs a program handle with Read, which gives its stop report and the address of its fault already; a rule for every program gets 0. The other fields (an operation number with no argument, durations, statuses, switches, lock waits) stay for every program: they are the clock that the right `Events` gives, and the document lists what each one shows.
614. **The program slots of the rules of each kind are a set in the table** (`Event_Table.programs`, one word for each kind, bit 0 for every program): an event point tests it after the observed kinds and before the cycle counter, so a rule for another program costs one load and one branch, and the path with no rule is unchanged. Published with the table, under the same change protocol. Not a mask in the record of each program: that adds a load to the path with no rule.
615. **"One in N" stays a division**: a countdown is not exact with a subtraction and a reset (a delay between the two loses a count and stops the sampling), and a compare-and-exchange loop has no bound.
616. **Each page of the site is the index.html of its own folder, and each old address redirects** (`tools/kerneltool/site_frame.odin`; owner, 2026-11-12: "j'aimerais des urls genre /reference/licenses au lieu de licenses.html (possible avec index.html dans dossier"). Each page but the home is `<folder>/index.html`, and its address is the folder with a final slash (`/reference/licenses/`), which GitHub Pages serves. One procedure gives the file of a page (`site_page_file`), and one gives each link from a page to a page (`site_page_link`): with a site address the link names the folder; with none it names the `index.html`, because a browser shows a folder of a disk as a list of files. The canonical address, Open Graph, the page graph, the sitemap, the feeds, `llms.txt` and the index of the search take their addresses from the same procedures; the script of the search applies no rule. The old addresses were public: a redirect page at each old path of a page that is not an index page, with a refresh, one link, `noindex`, and the new canonical address with a site address; no script. Redirect pages are not pages of the site: no sitemap, no search, no feed. The checks: a link to a page has the form of the build; each redirect page has a path of its own and names a page that is written. Refused: a redirect by a script (the pages hold no script of their own), and a redirect page in the sitemap (a search engine reads the canonical address of the new page). A canonical address on a redirect page with no site address is not written: a relative canonical address is wrong for the program that receives it.
617. **Two layouts of a page, stated by its writer** (`docs/SITE.md`, "The two layouts"; owner, 2026-11-12: "la doc, je veux un vrai truc de doc, comme Odin par exemple, où ils ont une sidebar, il y a des documentations techniques au centre, il y a les symboles à droite"). The home, the news and the releases keep one centred column. Each guide, reference page, module page, the status and their index pages have the documentation layout: the documentation tree at the left, the text in the middle, "On this page" at the right. The layout is a field of the page, not a rule on its path. The tree is generated from the pages that exist, and it is written once in the page, inside the site menu: the style sheet fixes it at the left of the window from 720 pixels, and under 720 pixels it is under the sections in the open menu. "On this page" is read from the headings h2 and h3 with an id and the elements that state a symbol (`data-outline`); it is a `details`, open beside the text from 1100 pixels, closed above the text below. The script of the search marks the part in view with an `IntersectionObserver`; with no script the list is a list of links. Refused: a second copy of the tree for the narrow screen (a screen reader reads it twice), and a guess of the layout from the path.
618. **A reference entry is a documented symbol** (`tools/kerneltool/site_documentation.odin`). Each kernel operation, command of the console, command of the tool and export of a module has a heading, its signature, the text of its source comment, its facts and a link to its source line, under the table of the page. A fact is shown only when the source states it: the statuses that the comment of an operation names, the parameters and the result of an export, and its error record. The command prints the count of symbols with no comment in their source; no text is invented for them. Not shown: the right that an operation needs, because the contract states it only in the comments of `Right`, and a comment line between two members of an enumeration belongs to the member above it for the reader.
619. **The links to the files that change between builds carry a version of their bytes** (`?v=<16 hexadecimal digits>`, the first 8 bytes of the BLAKE3 hash): the style sheet, the two scripts and the index of the search. The proxy of the site keeps a file for hours; the same bytes give the same address. The index of the search is written before the pages, so each page names its version; `search.js` reads it from the `data-index` attribute of its own element. The check of a link ignores the query.
620. **`FIRST_MILESTONE.md` is a working record, not a guide** (owner, 2026-11-12: only the guides that explain the system are published). The status page still reads it, and links its evidence items and next steps to their lines in the repository. A link of a guide to a working record names the file in the repository.
621. **The technical values of the site carry their kind** (`tools/kerneltool/site_values.odin`; owner, 2026-11-12: "que les valeurs techniques [...] soient affichées en monospace, qu'elles aient une couleur [...] accompagnées d'un icône"). One table of 15 value kinds gives each kind a class, an accent of the design rules with a shade for each color scheme (each at least 4.6 to 1 on both surfaces of a table), a meaning and an icon of 12 by 12 cells. The writer of a table states the kind of each column, and the writer of a symbol the kind of each fact; one procedure writes a value. The only rule on a column itself: a column of numbers is of the number kind, a column of dates of the time kind. A value is a `code` element in the monospace font, in the color of its kind, with a rule of 2 pixels at its left. The icon is once in the head of a column whose cells are all of one kind, once before the label of a fact, and on each value of a column of mixed kinds (handle or value, yes or no). Each icon is an SVG file that the style sheet uses as a mask: `img-src 'self'` covers it, so the Content-Security-Policy does not change. Refused: one sprite used with an external `<use>`, because the directive that covers an external `<use>` is not the same in each browser; a kind guessed from the text of a cell; an icon in a heading or in the prose of a guide.
622. **Each type that the site shows has a type card, from the compiler** (`tools/kerneltool/site_types.odin`, `docs/SITE.md`, "The types"; owner, 2026-11-12: "d'avoir un tool type qui explique le type. Genre sa carte d'identité, sa taille, des informations techniques"). The page `reference/types/` holds each type of `contract/operation`, `contract/errors`, `contract/fixed_addresses` and `contract/module`, each wire form, each record of a module and the basic types, as symbols. The size, the alignment, the offsets and the values come from `type_info_of` of the tool, which imports the contract; the declaration, the comment and the line come from the parser. One table holds a row for each type of the contract, with its package, its name and `type_info_of(T)`: the name is stated, because the bit sets of the contract are aliases and the type information of an alias has no name (the contract is not changed for the site). `kerneltool site` stops when a declaration has no row, a row has no declaration, or the row of a named type has another name than the compiler gives. An alias has the kind of what it names, and its card says so. A type with no card is written as plain text of the kind Type, and the command prints their count. Each place that shows a type writes it with `site_type_reference`: a button with `popovertarget` that opens the short card, a `popover` written once for each page at the end of its text. Chosen: the popover, because it is in the top layer (the box of a table that scrolls does not cut it), it opens with a click, a tap and the keyboard with no script, and Escape and a tap outside close it. The script of the site adds a pointer that rests on the reference and the focus of the keyboard. Refused: a tooltip of CSS only inside the reference (the box of a table cuts it, and a touch screen has no hover), a card that a script places with `position: fixed` (the content needs the script), and a copy of the card at each reference (the pages grow with each use). The tool and the system are built for two machines: a type that holds an address, a slice, a string, `int` or `uintptr` says so on its card; a size that the contract asserts is checked for both.
623. **The code of the site is colored by the tool, with the tokenizer of the compiler** (`tools/kerneltool/site_code.odin`, `docs/SITE.md`, "The colors of the code"; owner, 2026-11-12: "Un peu dommage aussi qu'on ait pas de coloration du code, peut-être avec Shiki"). Refused: Shiki, a library of Node, because the automation of the repository is commands of the Odin tool, and a grammar of regular expressions knows less of Odin than `core:odin/tokenizer`. The tool writes static spans of seven classes (keyword, type, literal, name at its declaration, directive, comment, punctuation), each an accent of the design rules with a shade for each scheme and at least 4.5 to 1 on a block of code; every byte of the source is kept. A text that the tokenizer refuses is plain and counted. Colored: the declarations of the page of the types (each type of the contract a reference to its card), the signatures (from their parts), and the blocks of the guides marked `odin`; a block with no language is not guessed. No lexer for another language: no block of the guides names one. With it, the polish of the parts of the browser (owner, 2026-11-12: "si tu peux styliser les scroll-bars"): thin square scroll bars in the palette, the selection, the focus, the marks of lists, the jump to an anchor, the forced colors and print, in the one style sheet, with no script.
624. **The console colors its code: the edit line, the echoed line, the rows of the completion list** (`docs/CONSOLE.md`, section 7; owner, 2026-11-12: "dans les suggestions de complétion, le "code" affiché pourrait-il être colorisé ? et dans la ligne de commande aussi ?"). One scanner, `support/console_language`, gives the tokens to the parser and to the colors: the parser had no tokenizer of its own (it read bytes as it evaluated), so its reads of a name, a number, a text and the spaces now call the scanner, and the two cannot disagree. The scanner reads an unfinished line and never stops: a byte that the language does not take is one Unknown token.
625. **The console sends kinds, the terminal chooses colors** (`terminal.Code_Kind`, `terminal.code_kind_classes`): a theme is one table. Not colors in the records: then each program that writes code would carry the palette.
626. **The palette of code is one table of the system, which the tool reads** (`support/graphics/code_colors.odin`, `docs/DESIGN.md`, section 4): the classes, accents and shades of `site_code.odin` moved there, and the site keeps only its class names. The colors of the site do not change.
627. **Two record forms, terminal record version 2**: a new kind `Code_Runs` for the rows that the terminal keeps whole (the edit line, a list row), sent just before its record so that a picture never shows a new text with old runs; and `Style.code`, in a byte that was reserved, for the echoed line, whose runs the terminal keeps already. No new form of the grid: each cell has its own color. Not runs in the values of the Edit record: 5 free values hold 10 runs.
628. **At most 64 runs for one row** (`CODE_RUN_LIMIT`: 4 bytes each in a record of 256 bytes). A line with more parts keeps its first 64 runs; the rest is whole and plain. A run that does not fit its row makes the row plain, and the terminal counts it and writes it once on the log.
629. **An unknown name is marked only after a byte follows it, and quietly** (the dim class, not the color "failed"): while the person types a name, it has no run. An unfinished text has the color of a text. So no part of the line changes to an error color and back at each key; the error comes when the line runs.
630. **A chosen row has its own blue, `COLOR_CHOSEN` `0x3C76BB`, and keeps 4.5 to 1** (`docs/DESIGN.md`, section 10; owner, 2026-11-12: "bah ajuste le contraste c'est important"). White has 4.29 on the title blue `0x3F7CC4`; the chosen blue is that blue at 96 %, where white has 4.67. Every chosen row that drew white text on the title blue takes it: the completion list of the terminal, the two menus of the bar, the chosen line of `interface.list_with_choice` (the launcher, `interface_elements`). The text of a chosen row is white, its hint too (the text color had 3.63). No tone but white keeps 4.5 on the chosen blue (`0xA8E0FF` 3.28, a pale yellow `0xFFF4C0` 4.21), so a matched character of a chosen row of the terminal is the reverse: the chosen blue on a white cell, 4.67. Not changed: the title bars (a gradient, not a chosen row), the open buttons of the bar and of `interface.button` (white on the title blue, 4.29: a button, not a row), the focus line and the caret of a text input (marks, not a background of text), the square of the focused window on a line that is not chosen, the selection of the site (its own shade).
631. **The names of the parameters, the modules and the variables have no color of their own**, as the names of the parameters on the site: the function green, the type violet and the literal teal stay the parts that a person looks for. A module has no blue: the accent of a matched character is a blue too, and the match must stay visible.
632. **The full check commits its numbers in the measure record** (`tools/kerneltool/measure_record.odin`, `docs/SITE.md`, "The measure record"; owner, 2026-11-12, on the published page: "0 of 14 numbers are measured in this build"). The site workflow runs no machine, so `kerneltool screenshots` now runs the second build after the machine tests, as `kerneltool check` does, and writes `docs/measures/measures.txt`: each number of `site_measures` as the whole line of its log, the stages of the start, the time, the release, the commit and the state of the tree read before the pass, the host (the model of the processor and KVM, no host name, user name or home folder) and the count of checks that passed. The checks now print through `test_passed_print`, which counts them. The site takes a log of `build/` only when it is newer than the record. The pass fails when a required number is absent; a required number is a line that a scenario waits for, and the scenario of `cryptography_vectors` now waits for the line of the seal with ChaCha20-Poly1305. The cost: one more build of every module in each pass of `kerneltool screenshots`.
633. **A pointer of a source comment to a document is a reference on the site** (`tools/kerneltool/site_document_references.odin`, `docs/SITE.md`, "The references to documents"; owner, 2026-11-12, of a module page: "ça devrait pas etre genre un \"tag\" ou une \"référence\" ? [...] je comprends pas ce texte en fait. K1 to K5 ?"). One procedure, `site_source_comment`, writes each source comment that a page shows; a document pointer becomes a link with the title of the document and the text of the heading that its labels name ("Architecture: Kernel systems"), never the path and never a bare label ("U3", "section 8"), in the color of the text and with the icon of a new value kind, Guide (a page with three lines). The references of the asides (a parenthesis, "see ...") go after the text in one line "See:", so that the sentence reads with no break and the reader finds the documents of a text in one place; a path that is a word of its sentence keeps its place. A code span of a guide that names a document is the same reference, with the heading that follows it. A pointer to a working record links to the line in the repository and says so. A label that finds no heading is counted and named by `kerneltool site`: a stale pointer is visible. The module built from the source of another with a setting of its own (`kernel_without_return_check`) says so first, and each module page states its build settings. The cost: each guide is rendered twice, once for its headings, so that a guide refers to a heading of a guide after it. A pointer whose label finds no place, in any comment of the sources of the system or any code span of a rendered document, stops `kerneltool site` with its file and its line: the five stale pointers that the count found are corrected in their comments, and the pointers of `FIRST_MILESTONE.md` to old numbers of `HANDOFF.md` name their decisions (D-079, D-161 and D-163 of `DECISIONS.md`).
634. **One section Docs, with the guides and the reference under it** (`tools/kerneltool/site_frame.odin`; `docs/SITE.md`, "The sections"; `docs/SITE.md`, "The redirect pages"; owner, 2026-11-12: "pourquoi \"reference\" et pas \"docs\" ? ça parle plus aux gens ?", then "on garde une page d'index sur docs avec un sous chemin reference et guides ?"). The bar is Docs, Status, News, Releases. The guides are under `/docs/guides/`, the reference under `/docs/reference/`, and `/docs/` is a page of its own. Every path comes from the folder constants and `site_section_folders`: `SITE_GUIDE_FOLDER` and `SITE_REFERENCE_FOLDER` are under `SITE_DOCS_FOLDER`, and each reference table under the reference; a parent for each section (`site_section_parents`) gives the breadcrumbs (Home / Docs / Guides / <guide>) and the section that the bar marks. The word is "Docs" for the section and its page: "Documentation" is already the term of the glossary for the text of a module. The status page stays at `/status/`, a section of the bar: it reports the state of the properties, not how the system works. The old addresses of two generations were public, the `.html` files and the folders before the docs; `site_old_page_paths` gives every old path of a page by rule from its path of today, and each one has a redirect page straight to the page of today, never to another redirect. Two pages with one old path stop the command, and the command prints the count of each generation. Each tree of the documentation starts with a link to the docs home. Refused: a typed list of old addresses (a new page would have none), and a chain of redirects (a reader waits for two refreshes, and a search engine sees a chain).
635. **The docs home is a door with its own search field, and a page has one search field** (`tools/kerneltool/site_docs_home.odin`; `docs/SITE.md`, "The docs home"; `docs/SITE.md`, "The search"; owner, 2026-11-12: "avec une belle \"page d'accueil de docs\" avec une searchbar, qui cherche parmi tout ?", and of a field in the bar beside the large one: "non c'est redondant. autant ne l'avoir que sur toutes les autres pages (exclusivité mutuelle)"). The docs home has the presentation layout: a large search field in the middle, what the search knows (the count of the entries of the index and of each kind, computed), a card for the guides (what a guide is, their count, the first six in the reading order) and a card for the reference (a table of its parts with the count of each, in the value kind Number), and a row of doors. Its bar has no field; every other page has the field of the bar only. One markup with the same ids for both places, so `search.js` finds the field of the page by its id and opens the site menu only when the menu holds the field; `/` and `?q=` act on the field that the page has. The `SearchAction` of the page graph names `/docs/?q={search_term_string}`. The check of a page: exactly one search field, in the bar but on a page that holds its own. With no script the field stays hidden, and the cards carry the page. The cost: the text of the docs home is written after the index of the search, because it states its counts, and the index has an entry for each page.
636. **`remote` over QUIC, and its own datagrams removed in the same step** (`docs/QUIC.md`, section 7; `programs/remote`, `contract/remote`). `remote` is a QUIC server of `support/quic`, with its own Ed25519 key from its generator (on the log and in its window), the ALPN "modus-remote-call", and one bidirectional stream for each call. The datagram form is gone with no form of compatibility: `HOST_PORT`, `DATAGRAM_LIMIT`, `ARGUMENT_LIMIT`, `PART_LIMIT`, `PART_CAPACITY`, `PEER_NUMBERS`, the status Started, the request number and the answer in parts. `Request` is 104 bytes, `Answer` 112; the field numbers of a refusal moved by one after `export`.
637. **Who may call: the address of the host, in the grant.** A UDP listen grant may name one remote address (`datagram.Grant.address`, only for UDP); the network service drops a datagram of another address before it admits it, and counts it (`Not_Granted`). The grant of `remote` names 10.0.2.2, and `remote` stops at its start with any other grant. Not a check in the program: one mechanism, the grant, which the person reads and revokes. A Retry makes the address proven for each connection, where a forged datagram made a call before. No authentication of the client is invented: each program of the host can call as the person, as before.
638. **A stream that ends before its header gets the answer Refused, not a reset.** The design said RESET_STREAM `Malformed`. One rule for each whole request (`request_check`, with its error record) is simpler, and the tool prints the sentence. The codes are three: Too_Large, Timeout, Cancelled.
639. **The bounds of a call stream, in time of the machine, each from the start of its phase**: a whole request 10 s after its stream came (not "10 s with no new byte": a request that trickles is cut too), the answer taken 10 s after the call, and a stream that `remote` reset ended by the host 10 s later, else CONNECTION_CLOSE. Each reset by a bound logs its time and a verdict against the bound and 5 s of a late wake.
640. **One isolated call at a time, in the order in which requests became whole; no QUIC during an isolated call** (5 s at most). The answers go in the buffer of the stream (about 64 KiB for each of the 16 streams), and the stream takes them as flow control allows.
641. **The tool gives a call to its peer on a Unix socket in a folder of the user with the mode 0700** (`tools/kerneltool/call_socket_linux.odin`): a header with the key, the request, the end of the sending. `$XDG_RUNTIME_DIR/kerneltool` when that variable is set, else `/tmp/kerneltool-<user number>`; the tool checks that the folder is a folder (not a link) of this user with no right for the group and the others, and refuses it otherwise. Not a port of the loopback: each user of the host reaches one, and a call acts for the principal. Not a folder of the repository: a repository can be readable by its group. `kerneltool run` takes `calls-run.socket` and prints the path; `kerneltool call` finds it by the same rule, and takes the public key of `remote` as its first argument, read in its window. What the earlier forms exposed: the datagram form took the call requests on 127.0.0.1, UDP port 7101 (`kerneltool run`), with no check of the sender, and gave each datagram of the answer to the sender of the last request; the first QUIC form took them on 127.0.0.1, TCP port 7101. In both, each user of the host could call as the person while `kerneltool run` or a machine test ran.
642. **The connection of the peer to `remote` is made again at a call when its last packet is older than 20 s**, under the idle timeout of 30 s of `remote`: QUIC of this system sends no keepalive, and a call must not go on a connection that `remote` may have ended. The design said one connection for the session; a session with pauses of more than 20 s has several.
643. **The step Call over QUIC**: `lose` drops datagrams of `remote` to the host, and QUIC sends them again (the waits for "came again" are gone); `parts` became `least_answer_bytes` (`manual.modules()` has more than 1200 bytes on its stream). The six malformed requests are a case of the peer (`Remote_Case "malformed"`), each on its own stream; the text of the first refusal says 104 bytes, not 112.
644. **The TCP case "page changed during its answer" does not race the slow-reader rule**: 1000 pipelined requests, read at 12 288 bytes in one second (three times the least of the rule) until the removal, then as fast as possible. The peer reads each answer and passes only when the RST came after the removal and each whole answer before it was the page (status 200, 3200 bytes); it says whether the RST cut an answer, with its bytes against its Content-Length. Before, the client read nothing until the removal, and under load the server ended it first by the slow-reader rule; the peer passed on any RST.
645. **A bound of a call stream counts only the time in which the stream could move** (`call_bounds_pause` in `programs/remote`): after each isolated call, the start of the bound of each stream that is reading, answering or discarding moves later by the time of that call. Before, three calls of 4.5 s made a fourth request time out before it ran, and reset the answer of the first. No host test: the procedure is in the program, whose package links only in the machine. No machine case: no export that `remote` reaches takes seconds at a known time.
646. **After an isolated call of 100 ms or more, the next call waits 50 ms, in which `remote` only moves datagrams**: the handshake of a second connection completes between two slow calls when its round trip is under about 15 ms. A fast call adds no wait, so the measure of a row of calls does not change. The deadline of a handshake in the library (10 s) still counts the time of the isolated calls; the guide states the limit.
647. **A STOP_SENDING of the host during the answer ends the bound of the answer at once**: the stream goes to discarding, and its record frees when the host acknowledges the reset, not after 10 s.
648. **No machine of the tool forwards a UDP port of the host to the port of `remote`** (`network_device_problem`): QEMU user networking gives each forwarded datagram the address 10.0.2.2, the one address that the grant admits. The tool refuses to start such a machine; a host test covers each form of the forward.
649. **The TCP case "page changed during its answer" compares the RST with a mark** (step `Peer_Mark`, just before the step that types `server.remove`), not with the step `Peer_Result`: the server can find the change and send its RST before the tool reaches `Peer_Result`, which made the case fail with a wrong reason.
650. **A pin of the key of `remote` belongs to one start of `remote`, and a key that does not match fails the call at once.** The test tool forgets the pin after a step that starts or closes `remote` (`peer_remote_unpin`), and refuses a scenario with a step that calls `remote` and no step Remote_Key since its last start (`remote_key_problem`, a host test over each scenario). The peer of a machine test refuses a key that a call names; only the peer of `kerneltool run` pins it. A handshake that ends with bad_certificate fails the calls with the pinned key and the presented key, and no next connection is made until a new pin; a connection that ends before its handshake ends fails the calls that wait for it. The client of `support/quic` keeps the key that it refused (`refused_server_public_key`), a public value. Before, a call of the socket with a wrong key changed the pin, closed the good connection, and each later handshake failed and was tried again: 44 connections in one part of the whole check.
651. **A call on the call socket carries a fixed mark and the count of its request**: bytes that are not a header at their start and the request that it counts fail the call (`Bad_Socket_Call`), with the count that the peer read.
652. **The case "silence" starts the silence only on a quiet connection** (no call, no stream record of the client in use, no acknowledgement owed by the client), within 10 s of the host. Before, it started right after the call of the case "stranger": the acknowledgement of the end of that answer was dropped, `remote` kept the stream, reset it at the bound of an answer (10 s) and closed the connection 10 s later by the bound of a reset stream. The connection then ended by that rule, with its own line, and never at its idle bound, which the step waits for. `remote` was right; the case did not test what it said.
653. **A chart of what the search finds replaces the paragraph of the counts, and follows the search; the icons of the values are inside the style sheet** (`tools/kerneltool/site_docs_home.odin`; `tools/kerneltool/site_search.odin`; `docs/SITE.md`, "The docs home"; `docs/SITE.md`, "The kinds of values"; owner, 2026-11-12: "cette liste rébarbative d'informations sans aucune mise en forme [...] c'est nul. A la limite, mets un graphe en dessous du guide et de la référence", then "Et tant qu'à mettre un histogramme, autant le rendre dynamique.", then "on sent que c'est lourd à charger"; the reading of "dynamique" is the coordinator's). Nothing is under the search field of the docs home but the room where the list opens. Under the two cards, a card "What the search finds": one row for each kind of entry that the index has, the largest count first, each with the icon of its kind, its name for its count ("1 news entry", "16 news entries"), a bar and the count as a number value; the total under the heading. One table, `site_search_kinds`, gives each kind its name, its value kind (its icon and its color, which the list of results now shows too) and the folder of the page that lists it; a row of a kind with a page is a link to it. A bar is an SVG `rect` whose width is a percentage attribute, and a second `rect` of 4 pixels is its least length: a page holds no style attribute, and an attribute scales with the page with no rule for each length. The names and the counts are text of a list; the bars are hidden from a screen reader. Under 720 pixels the bar goes under the name. With the script, while the field holds a text, each row shows the count of the matches of its kind (the match of the list, over all the matches), the longest count gives the whole length, a kind with no match stays in its place, dim, with 0, and the total reads "N of T entries match"; the rows do not move. A row is then a button (`role="button"`, `aria-pressed`): a press keeps the list to its kind, a second press gives every kind back, and the focus returns to the field. With no text in the field, a row is its link again. A bar changes its length in 150 ms (`transition: width`, ease-out), and at once under `prefers-reduced-motion: reduce`; a browser that does not take the `width` attribute of a `rect` as a property of the style sheet changes it at once. The self test of `search.js` has seven more cases: the counts by kind of a query, the restriction to a kind, and the empty field, which gives the counts of the whole group; a host test runs them over the matcher of the console. The icons of the value kinds were 16 files that the style sheet named, 9 801 bytes in all; a browser asked for each one alone after the style sheet, and on `/docs/` the icon of numbers arrived 349 to 635 ms after the start, after the first paint at 408 ms. The style sheet now holds each icon once as a data address (`site_svg_data_address`, which also writes the grid of points of the home), and the files are not written. The cost: the style sheet grows by about the bytes of the icons, which its compression mostly removes, and the command prints its size with the count of icons inside.
654. **The system description is the existing term, with a format and a version** (`contract/system_description`, `docs/TRUSTED_BOOT.md`, section 3; the coordinator asked for "the system list", 2026-11-12; `GLOSSARY.md` already had "System description" for property 11, so "system list" goes to its column "Do not use"). One canonical file: a header of 16 bytes (magic `SYSDESCR`, version 1, count) and entries of 80 bytes (name, kind, size, BLAKE3 hash), the kernel first, then the modules and the resources in the order of the boot image; the reader refuses every other form, so one system has one hash. The boot image holds it and its signature as its two last resources, which it does not list. Refused: a description that lists the boot program (the boot program holds the boot image, so it cannot hold its own hash: Secure Boot covers it), and a separate file on the boot volume (the boot program then needs the file system protocol of the firmware; the boot image is already in the boot program).
655. **The signing key signs a signed message, not the description itself**: the label `system description signature 1` in 32 bytes, then the BLAKE3 hash of the description. The label keeps a signature of the same key for another use (a grant, a later format) from being taken for this one.
656. **The public key is in the boot program, not in the boot image** (`#load` of `build/system_public_key.bin`): a key next to what it verifies proves nothing, and with Secure Boot the signature of the firmware covers the key. The root service reads the key and the hash that the boot program verified in the trust record (`boot_info.Boot_Trust`), which the kernel copies into the boot record without reading it.
657. **The test signing key is the key of RFC 8032, section 7.1, TEST 1** (`resources/keys`): it is published, the repository already holds its vectors, and the boot program recognises its public key (`system_description.TEST_PUBLIC_KEY`) and says "not trusted" on the log and on the boot display. The build uses it when `SYSTEM_SIGNING_KEY` is not set, so every test of today runs with no key of a person. `kerneltool key new <folder>` writes a key outside the repository (the folder 0700, the private key 0600); the tool refuses a path inside the repository.
658. **The root service checks the whole boot image once, before the first program** (`programs/root/trust.odin`), not each module at each start: the kernel gives it the pages of the boot image with Read and no Write, so no program can change a module after the check. It takes the description only when its BLAKE3 hash is the one in the trust record; it does not verify the signature again (the same key, the same bytes, in the same domain of trust as the boot program). The kernel is not in the boot image: only the boot program checks it.
659. **The check also compares the hash that the boot image states in its entry** (`Stated_Hash_Differs`): the root service gives that hash to the loader as the identity of the program, so an entry with the right bytes and a false hash gives a false identity.
660. **A failed check stops the boot with the failure on the screen, and the watchdog of the firmware off**: the boot display shows the file and the first 16 digits of both hashes, the log has all 64. Not a shutdown: a person must see why. The boot program checks the copy of the boot image that the kernel receives, before it loads the kernel.
661. **Ed25519 in the boot program needed one change of `runtime/memory.asm`**: the form `WIN64` has a wrapper for `erase_secret_bytes` and `erase_stack_below_the_caller` that takes `rcx` and `rdx` and keeps `rdi` and `rsi`. The compatibility layer declares them `"c"`, which is the Microsoft convention on the target of the boot program; the System V body read `rdi` and `rsi`. The other forms are byte for byte the same. The boot program holds SSE code already (its copies and the compiler's defaults), not AVX: the rule "no vector instruction" is for the kernel only.
662. **The measure goes into PCR 11, after the checks, and the boot continues with no TPM** (`docs/TRUSTED_BOOT.md`, section 6). PCR 8 to 15 are for the operating system (TCG); the conventions of Linux give PCR 11 to the whole image that a boot stage measures before the kernel. The firmware hashes the description itself (SHA-256, each active bank); the event is `EV_IPL` with the signed message as data, so the log names the BLAKE3 hash. The boot program reads PCR 11 back with `TPM2_PCR_Read` through `SubmitCommand`. A machine with no TPM boots and says "not measured": a missing measure is visible to a sealed key and to a remote party; a refused boot is not a protection, because a replaced boot program can skip the measure anyway.
663. **The machines with a TPM use a second firmware**: `OVMF_FULL_CODE` and `OVMF_FULL_VARS` from `pkgs.OVMFFull` (TPM driver, Secure Boot, system management mode; built by Hydra), with `-machine q35,smm=on` and the secure flash. The other machines keep `pkgs.OVMF`: no test of today changes its firmware. `swtpm socket --tpm2 --terminate` with a new state folder for each machine; the tool waits for its socket with a bound and stops it after QEMU.
664. **The boot program reads the variable `SecureBoot`** and writes `Firmware_And_Boot_Program` when it is 1. No machine of the test has Secure Boot on yet: this path is not run.
665. **Tension with property 21, layer 2** (for the owner): property 21 says "Principal 0 is the machine. Its key is in the TPM. It signs the system description." Here the signing key of the person who owns the machine signs the description, as property 17 says ("the TPM releases the key to any system list that the key of the person who owns the machine signed"), on the build host, before the description reaches the machine. The nearest form of property 21: the key of the person signs at build time; the key of the machine in the TPM can later sign that it accepted a description (an attestation). Not decided here. Settled on 2026-11-12: the publisher of the system signs; the TPM measures, seals and keeps the key of the machine; property 21 says so now.
666. **The machines with the firmware of the TPM run with one processor, and wait for "boot program: started" by name for 600 s** (`tools/kerneltool/trusted_boot_scenario.odin`). In two runs of four machines, the coordinator saw one machine of the TPM of each run stop after the firmware set its console, with no line `BdsDxe: loading`: the firmware never reached its first boot option within the bound. Read as: each access of the firmware with system management mode to its variables is an SMI that all processors enter together, and with 4 processors on a host at load 17 one of them waits for its thread at each SMI; not refuted, not proved. Refuted: a race of swtpm (the failing machine was the first one of run 2; `tpm_stop` kills and waits); a fall to TCG (KVM refuses no SMM, and QEMU does not fall back after KVM started); a prompt of physical presence (QEMU gives an empty request; a picture now shows the screen); a boot menu of an earlier failure (each machine has a fresh variable store). Each machine now has its own TPM folder, socket and swtpm log; the firmware writes its debug console to `<output>_firmware.log`; on a failure the tool saves the screen and prints the end of both logs; the first wait prints its time. The boot volume has `bootindex=0` on every machine, so no firmware with a network stack tries a network boot first.
667. **Secure Boot is built with three programs of Nixpkgs**: `openssl` makes the firmware signing key (RSA-2048, self-signed, `-days 36500`: the firmware does not check the dates of `db`), `sbsign` (`sbsigntool`) signs a copy of the boot program, `virt-fw-vars` (`python3Packages.virt-firmware`) writes a variable store with the certificate as PK, KEK and the one entry of `db`, Secure Boot on. The unsigned boot program stays the output that the repeated builds compare. The certificate subject names the role. The test pair is in `resources/keys`, made once by `kerneltool key test-firmware-new`.
668. **A refusal of the firmware is read from its boot manager line** `BdsDxe: failed to load Boot`, then the absence of `boot program: started` in the whole log. The text of that line is from the source of EDK II as remembered, not seen in a run: if the firmware writes another line, the machine fails at its first wait with the screen and the firmware log.
669. **The key rules after the independent read**: the repository is the folder of `flake.nix` above the working folder; paths are compared resolved; a private key file of another user, or with any bit for the group or the others, is refused; `key new` creates each file exclusively (a link at the path is never followed); the private key of the build is cleared after the signature; the reader refuses two entries with one name. Beside each boot volume, `<volume>.signed_by` says which key signed it, and for the test keys: "do not install this volume on a real machine". No command of the tool publishes a boot volume: the site and its pictures use the test keys, and say so through the boot display line.
670. **A refused measure is a state of its own, after 4 tries 0.5 s apart; the boot continues** (`boot/trust.odin`, `docs/TRUSTED_BOOT.md`, section 6). The coordinator saw `HashLogExtendEvent` give `EFI_DEVICE_ERROR` with no command at the TPM, at one and at four processors. Read from the swtpm log: QEMU sent the cancel command (`00 00 00 09`) two times before the measure of the boot program, which is what QEMU does when the TIS driver of the firmware abandons a command; `Tcg2Dxe` then clears its flag "TPM present" (as its source is remembered), and each later call fails at once. Proof to read in the next runs: the count of cancels that the tool now prints for each machine of the TPM, and the read of PCR 11 that the boot program does after a refusal. The trust record keeps the count of tries; the display says "TPM refused the measure" in red; the root service writes a warning. Not built: an extend through `SubmitCommand` with a SHA-256 of the boot program, because the same flag guards `SubmitCommand`; a TPM driver in the boot program is the only path that does not depend on it, and the boot program has none. The test machines use `tpm-crb` in place of `tpm-tis` (fewer exits to QEMU for each command); their steps after the start have 300 s each; the wait for the measure names its refusal line.
671. **The boot display keeps 5 rows of tiles, and the trust is one line** (`support/bootscreen`): the card of the line of time loses 10 pixels (its six rows of names end at its bottom), and the card of the boot image gains them. A tile shows the whole name of its module or no name; a name is never cut. After the first draw of the display, `print` of the boot program writes on the serial port only (`serial_port_print`, which the coordinator wrote), so that no line of a good or a failed boot lies over the display.
672. **The machines of the measure use `pkgs.OVMF.override { tpmSupport = true; }`, with no system management mode; the machines of Secure Boot have no TPM device** (`flake.nix`, `tools/kerneltool/main.odin`, `docs/TRUSTED_BOOT.md`, "The machines of the TPM"). Two runs of the coordinator alone on the host (load 1.9 and 12): a refused machine has exactly one abandoned command (`GetCapability` of the PCR banks, answered by swtpm, then the cancel of QEMU), one `Startup`, no `PCR_Allocate`, and its boot program starts about 130 s after QEMU (the 90 s time-out of the interface driver of EDK II, counted by its delay loop); a good one has none and starts in 2 s. So the command is lost before the boot program runs, whatever the load; not a reset (one Startup), not the banks (no allocation), not the interface (TIS and CRB both). The one difference of the TPM machines that is not needed for the measure is the system management mode of `OVMFFull`: it goes. Cost: one build of EDK II from source in the flake (not in the binary cache), kept in the store after. `swtpm_setup` with one bank was not taken: the firmware accepts four banks, and fewer bytes do not change a lost answer. The tool now prints, for each machine of the TPM, the count of `Startup`, the count of abandoned commands and the command before the first cancel, and QEMU writes a trace of the TPM device with times (`-msg timestamp=on -D ... -trace enable=tpm_emulator_handle_request -trace enable=tpm_crb_mmio_write -trace enable=tpm_emulator_set_locality`; the trace events are in the help of the QEMU of the store; the timestamp of the log backend with `-msg timestamp=on` is from memory). The Secure Boot machine no longer compares PCR 11.
673. **The program `fault_ring` bounds a stall of a child, not its whole run: 10 s with no progress, 120 s in all** (`programs/fault_ring/main.odin`, `child_run`; `/tmp/disk/reviews/review_fault_ring_early_answer.md`). The case "10 000 answers given at once" failed once at its bound of 10 s on a loaded host. An independent read of the kernel found no lost answer and no lost wake: one round trip waits for another virtual processor three or four times, the budget of the child (0.5 processor) throttles it, and 1 ms for each fault gives 10 s. The child now writes its count of faults in the shared page after each one, and its handler writes its count of answers there; each growth of the count starts the 10 s again. A child that writes no progress keeps a bound of 10 s. A timeout logs the step, the count of answers and the time ("the child did not stop: no progress for 10 s at step N of 10000, K answers, after T ms", or "the child did not stop in 120 s: at step N ..."): a step that stays still tells a lost wake from a slow host. The success line gives the time of the case (", in T ms"), so that each run shows its margin. The budget of the child and the spin of the handler do not change: the case keeps its pressure. The kernel does not change.
674. **The card of the boot image is a size strip and rows of names by kind, not a grid of tiles** (`support/bootscreen/image_card`, `docs/DESIGN.md`, section 12). With 77 modules in 5 rows of 536 pixels, a tile as wide as its share of the bytes was 20 to 50 pixels for most modules: only `log` and `net` showed a name, and the failed file was named only in the head. A strip by size cannot do better with these sizes: a simulation of the image of the last build gives 1 name in 3 rows of size tiles and 2 in 4. So the card splits the two facts. The size strip (12 pixels) shows the bytes of each file by kind, with no name. Four rows of names show each kind with its count and its bytes, then the names of its largest files as tiles as wide as their whole name, then "+N": 12 names for the image of the last build (7 programs, 3 modules with no entry, 2 resources). A line "check" names the failed file whole, in red ("failed: console"), or counts the verified files. The kinds come from the bytes of the image: a module with an entry export is a program, a module with none is "no entry". A service cannot be told from a program there: no field of the image or of the module says it, so the card has no kind "service". The kernel is not a file of the image: the card of the machine gives its size, and the line of the check counts it. Two colors of concepts are new: a module with no entry (`0xC8C058`) and a resource (`0x9AA8C8`). The color from the hash of a module stays on the tiles of the site only.
675. **A comment that names a property, a choice, a step, a review or a decision by its number, its label or its date fails `kerneltool check`** (`tools/kerneltool/comment_rule_check.odin`, `docs/TOOLS.md`, "The comments of the sources"). The 183 comments of the sources that broke the rule now give the reason in plain words, or a pointer to a document and its heading. The check reads each comment with the scanner of the site, which now knows the comments of `.asm` and `.nix` files and the rune literals and raw strings of Odin: one scanner for the site and the check. The rules are a table of narrow forms, each with an example that a host test finds; a host test proves that 20 correct comments give no breach (a pointer with a section, `P256`, `SHA-256`, `0xC2`, a step of an algorithm, the date of a format), and that the repository has none. A rule that refuses a correct comment costs more than a breach that it does not see: `P13` with no parenthesis, a label of a finding in a sentence, and a date of a decision with no word of a decision before it pass. The files that `support/cryptography/origin.manifest` lists are not read: their comments are those of Odin, and `kerneltool cryptography-origin` proves it. The console printed the first sentence of the comment of `modules/math`, "(property 19)" with it: the sentence and the wait of the machine test change together.
676. **Every fault kind of a thread is a record of the fault ring, for a handler that asks for it** (`kernel/fault_ring.odin`, `contract/operation`, "The fault ring" of `ARCHITECTURE.md`). The fault kinds are the vectors of the processor exceptions that an instruction of a program causes: divide error, debug, breakpoint, invalid opcode, segment not present, stack fault, general protection, page fault, x87 and SIMD floating point, alignment check. (1) The record keeps its 64 bytes and the thread record its size: the kind and the whole error code take two of the three reserved words (`kind` at offset 40, `error_code` at 48); `instruction` is the address where the thread continues, and `address` and `access` stay for page faults (0 for another kind). (2) Never delivered: the non-maskable interrupt, the double fault and the machine check (not faults of an instruction of a thread), overflow and bound (invalid instructions in 64-bit mode, which give an invalid opcode), device not available and invalid task state (mistakes of the kernel), the reserved vectors; and, of any kind, a fault of the handler itself, a fault of a program that stops, a fault in the kernel, a refused return. (3) `Resume` is refused with `Bad_State` after a fault other than a page fault, until an operation writes the registers of a waiting thread: such a fault runs the same instruction on the same registers, and faults again with no end, and the extended state of the thread is in the kernel. The other form, to accept `Resume` and charge each record to the budget, makes a loop that never ends and that no line of the log names; the refusal is seen at the first answer, and the thread still waits for `Stop`. (4) The kernel event of a fault already has the vector in its detail and the outcome `Fault_Ring`: no change. (5) The kinds are a third argument of `Fault_Handler_Set`, 0 for page faults only, so that a handler of pages does not get a divide error; no new kernel operation (32). A fault reads the kinds of its program with no lock first, so a fault that is not delivered takes no lock: a page fault of a program with no handler took the program lock before, and takes none now. The gate of `int3` has the privilege level 3, so that a breakpoint of a program is a breakpoint and not a protection fault; with no handler it stops the program with `STOP_CODE_FAULT` + 3, where it gave + 13. The names of the other vectors are on the log line of a stop. After the independent read: the kernel writes its own control registers on each processor (`processor_setup`; `ARCHITECTURE.md`, "The control registers of a processor"). CR0 is one value, 0x80050033: NE, so that an x87 error is `X87_Floating_Point` of the thread and never a request on line 13 of the interrupt controller, which a device service can own; AM on, so that `Alignment_Check` is raised on every machine for a program that sets AC (AM acts at level 3 only, so the kernel and its `stac` and `clac` do not change; the other form, AM off and `Alignment_Check` out of the delivered kinds, would make the kind depend on nothing that a program controls, and every 64-bit processor has AM). CR4 keeps five bits (PAE, the count of levels, `OSFXSR`, `OSXMMEXCPT`, `OSXSAVE`) and adds machine check, UMIP, SMEP and SMAP when `CPUID` has them; every other bit of the firmware is cleared. With UMIP, `sgdt`, `sidt`, `sldt`, `smsw` and `str` of a program give a protection fault; with no UMIP a program reads where the kernel placed its segment table and its interrupt table, which it cannot reach. A boot line gives both registers and says whether UMIP is on. DR7 and DR6 are cleared: no breakpoint of the firmware stays on. A non-maskable interrupt, a double fault or a machine check stops the machine with a line also when a program ran, where it stopped only that program: a double fault is a mistake of the kernel, a machine check an error of the machine, and the kernel sends no non-maskable interrupt, so one reports the machine. The console names the stop code of each delivered kind and of each other stop code of the kernel.
677. **No test depends on the speed of the hardware** (`docs/TOOLS.md`, "The bounds of the tests"). A test checks a condition; a time in it has one role, to end a hang, with one generous bound: `HANG_BOUND`, 120 s, for each step of a machine test that has no reason of its own (20 s before; the QUIC cases and the cases of `remote` of the peer, the HTTP client of the host, the calls of the host, the wait for a key and the start of swtpm too; a TCP case of the peer keeps 180 s for the timers of the server), and `system.TEST_HANG_BOUND_MILLISECONDS`, 60 s, for the checks of a program (1 s to 5 s before, in `fault_ring`, `kernel_events`, `address_space`, `errors`, `frames`, `fetch`, `locks`, `net`, `processors`, `return_fault`, `scheduler`, `disk`). A case that counts its progress (the 10 000 faults of `fault_ring`) has a bound on a stall of 60 s and 600 s in all; its step has `PROGRESS_CASE_HANG_BOUND_SECONDS`, 660 s. A step names a bound of its own with a constant whose comment gives the reason, and `kerneltool check` refuses a number there (`test_bound_check`). What asserted a speed now prints it: a host test of the composite glyphs (the problem `Composite_Too_Deep` is the proof that the budget of parts stopped the reader), "EEVDF woke more often than round robin" (now: a wake ended turns under EEVDF and none under round robin, from the counts of the kernel), "the waiter woke at once" after a decommit and after a revocation (now: before its deadline, which is the hang bound, or with no deadline). The behaviours in time keep a lower bound and lose their upper bound of real time: the idle end of QUIC in `quic_echo` and `remote`, and the bounds of a call stream of `remote`, are "held" when the end came no earlier than the bound; the line prints how late. A lost wake in `locks` and `scheduler` is a wait that ended at a deadline of 60 s with its word changed (1 s before: a vCPU that the host took for 1 s counted as a lost wake). A date of the machine is checked by order: between a date of the host before it was written and the date of the host when the tool read it. The waits for a time that stood for a condition now wait for the condition: the waker of a word wait in `fault_ring` and the revocation in `address_space` wait for `Waiting`; the stop in `fault_ring` "while a thread waits" waits for the record; the contention of `errors`, the stress of the frames in `locks`, the samples of `limits` and the last run of `scheduler` go on until each side did its work at least once. `kernel_events` waits for the revocation by the person with no bound: the machine test bounds its own steps.
678. **Every fault kind of a thread is a record of the fault ring, for a handler that asks for it** (`kernel/fault_ring.odin`, `contract/operation`, "The fault ring" of `ARCHITECTURE.md`). The fault kinds are the vectors of the processor exceptions that an instruction of a program causes: divide error, debug, breakpoint, invalid opcode, segment not present, stack fault, general protection, page fault, x87 and SIMD floating point, alignment check. (1) The record keeps its 64 bytes and the thread record its size: the kind and the whole error code take two of the three reserved words (`kind` at offset 40, `error_code` at 48); `instruction` is the address where the thread continues, and `address` and `access` stay for page faults (0 for another kind). (2) Never delivered: the non-maskable interrupt, the double fault and the machine check (not faults of an instruction of a thread), overflow and bound (invalid instructions in 64-bit mode, which give an invalid opcode), device not available and invalid task state (mistakes of the kernel), the reserved vectors; and, of any kind, a fault of the handler itself, a fault of a program that stops, a fault in the kernel, a refused return. (3) `Resume` is refused with `Bad_State` after a fault other than a page fault, until an operation writes the registers of a waiting thread: such a fault runs the same instruction on the same registers, and faults again with no end, and the extended state of the thread is in the kernel. The other form, to accept `Resume` and charge each record to the budget, makes a loop that never ends and that no line of the log names; the refusal is seen at the first answer, and the thread still waits for `Stop`. (4) The kernel event of a fault already has the vector in its detail and the outcome `Fault_Ring`: no change. (5) The kinds are a third argument of `Fault_Handler_Set`, 0 for page faults only, so that a handler of pages does not get a divide error; no new kernel operation (32). A fault reads the kinds of its program with no lock first, so a fault that is not delivered takes no lock: a page fault of a program with no handler took the program lock before, and takes none now. The gate of `int3` has the privilege level 3, so that a breakpoint of a program is a breakpoint and not a protection fault; with no handler it stops the program with `STOP_CODE_FAULT` + 3, where it gave + 13. The names of the other vectors are on the log line of a stop. After the independent read: the kernel writes its own control registers on each processor (`processor_setup`; `ARCHITECTURE.md`, "The control registers of a processor"). CR0 is one value, 0x80050033: NE, so that an x87 error is `X87_Floating_Point` of the thread and never a request on line 13 of the interrupt controller, which a device service can own; AM on, so that `Alignment_Check` is raised on every machine for a program that sets AC (AM acts at level 3 only, so the kernel and its `stac` and `clac` do not change; the other form, AM off and `Alignment_Check` out of the delivered kinds, would make the kind depend on nothing that a program controls, and every 64-bit processor has AM). CR4 keeps four bits (PAE, `OSFXSR`, `OSXMMEXCPT`, `OSXSAVE`; the bit of the fifth level is 0, see a choice of `docs/FIRST_MILESTONE.md`) and adds machine check, UMIP, SMEP and SMAP when `CPUID` has them; every other bit of the firmware is cleared. With UMIP, `sgdt`, `sidt`, `sldt`, `smsw` and `str` of a program give a protection fault; with no UMIP a program reads where the kernel placed its segment table and its interrupt table, which it cannot reach. A boot line gives both registers and says whether UMIP is on. DR7 and DR6 are cleared: no breakpoint of the firmware stays on. A non-maskable interrupt, a double fault or a machine check stops the machine with a line also when a program ran, where it stopped only that program: a double fault is a mistake of the kernel, a machine check an error of the machine, and the kernel sends no non-maskable interrupt, so one reports the machine. The console names the stop code of each delivered kind and of each other stop code of the kernel.
679. **A page table that a mapping emptied returns to the budget that paid for it** (`kernel/memory.odin`, `kernel/program.odin`; `ARCHITECTURE.md`, "The page tables of a program"). Before, a table that a mapping made stayed until the program ended: a program that mapped at many addresses lost one page for each 2 MiB that it ever touched, and more for the upper levels; a parent was one page short after its first child (the loader maps each module of a child in the parent, writes it, and unmaps it). (1) An entry is held while a mapping covers it: each page of a mapping, present or not, each large page, and each entry that names a table with held entries. `map_region` still makes every table of a mapping, so a page fault never needs a table, and a decommit keeps the tables: the mapping still holds its range. (2) The count of the held entries of a table is in the bits 52 to 62 of the entry that names it, which the processor ignores in an entry that names a table. The kernel has no record for each frame (a frame is one bit), so the count costs no memory; the other form, an array of counts for each frame, costs 2 bytes for each 4 KiB of the machine and a second place that must agree with the table. A scan of the 512 entries at each unmap was refused: its cost grows with the table, not with the change. (3) A map adds the pages of the mapping to each table of the last level with one atomic addition for each 2 MiB, and an unmap subtracts them with one atomic subtraction for each 2 MiB; a table at 0 leaves its parent, up to the top table, which lives as long as the program. Then the shoot-down that the unmap makes anyway, a reload of `cr3` on this processor if it uses the table (a processor can keep an entry of an upper level in its paging-structure caches with no present page under it, and the flush of one page runs only for present pages), then the frames return and the budget gets one page for each table. An unmap that frees no table does no other work. (4) No reserve of freed tables: a map, an unmap and a map at the same address take and fill a frame again; a reserve would hold pages that no budget sees. Not measured. (5) The machine stops with a line if a count falls below 0, if a walk of an unmap finds no table where its mapping holds entries, or if the top table of a program that ends still has a present entry in its program half; the test build checks that no entry of a freed table is present. The checks read the bit Present only. The free of the tables at the end of a program is the free of the top table only. (6) Tests: `address_space` maps one page at 64 addresses with their own entries at three levels (3 pages of tables while mapped, 0 kept), a reservation of 4 MiB through a commit and a decommit (4 tables, the same after the decommit, 0 kept), a large page (2 tables, 0 kept), and 1000 rounds of a map and an unmap beside a page that another thread writes and reads (the page stays right, 0 kept); an Uncommitted object of 1 GiB keeps 0 pages after its unmap, where it kept 513. `fault_ring` compares the budget of the parent before the start of a child with the budget after its end again, `locks` and `shortage` require 0 pages kept, each after a wait bounded by the hang bound: a thread is Stopped for `thread_wait` before the kernel unmapped its thread area. (7) A processor answers a shoot-down with the table of its current thread only: between the pick of a thread and `run`, `current` named the thread while `table` was still the one of the program that the processor left, and a wait for a lock there (a refused return takes the control lock) reloaded that table, which its cleanup could free. Now `sync_acknowledge` loads the kernel table when `table` is not the one of the program of the current thread, and `run` loads the right one. (8) CR4 no longer keeps the bit of the fifth level of the page table: the boot program loads tables of four levels and does not change CR4, so the bit is 0 when the kernel starts (else the tables of four levels would not translate the kernel), and each walk of the kernel has four levels.
680. **A program stops one of its threads, reads and writes its registers, and resumes it** (`kernel/thread_control.odin`, `contract/operation`, "The control of a thread" of `ARCHITECTURE.md`). (1) One new kernel operation, `Thread_Control` (33), with a command: `Stop`, `Resume`, `Read_Registers`, `Write_Registers`, `Read_Extended_State`, `Write_Extended_State`. No operation stopped a thread without its end or gave its registers, and a debugger, a garbage collector, a sampling profiler and a guest need both; one operation with a command in place of four. The kernel has 33 operations. (2) The target: a thread handle with `Control`, or 0 and the number of a thread of the calling program; a thread of another program only through a handle that it received (property 20, line 2). (3) The stop is a count, not a state: two holders each stop and each resume. The count does not change the wait: a thread in a word wait stays in it, its wait ends, and it is `Ready` in no queue until the last `Resume`. The three places where a thread enters a queue check the count under the run lock. A stop of a thread on another processor sends an interrupt and returns after the thread left it (`stop_wait`); two threads that stop each other do not wait for each other. `Thread_State` gives the count, and the size, the components and the form of the extended state area: a program learns the size there. (4) The registers are read and written only while the thread is held: a stop count above 0, or a wait for an answer of the fault ring, and on no processor. (5) A write is checked whole, from a copy in the kernel, and changes nothing if refused (`Bad_Operation`): the code and stack selectors of a program, the flags (interrupts on, no I/O privilege level, no nested task, no virtual-8086 flags; the trap flag allowed, as a program sets it itself with `popfq`), an instruction address below `USER_END`, a canonical stack address. The kernel returns only with `iretq`, so the fault of `sysret` does not apply. The extended state is checked, not caught: `MXCSR` within the mask of the processor (measured at boot with `fxsave`), `XSTATE_BV` within XCR0, the rest of the header 0. (6) `Resume` after any fault kind is accepted when `Thread_Control` wrote the registers or the extended state of the thread since the fault; with no write it is refused as before. A loop of faults after a write is a record and an answer of the handler each turn, paid by its budget: never a silent loop. (7) The test is in `fault_ring` (no new program, so no new row in the build tables and the boot image): cases 20 to 28 for the control, and the five entries that the independent read of a choice of `docs/FIRST_MILESTONE.md` asked for, cases 29 to 33 (`int 3` in two bytes, `int 14`, `int1`, the trap flag set with `popfq`, `mov ss` then `syscall` with the trap flag). The budget of `fault_ring` in the root service has one more thread.
681. **Principals: the machine, one named principal, its unlock, its session and its stores** (`docs/PRINCIPALS.md`). (1) `CALLER_PERSON` is `isolated.CALLER_ACTS_FOR_PRINCIPAL`; the grant "person" is `acts_for_principal`; the error unit `Person` is `Acts_For_Principal` ("to act for the principal"); the texts that a program prints for a key say the key ("flood: Space: ...", "error: Escape stopped the call of ..."). The glossary has no entry "Person": "Input" names the keys and the pointer. (2) The grant of a connection (values[6] of Connect and Grant) has the number of the principal of the session of the caller in bits 48 to 62; the amount keeps bits 0 to 47, the flag bit 63. A caller acts for the principal of its own session only: the console, started at boot, acts for principal 0. (3) Principal 0 is a record of the root service from the trust record: the name `machine` and PCR 11 when measured; no key (the attestation key of the TPM, not built: the two machines with a software TPM are opt-in). (4) One named principal: the root service holds two records. Its record is a file of 216 bytes in the store of the program `unlock` (a store of principal 0): its name, its Ed25519 and X25519 public keys, and its two secret keys sealed with ChaCha20-Poly1305 under a key of Argon2id (19 MiB, 2 passes, 1 lane) of the passphrase, with the public part as additional data. Argon2id, BLAKE2b and `_blake2` are taken from Odin's `core:crypto` by the rule of the cryptography, with three listed adjustments that take the allocator away; the module `cryptography` exports `argon2id`. The other form, a derivation from HKDF or PBKDF2, was refused: a function that is not memory-hard makes each try on a copy of the disk cheap. (5) The root service trusts `unlock`, the one program with the grant `Principals`, for the record and the unlock, as it trusts `settings` for the settings page; a signed proof to the root service adds nothing while `unlock` gives the public key itself. (6) The secret keys are erased right after the seal opens: nothing uses them yet. (7) The principal of a program: 0 for the programs that the root service starts itself and for the services on demand; the unlocked named principal for a program that the desktop starts; the principal of the program that asks for a program that a program starts. (8) The name of a store comes from one procedure, `principals.store_name_write(program, principal, history)`: the name of the program for principal 0, "<program>.principal.<n>" else; a name over 31 bytes refuses the connection. The identity of the package of a program enters there later. (9) The lock stops the programs of the session and their terminals, and starts `unlock` again; a frozen program needs a kernel operation that does not exist. (10) Five commands of the root ring (`Principal_Create`, `Principal_Lock`, `Principals`, `Principal_Record`, `Principal_Unlock`) and the principal in the completion of `Grants`; a new error unit `Principals`; no new kernel operation. (11) The console: `principal_create`, `lock`, `principals`, and a line "<program> runs for principal <n> "<name>"" after `grants`. The passphrase is never a text of the console, which keeps its lines. (12) `kerneltool test principals`: two machines on one disk (`tools/kerneltool/principals_scenario.odin`); host tests in `support/principals` and the vectors of RFC 7693 and RFC 9106. Not built: the seal by the TPM, recovery (which property 21 asks for the first version of layer 3), the encryption of the stores, the limit of acting for a principal in storage, a desktop that shows only `unlock` while locked, several named principals.
682. **The site: the chart of the search under the field, the declarations of the sources in the guides, the scroll bars of the system** (`tools/kerneltool/site_docs_home.odin`, `site_declarations.odin`, `site_style.odin`; `SITE.md`, "The chart of what the search finds"; `SITE.md`, "The declarations of the sources"; `SITE.md`, "The scroll bars").
