(1) The chart of the docs home is directly under the search field, over the whole width: one bar of segments in the order of the kinds and one row of chips, about 3 lines at 1420 pixels; the list of results opens under the chart, over the cards, so the field, the chart and the list stay in view while the reader types. A chip keeps the name of its kind for many entries at every count, so its width and the row do not change while the reader types. Before, the chart was the second card of the column of the reference, below the first screen. (2) The tool reads each procedure, type, constant and global variable at the top of a package of the sources of the system into one declaration index, with the parser of Odin, one arena for each file. A code span of a guide, a news entry or a source comment that names one (`name`, `name()`, `package.name`) links to it with the icon of its kind and a declaration card, written once in the page; a name that several packages declare is resolved by the folders that the heading and the paragraph name, else it stays code and is counted; a common word links only when placed. Not a file of cards for the script: it would hold every declaration for each page. A span that is a fragment of Odin is colored by the tokenizer, its names links with no card; the test leaves a command, a path, a log line and a key name plain. Three value kinds are new (procedure, constant, global variable), not one: a constant and a global variable are two concepts. "Declaration" is the term, because "symbol" names a documented thing of a reference page. (3) The scroll bars of the site have the form of those of the programs of the system: a button with a triangle at each end, a track with a lighter edge, a square thumb with a border and a grip, blue under the pointer. The engines with `::-webkit-scrollbar` take those parts; the others take `scrollbar-color` only, inside `@supports not selector(::-webkit-scrollbar)`, because Chromium ignores the parts of an element that has the standard properties. The border of the thumb carries its contrast of 3 to 1 on the track: the quieter fill of the pictures has less.
683. **A store names a tree; a tree holds objects and trees; a connection reaches a part of a store only** (`docs/STORAGE.md`, `contract/store` `STORAGE3`, `support/storage_tree`, `programs/storage/trees.odin`, the program `hierarchy`). The model of the stored data has three things: an object (bytes named by their BLAKE3 hash), a tree (an object that names objects and trees), a store (the one cell that changes: it names one tree now). (1) Each tree is its own B+ tree of nodes of 4096 bytes, copy on write as every other block: a leaf holds 24 entries (a name of 64 bytes, its kind, its size, its generation, time and date, the hash of an object, the pointer of its list or of the root of the tree inside it, its version index), a branch 50 children. A B+ tree keyed by the name was chosen over a hash tree: a listing comes in the order of the names, and its page continues after the last name of the page before, with no cursor kept in the service. One tree for each tree, not one tree of the store keyed by a parent: a tree is then a value, and moving a tree moves one entry. (2) The table of 39 objects and the format `STORAGE2` are gone; the store block holds the root of the tree of the store, the removal queue and the counts. (3) The interface: a place is a number of a connection that names a tree by the names of its path; the place 0 is the root of the connection. A name is one step: `..`, `.`, a byte `/` and a zero byte are refused, so no name reaches above its place. `open`, `close`, `add` (up to 256 empty entries in one commit), `list` (a page of a table and the bytes of the names), `rename` (one commit; a tree cannot move inside itself), `remove` (a tree with entries goes into the removal queue in one commit; the service frees it in steps of 64 entries, one commit each, when no call waits), `share` and `unshare` (a connection that the service makes, rooted at a place, read only or not; `unshare` revokes its two handles with `Handle_Revoke`), `usage`, `open_store` (a place on another store, for a caller that may name it: one procedure, `may_name_store`, decides it; the exports of versions lost their argument `store`, so that `compare` keeps the 6 parameters of the call form). The exports that name an object take a place; `objects()` is gone (`list` replaces it). `notes`, `settings`, the history of the console, `server` and `errors` use places. (4) Versions stay for each object, and follow its entry at a rename; a tree has no version in this step (`STORAGE.md`, section 1.3). (5) The open walks each node of each tree and the removal queue, and takes a node that breaks a rule (its magic, its level, its count, the order of its names, an entry whose count is not the count of its tree) for damage of its store. The tool reads each image of a loss of power through the same trees: each entry and each version byte for byte. (6) No new kernel operation (32). Errors: the kind `Exists`, the units `Places`, `Trees`, `Changes`. The program `hierarchy` makes 100 000 objects in 1100 trees in 1201 commits, lists them in pages of 16, renames an object and a tree of 1000 objects, removes a tree of 1001 objects, checks each refusal of a share, then a second machine checks the counts and the contents.
684. **The debug exception, the NMI and the machine check have their own stacks; a thread handle gives only the control that its rights name** (`kernel/processor.asm`, `kernel/processor.odin`, `kernel/thread_control.odin`; "The return to a program", section 5, and "The control of a thread" of `ARCHITECTURE.md`). After the independent read of the control of a thread. (1) Only the double fault had a stack of its own. A debug trap that a processor gives at the first instruction of `kernel_enter` (`mov ss` or `pop ss`, then `syscall`, with the trap flag) came at privilege 0 with the stack pointer and the gs base of the program: the processor pushed its frame where the program chose. Now vectors 1, 2 and 18 have interrupt stacks too, 2 pages each between guard pages, in the slot of each processor (6 guard pages for each processor, 24 for the 4 of the test, where there were 48). `interrupt_common` drops a debug trap whose `rip` is in the window of the entry (labels `kernel_enter` to `kernel_enter_on_kernel_stack`): it clears DR6 and returns to the entry, and the program gets its next trap after the return (its trap flag stays in `r11`). The other form, a debug record for the program after the entry, needs a mark in the thread for a trap that the program sees as one step anyway. An NMI and a machine check from the kernel take the gs of the kernel by the sign of `MSR_GS_BASE` (the rule of `paranoid_entry` in Linux): they can come at any instruction, also in a stub, so no list of labels covers them, and a program cannot write a base of the kernel half (CR4.FSGSBASE is clear, an assert). The faults of `iretq` keep the labels of `resume`. A debug trap, an NMI or a machine check from a program moves its frame to the kernel stack before any compiled code, so the interrupt stack is free again when the kernel switches threads. (2) `Thread_Control` needs `Inspect` for each command, and `Write` too for the writes (a writer reads the frame first, and a handle with `Write` alone has no use); `Control` gives none (`op.thread_control_rights`). Before, `Control` was the right to end a thread, and the operation made it the right to run any code in its program: a handle given only to end a thread became a grant to debug it. The four users: a sampling profiler holds and reads (`Inspect`), a collector that reads its roots too, a collector that moves objects, a debugger and the program of a guest also write (`Write`). A hold has no right of its own: it is no more than an end. The other form, one right for all the commands, gives a profiler the write. A new thread handle has `Give`, `Read`, `Write`, `Control` and `Inspect`. (3) The answer `Stop` reports the instruction and the frame pointer of the fault, which `fault_deliver` keeps, not those that a write put in the frame. (4) `Stop` by a held caller returns before the target left its processor: the contract says so; a status for it would make a caller tell two outcomes of one stop. (5) The error record of a refused write comes from the copy of the kernel, with the refused part (`op.Thread_Write_Refusal`) in `found`. (6) Each other processor measures its MXCSR mask at its start; a processor with another mask runs no thread, as a processor with another layout of the extended state. (7) Each return to a program loads the null selector in `ds`, `es`, `fs` and `gs` when one is not null, and writes the gs base of the program back. (8) The tests: case 26 of `fault_ring` is the rights of a thread handle; the handler of the cases 14, 15 and 18 writes the instruction address before `Stop`, and the stop report still names the fault; the line of case 33 says what this processor did; a third machine of `kerneltool test return_fault` sends an NMI through the QEMU monitor (a new step `Monitor`) while `locks` runs, and the machine stops with the line of the NMI. (9) After the independent read of this change: a held trap can also come at the first instruction of a stub (`mov ss`, then `int3`, `int1` or a fault). Each debug trap from privilege 0 is now dropped the same way, wherever it comes, with no compiled code and no gs. After `int1` the second trap takes the place of the frame of the first on the one stack of vector 1: the program, which asked for it, stops with the stop code of a debug exception and the instruction address 0 (`VECTOR_DEBUG_FRAME_LOST`); a second stack cannot help, since the second trap comes before any instruction of the stub. A double fault takes the sign rule before the test of `cs`, which the processor leaves undefined; only a debug trap moves to the kernel stack. A write needs `Inspect` and `Write`. `processor_setup` writes the gs registers before it loads the interrupt table. Cases 34 and 35 of `fault_ring` (`mov ss` then `int3`, `mov ss` then `int1`, each with the trap flag): 35 cases.
685. **The module graph: a page of the site for the build, an export of the manual for what runs, and one identity card of a module** (`tools/kerneltool/site_module_graph.odin`, `support/information`, `programs/manual/information.odin`; `SITE.md`, "The module graph"; `MODULE_CONTRACT.md`, "Replacement of an import"). (1) An edge is an import of `module_builds`, the import table that the build writes: not a library of `contract` or `support` compiled into each module, not a load by name, not a connection to a service, not a replacement. The term is "module graph"; "dependency" stays a word not to use (`GLOSSARY.md`, "Import"). "Edge" has no row of its own in the glossary: the site links the first use of each term, and "edge" is a common word of other guides (the edge of a box); the row of the module graph defines it. (2) The layout is written in the tool, in whole pixels: the layer of a node is the depth of its imports, the layer 0 at the left; a node with no edge is in a band under the layers, by kind; one barycentre pass from the left and one from the right, with whole numbers; each edge three straight segments on a lane of its own. The build has few imports, so most nodes are in the band: the layers stay readable. A cycle, an import of no module, a module that imports itself and an import named twice stop `kerneltool site`. (3) A node is a bar as long as the bytes of the module, with its name whole at its right: a name inside a bar sized by bytes would be cut or hidden for most modules. The picture keeps its pixels in a box that scrolls sideways, as a table. (4) The identity card is one procedure for the page of the graph and the page of the module; on the page of the graph the script shows the card of the node under the pointer or the focus in a box fixed at the bottom right, by the attribute `hidden`, with no style. A popover was not taken: its place comes from anchor positioning, which this work did not verify on an element of SVG, and a fixed box needs no place. (5) Each module page has a figure of its neighbours, from the same layout on its part of the graph. (6) What runs: `manual.edges()`, one export of the service that gives `running()` and `importers()`, with rows (program, slot, module, hash, imported_by, replaces). Not a command of the console: the console combines the tables of a service, and the manual already holds the grant (the machine with Inspect) and the catalog. The manual has 17 exports. Not built: whether the code of a module is shared, and a view of the graph in the window of the manual (`MODULE_CONTRACT.md`, "Replacement of an import"). No new kernel operation.
686. **An object is a tree of blocks: holes, ranges, and a commit that hashes what it changed** (`docs/STORAGE.md`, "Large objects"; `contract/store` `STORAGE4`, `support/storage_object`, `programs/storage/ranges.odin`, the program `ranges`). (1) The list of 32 blocks of an object is gone. A state of an object is a block tree: blocks of data of 4096 bytes under object nodes of 64 children; the level of the root comes from the size; five levels: 4 TiB. 64 children, not the 72 that fill a block: a node then covers a whole subtree of the BLAKE3 tree. A child holds its block, its check, its count of blocks and its value (56 bytes). (2) A hole is a part of zero bytes: nothing names it, it costs no block, it reads as zero bytes; the service never writes a block of zero bytes, so the blocks of a state come from its bytes alone. An object of 8 GiB from three writes holds 13 blocks. (3) The check of a block of data is its value: its BLAKE3 chaining value at its place. The hash of an object comes from the values on the path of its last block. A hole counts with the value zero, so an object with a hole before its last block has another hash than the BLAKE3 hash of its bytes: the BLAKE3 hash of zero bytes depends on their place and costs as much as their size. The name of a module (P11) in a later step must choose one of the two hashes. (4) Ranges: `write_range` and `set_size` are pending changes of the connection, in the memory of the service (1024 blocks and 16 objects for all the connections), with no write and no hash; `commit` makes them one commit. Another change of the object first drops them (Absent, unit Pending, found 1). `read_range` and `read` see the pending changes of the connection. `write` and `read` of a whole object go through the same block tree: a whole write is a cut, a size and a change of each block, and a block that keeps its bytes keeps its block. (5) The cost of a commit of K blocks in an object of N blocks: K + 1 blocks hashed, at most K × L object nodes for L levels, the path of the last block for the hash; never N. The service writes the counts of each commit of ranges on its log, and `commit` gives them to the caller. (6) A version names the root of its state; a copy of a node names its children; a node with no name left releases its children. The thinning of a version frees the blocks that no kept state reaches. One commit can now release each block of the disk (the table of the changes of one commit: 524288 places), and write 4096 blocks; the storage service has 10240 pages. (7) The open walks each block tree once, checks each block of data by its value at its place, and each object node by its check, its rules, and the counts and values of its children. (8) The console store has 3 blocks (a small object holds one block, no list). The error code -13 and the unit `Pending` are new; no new kernel operation (33). (9) Tests: host tests of `support/storage_object` (the official vectors of BLAKE3 through the block tree, a sparse object of 8 GiB with its hash from its definition, block and level edges, a cut, an extension, a hole) and of the open; `kerneltool test ranges`, two machines; a third loss of power in a loop of range commits. Not built: the disk past 1 GiB.
687. **A stop report names its procedures and its lines; the debugger is a design** (owner, 2026-11-12: "et on a de quoi faire du déboggage de modules ?"; `MODULE_CONTRACT.md`, "Debug information"; `TOOLS.md`, "Names and lines" and "The debugger: the design"; `contract/debug_object`). (1) The kernel tool compiles each module with `-debug` and writes, for each module, its debug object `build/modules/<name>.debug`: a procedure table from the function symbols of the linked file, a line table from the line program of DWARF 2 to 5, a file table and strings, as sorted fixed records. The system reads no DWARF: the tool runs that parser at the build on its own output; a program reads two binary searches over tables that `debug_object.check` proved. This corrects decision 5 of `MODULE_CONTRACT.md`, which said that the module names its debug object and that the format is DWARF. (2) The debug object names its module by the hash of the module; the module names nothing and the system description lists no debug object: the module is the same bytes with and without its debug object, and `kerneltool check` builds every module a third time with no `-debug` and compares each one. (3) The debug image (`build/debug.image`, the form of a boot image) is a file of the boot volume beside the boot program (`DEBUG/OBJECTS.IMG`), not a part of the boot image: the boot image, the boot program, the system description and the measure do not change with it. The boot program reads it with the file system protocol of the firmware, with no check; the kernel gives its pages to the root service (`ROOT_DEBUG_IMAGE`) and reads none; the root service maps it read-only at the new fixed range `Debug_Image` for the programs with the new grant `Debug_Image` (`console`, `inspect`, `errors`), with its size at the value 21 of the context. Refused: the data disk (an object of the storage service has at most 128 KiB; the debug object of `console` is larger); the network (a reader would need a network grant); the boot image (it is what the owner wants smaller). (4) `system.frame_text` writes "fault::three + 0x8 in fault (programs/fault/main.odin:15)"; a return address names the byte before it, the call. The console (`fault_print`), `errors` and `inspect` (its names made when the stop report changes) use it; the line of the kernel stays an offset. (5) The debugger is designed, not built: four additions to the kernel, each a command or an argument of an existing operation (a right `Debug` on a program handle that the root service gives to a caller that acts for the principal of the session of the program; the faults `Debug` and `Breakpoint` of that program to a handler thread of the debugger; the debug registers of a thread in place of a byte `CC` in shared code; `Read_Memory` of a thread). The first two change the delivery of a fault and the switch of threads, which need an independent review before they are written. (6) Not done: the names of local variables and types; a check that the names are true (the debug image is not verified); the debugger. (7) After the independent read: a name or a path of a debug object has visible characters of ASCII only (0x21 to 0x7E; the real names need `$ ( ) , " > ^ [ ] { } . -`), and a path has no part `..`, so no hostile name changes a line of the serial log; `system.log_in` and `system.log_text` also write `?` for each control byte, whatever check came before. A reader keeps the verdict of each module that it searched (one check for each object), walks at most 4096 entries of the image, and skips an object that does not start on a page. The boot program reads the debug image after its last allocation that stops the machine, refuses a file over 32 MiB (about 14 times the debug image of the build), and gives back the pages of a failed read; the kernel checks the range (on a page, inside the bound, inside memory of the boot) and logs a refusal. The pages are paid by no budget: memory of the boot, as the boot image.
688. **A term of the glossary is an entry, not a row: a short definition that stands alone, then an explanation** (`tools/kerneltool/glossary.odin`, `tools/kerneltool/site_glossary.odin`; `SITE.md`, "The glossary"). The owner, 2026-11-13, of the entry "Handle": "many prhrasings in the way you explain the \"terms\" and concepts feel very \"summarized\" and incompete." A row of three cells said what a handle is made of, not what it is for. (1) The form: each term is a heading of level 3 in its group; its first paragraph is the short definition, one sentence with no reference, which the site shows alone (the tooltip of each link to the term, a result of the search, the page graph); the explanation follows in Markdown; the last paragraph is "Do not use:". One reader, `glossary_read`, gives the terms to the page, the links, the search, the page graph, the references and the check: no second parser. The anchors `term-<name>` stay, so no public address changes; the one exception is the second row "Lent handle" of the group Communication (`term-lent-handle-2`), merged into the first: one concept had two rows. (2) A label such as "(P2)" is a document reference to the property by its name (`PROPERTIES.md`, "Sandbox by default"), in all the groups. (3) The groups Code, Running code, Access and Communication are rewritten from the guides and the code (201 terms); the ten other groups have the new form with their text of before as the short definition and no explanation. `kerneltool check` prints the terms with no explanation as a warning; it becomes a failure when each group has its explanations. (4) Inside the page each entry links the first use of each other term to its entry, so a reader finds the neighbours of a term; the list "On this page" is the index of the terms. The page stays one page: estimated at 0.6 to 0.7 MB before compression with the four groups explained; above 1 MB it splits by group, with a script on the page of today that sends each old anchor to its page. (5) The glossary has two terms "Run" (pages with one protection; bytes of text with one style), and the terms "Connection" and "Frame" in two groups each: one name for two concepts, which the rule of one term for one concept forbids. They keep their anchors (`term-run`, `term-run-2`) until a rename. No new kernel operation.
689. **A source comment is Markdown in a small part, and the reference pages render it with the renderer of the guides** (`tools/kerneltool/comment_markdown.odin`, `site_comment_links.odin`, `site_render.odin`; `TOOLS.md`, "The form of a documented comment"). The owner, 2026-11-13: "I suppose you extract text from the code BUT it lacks emphasis (bold, italics, lists, shit like that) and makes the paragraphs displayed in the docs look very plain and boring to read. we could use markdown for this (and have []() references to symbols and stuff if that's not already done". (1) The subset: paragraphs, bold, italics, code spans, lists `- ` and `1. ` (a line right after an item continues it), a fence (Odin when it names no language), lines that start with a space as an example that is not colored, and links. A heading, a table, a quote, a line of HTML and a picture are words in a comment: a comment sits under the heading of its symbol, or in a cell. One renderer, with a mode for comments; italics are new in the guides too. (2) A link `[text](target)` names an address, a term (`term:`), a guide and the id of a heading, a row of a reference table, an export (`manual.edges`) or a declaration (with its card). The bare code span stays the form of a name: there is no short form `[name]`. A link whose target names nothing stops `kerneltool site` with its file and its line. (3) The system side: the module carries the text with the marks removed (a list stays lines with their marker, a link becomes its text, a code span keeps its backticks), built by the tool; the help of an export is the first sentence of that text. `manual` does not read Markdown: it would need a reader in `support`, the bold font in its drawing and targets in the system for the links, for little gain in 84 columns. (4) The 33 kernel operations have a full text from the kernel code: what each does, its arguments, its results, its statuses and when each comes, its rights in bold, links to its records and guide, examples; and the documentation of `manual`, `cryptography` and the exports of `storage`. (5) The readers of the reference tables keep the lines of a comment. Not reviewed by the owner.
690. **A log record keeps the kind of each value of its text** (owner, 2026-11-13: "Petit détail juste pour le plaisir, histoire d'enrichir l'UX [...] les logs système, s'ils peuvent être structurés et avoir des marqueurs de couleurs et ce genre de choses pour qu'ils soient très riches visuellement à lire. Tu me dis c'est une idée de merde."; `TOOLS.md`, "The log"; `DESIGN.md`, "The colors of values"; `contract/value_kinds`). (1) A call of `log` already gives typed parts; the join into one text threw the types away. The record now keeps them: a value run is a start, a length and a value kind (3 bytes); a record has 152 bytes of text and at most 8 runs, in the 24 bytes that the text gave up, so a record keeps its 256 bytes and the log its 2047 records. The serial port prints the text only, byte for byte as before: the machine tests read it. (2) The kinds are the value kinds of the site, now one enumeration of the system (`value_kinds.Value_Kind`), and their colors one table of the system (`support/graphics/value_colors.odin`) that the site reads: no new kind and no new color, so no new decoration. A log line writes Number (an integer, a `Hex`), Size (`Size{value, unit}`: bytes, KiB, MiB, pages, blocks), Time (`Duration{value, unit}`: ns, us, ms, s), Version (`Hash{bytes}`), Module (`Module_Name{name}`) and Status (a status of the kernel, as its number). Each wrapper writes the number and its unit as the call wrote them before: a host test compares the bytes of each. No `Address` or `Handle` wrapper: an address is a `Hex`, the site counts it as a Number, and no line of the log prints a handle. (3) The operation `Log` takes a count of runs in its seventh value, which was free (at most 32); the runs follow the text at its address. The kernel copies them once and checks them in its copy; a wrong table gives a plain record, never a refusal of the text. No new kernel operation (32). (4) The program `log` draws each run in the color of its kind, the head of the column of the text is the legend, and a start of the text that repeats the program and the channel ("kernel: boot: ") is not drawn; the record keeps it. (5) `log.records(after)` gives one row for each record (the text of a record fits a row now) with its count of runs, and `log.value_runs(after)` the runs, with the name of the kind. (6) Converted: the kernel, fully (13 calls with a new wrapper; each number of its other calls is a Number run already), the root service (29 of 53 calls) and the storage service (4 of 26 calls: its sizes and times; its other values are counts, Number runs already). Left: the calls of the other programs, each with Number runs for its numbers and a plain text for its sizes, times and names. Not built: the colors in the console (it prints the two tables, with no colors) and on the boot display (it shows no log line).
691. **A cell that holds several names is a list of values; a set of the root service is read by the parser and shown as its members; a page never shows a bare name of a document; a page of an older build says so** (`tools/kerneltool/site_source_readers.odin`, `site_values.odin`, `site_document_references.odin`, `site_search.odin`; `SITE.md`, "The tables"; `SITE.md`, "The count and the check"; `SITE.md`, "A page of an older build"). The owner, 2026-11-13, of the page of the grants and of a type card: "la coloration syntactique elle est partie du code normal" and "il reste des références là, par exemple docs/TOOLS.md. Alors, soit on met une référence vers quelque chose de réel, soit on met un lien, soit on l'enlève, mais on peut pas laisser ça comme ça." The site served with an empty cache had the colors and the reference: the owner saw pages that his browser or the edge kept. (1) The table "The grants of each program" showed each set as its Odin literal on one line that pushed the column of the reasons out, and its programs started lines with a comma. A new kind of column, List (`site_value_list`): one value in each item, the list wraps between its items. It is used wherever a cell or a fact holds several names: the grants and the programs, the imports of the modules, the edges of the module graph, the statuses that an operation names, the parameters of a command and of an export, the variants and the places of a type, the evidence items and the gaps of a property. (2) `grants_of` and `things_of` were read line by line, a form that breaks with no word when the source changes form (a set on two lines). They are read by the parser of Odin, as the declarations are; any other form, and a member that `Grant` does not have, stops `kerneltool site` with its line. (3) The check `site_document_name_problem` stops the command on a name of a document in the text of a page, outside a link and code, with no exception; the 8 that it finds today are made references (the guide of the interface) or code (the texts of the commands of the tool). (4) The site cannot set the headers of its host. Each page names its commit (`<meta name="build-commit">`), `version.json` names the commit of the build, and `search.js` compares them once in each view: a line "This page is from an older build: reload" with a button; never a reload by itself.
692. **The glossary is one page for each group and an index from A to Z** (`tools/kerneltool/site_glossary.odin`; `SITE.md`, "The glossary"). The single page of the glossary had 1 301 073 bytes before compression once each of its 460 terms had its explanation; the rule of `SITE.md` split it above 1 MB. (1) The page of a group is `docs/reference/glossary/<the slug of the group>/`, with the entries of the group as before. (2) The index keeps the address of the single page: the introduction, how to find a term, the groups with their counts, then every term from A to Z with its group as a dim label and its short definition. A reader looks for a word, so the order is the order of the words. (3) Each row of the index has the id of its term, as the entry has: an old address `…/glossary/#term-<name>` finds the row with no script, and the script `glossary.js` goes on to the entry; the page of the group is an attribute of the row, so the script needs no second file. (4) Every link to a term names the page of its group: the term links, `term:`, the references, the search, the page graph. (5) `site_glossary_problems` checks the bound of 1 MB, one row for each term that names the page of its group, and one entry on that page; the command prints the bytes of each page. (6) Two groups of one slug are refused: they would be one page.
693. **The program `manual` reads the Markdown of the documentation of a module** (`support/source_comment_markdown`, `programs/manual/page_text.odin`; `SITE.md`, "The reader of the system"). (1) One reader of the part of Markdown of a source comment for the programs of the system: a library with no allocator that gives Markdown blocks (paragraph, list item with its mark and its depth, fenced block, example) and Markdown spans (bold, italic, code, link with its target) as ranges of the text, to two procedures of the caller or into two tables. Its work is in proportion to the text. The tool keeps its renderer; the host tests of the library read the inputs of the tests of the tool. (2) The build no longer removes the marks: the documentation text of a module is its comment. The line of a module and the help of an export stay the first sentence with no mark, so the console and the machine tests see the same lines as before. This is the smaller form: no reader of a help line changes. (3) `manual` draws the words in the interface font, bold in its bold weight, code in the font of the cells, an example as a band of rows, a list item with its mark. The system has no italic face: a word in italics has a line of 1 pixel under it in the dim color. A link is a colored name; a link to a module or to an export of the boot image has a line under it, and a click shows that page. (4) A row is broken between words by the measured width of each word; only a word longer than a row is cut. (5) `manual` checks its own page when it starts, and the machine test waits for "manual: the Markdown of its page: ...: as it must". Not done: the tool does not use the library; a link to a term, a guide or a declaration opens nothing; `manual.described` compares with the text that has its marks.
694. **The debug object of a module holds its types, its global variables and the variables of each procedure, and names the content of each source file** (`contract/debug_object`, `tools/kerneltool/debug_information.odin`, `tools/kerneltool/debug_object.odin`; `MODULE_CONTRACT.md`, "The types and the variables"; `TOOLS.md`, "The debugger: the design"). The data only: the debugger that reads it is not built. (1) The format of the debug object is the version 2: a header of 88 bytes, and six new tables after the line table (types, fields, enumeration values, globals, variables, variable ranges), each of fixed records with indices and names in the strings. (2) The source is the DWARF that `llc` already writes (the version 4): the tool reads the entries of `.debug_info` with `.debug_abbrev`, `.debug_str`, `.debug_loc` and `.debug_ranges`, with a bound on every read; a malformed section is an error of the build with its offset. (3) A variable location is a register, or memory at the frame pointer, at the stack pointer or at another register plus an offset; each location that DWARF gives as another expression is the kind "not readable", never a guess. The registers have the order of `Thread_Registers`. (3b) A variable is one record of 16 bytes and each of its ranges one record of 12 bytes; ranges with one location that touch are one range, a variable that is readable nowhere is one range, and a variable of a procedure that the compiler put inside its caller keeps its readable ranges only: the first form (one record of 32 bytes for each range of DWARF) made a debug image of 21.2 MB, which the machine of the test could not hold. (4) The tables have a fixed order that does not depend on the order of the entries of DWARF, and equal records are one record, so the second build gives the same bytes. (5) The module file does not change: the debug object stays a separate file that names the hash of its module, and `debug_object.describes_module` is the test of a reader before it uses the tables. (6) The file table holds the BLAKE3 hash of the content of each source file at the build; `debug_object.source_line_of` gives the path, the hash and the line, so a reader shows a line only from the same version of the source. A source file that the tool cannot read stops the build. (7) The frames: each procedure that the compiler wrote keeps the frame pointer, so the walk of the stack needs no table; the debug object has no call frame information, so the registers of a frame of a caller are not known and only a location from the frame pointer is readable there. (8) Debugging from another machine is a rule of the design, with no code: the commands of the debugger are exports of a debugger service, and `remote` carries them over QUIC; the serial port stays the log of the kernel. Tests: the host tests of the parser (each form, each malformed case, each byte of a sample changed), of the writer and the reader (nested records and arrays, each kind of location, two orders of the source that give the same bytes), of the reader on hostile tables, and the test of the build on the debug object of `fault` (the tie to the module, the hash of `programs/fault/main.odin`, the parameter `address` of `fault::three`).
695. **A command of the console gives a result, and the console draws it by its kind** (`programs/console/results.odin`, `support/console_results`; `docs/CONSOLE.md`, "Results and their drawers"). Before, 23 of the 37 built-in commands wrote text to the terminal and gave no value, so a line could not filter what they showed. Now no command writes to the terminal. A command that lists things gives a Table whose record the console describes in the format of the module contract (`console_results.description_write`): `help`, `programs`, `program_modules`, `locks`, `modules`, `loaded`, `grants`, `principals`, `replacements`, `variables`, `measure_completion`. A command that shows one thing gives a Record: `memory`, `load`, `isolate`, `connect`, `unload`. A command that does something gives its value (`revoke`, `lock`, `history_clear`: an Integer) or nothing. The console has 43 built-in commands: the new ones are `describe`, `program_modules`, `locks`, `loaded`, `bytes` and `skip`. One drawer exists for each kind of result (Integer, Text, Record, Table, Handle, Bytes, an error), in a package with no state that host tests draw into a text. A value has the color of its value kind, from the table of the log window and of the site. The console draws 20 rows of a table and 256 bytes, then says how many it does not show; a line wider than the window (96 columns) goes on in the next row under its column, so no value is cut. Rejected: one text column cut at 24 characters with `..` (the form before), because it cuts a value in its middle. Rejected: a record form for each row of a wide table, because the rows of one table then have two forms. What is not built: the description of a field has no value kind, so a Text field is plain and an Integer field is a number; a size or a time in a field has its unit only in the name of the field.
696. **The failure of a line is one value, with the error record of the call** (`Line_Failure`). `fail` keeps a sentence and prints nothing; an export that fails, and a refusal of the root ring, add their error record with the names of the operation and of its parameters; a load and a fault add their lines. The drawer of an error writes the sentence after "error: " (the text that the machine tests wait for is the same), then the kind, the operation, the argument and the three values of the record, then the error details. An error of the language itself (an unknown name, a wrong kind) has a sentence and no record: `contract/errors` has no kinds for the faults of a line, and none is added.
697. **An Integer of the console does not wrap** (`support/console_language`: `number_value`, `integer_add`, `integer_subtract`, `integer_multiply`, `integer_negate`, `integer_divide`). A literal past the largest Integer and a result of `+`, `-`, `*` or `/` that does not fit are an error of the line, with a record of the kind `Over_Limit`. The division of the least Integer by -1, which the processor refuses with a fault, is such an error and runs no division. The least Integer has no literal.
698. **The log records of what a line calls show under the line** (`programs/console/line_records.odin`; `docs/CONSOLE.md`, "The log records of a line"). The console has no grant to read the memory of the log, and gets none. It reads the records as a caller that acts for the principal: it asks the root service for a connection to the service `log`, with no command, and calls `log.records(after)` and `log.value_runs(after)`. It draws the records of the programs that the line called, grouped by program and channel, with each value run in the color of its value kind, each 100 ms during an isolated call and once at the end of the line; the lines go to the terminal only. Rejected: the grant `log_records` for the console, because it is a new ambient access for a fact that a service already serves. Limits: with no service `log` a line shows no record; the records of a program that `start` starts are not shown, because the line ends before the program writes; during a direct call the console waits in the module, so the records show when the call returns; a line that calls `log` itself shows none.
699. **`programs()` is the export `monitor.programs()` under a short name** (`bound_call`). The list of the programs is a fact of a service: the built-in command connects to `monitor` when it must, and calls the export as any export. `connect` of a service that is connected already gives its record again, in place of an error, so a line `connect("monitor")` after `programs()` works. The cost: `programs()` fails when `monitor` does not run or lost its machine grant. Left in the console, with the reason: `modules`, `replacements`, `program_modules`, `memory` and `locks` read what the root service granted to the console, and the programs that serve the same facts (`manual`, `monitor`) do not run at each boot; `start`, `stop`, `grants`, `revoke`, `principals`, `principal_create`, `lock` and `replace` are commands of the root ring, and the root service serves no exports. To do, if the root service becomes a service with exports: these become its exports.
700. **A program sets the weight and the request of a thread that lives: the first layer of the policy of scheduling outside the kernel** (`kernel/thread_control.odin`, `kernel/strategy.odin`, `contract/operation`; `SCHEDULING.md`, "The schedule of a live thread"; "The control of a thread" and "Locks of the kernel" of `ARCHITECTURE.md`). (1) No new kernel operation: two commands of `Thread_Control`, `Read_Schedule` (7) and `Write_Schedule` (8), with the record `Thread_Schedule` (a weight, a request and a weight limit, 12 bytes). They name a thread as the other commands do; the kernel keeps 33 operations. (2) The values have the rules of `Thread_Create`, in one check of the contract (`thread_schedule_accepted`), then the weight limit of the thread: a weight above the limit or a request above its limit is `Bad_Operation`, with the refused value and the most that it can be in the error record. (3) The thread is not held: the write is one section of the run lock of its home, and the sum of the weights of its queue stays exact. (4) The rights: a program writes the schedule of its own threads; a thread handle reads with Inspect and writes with Control (a holder that can end a thread can give it a smaller share); the machine handle with Schedule, the right of `Strategy_Set`, names any thread, for these two commands only, and a read through it needs Inspect too, as each report of the machine: Schedule alone reads no weight and cannot test which machine numbers name a thread. (5) The form of the operation names no right of the thread handle: each command has its own. (6) The placement of a thread on the processors and a share for each budget are the next layers: not built.
701. **A thread has a machine number, which the thread report gives** (`Thread_Report.machine_number`, `kernel/report.odin`). The thread report named a thread by its number in its program only, with the serial of the program; nothing told a new thread from an ended one with the same number. The machine handle needs a name for any thread. The machine number is the place of the thread in the thread table (low 32 bits) and the serial of the thread in that place (high 32 bits), a value that the kernel already kept. The kernel compares the serial under the control lock, so a number of a thread that ended is `Bad_State`. The record of the report grows from 72 to 80 bytes. A reference to the thread, as a handle holds, was not chosen: a number is a name, not a grant, and the right is on the machine handle.
702. **A stop of `Thread_Control` forgives no debt** (`thread_control_stop`, `strategy_leaves_list`). A `Stop` of a thread in a run queue gave its strategy fields their zero values, and the `Resume` placed it as a new thread: a `Stop` and a `Resume` from another thread of the program removed what the thread owed. Now the thread leaves the list as a thread that starts to wait (the `stopping` of its strategy, with no time to charge), and the `Resume` places it as a woken thread. A thread of the list whose budget has no time left is not in the average of EEVDF: its lag is taken against the other threads and scaled as the average would scale it, so that repeated stops do not grow it.
703. **Each thread has a weight limit, which only the holder of Schedule writes** (`Thread.weight_limit`, `Thread_Schedule.weight_limit`; `SCHEDULING.md`, "The schedule of a live thread"). Without it a program wrote its thread back to the full weight in one operation, and a weight that the holder of Schedule lowered did not last. (1) Only a write through the machine handle sets the limit; a weight above the new limit becomes the limit in the same section of the run lock. (2) A write for an own thread or through a thread handle does not read the limit of the record; its weight is refused above the limit of the thread, with the refusal `Weight` and the limit in the error record. (3) The weight 0 is the limit of the thread, not the full weight: a program that does not know of limits and writes 0, or creates a thread with 0, gets the most that it can have and no refusal. (4) A new thread gets the limit of the thread that creates it, and the first thread of a new program the limit of the thread that starts it: with the full weight for each new thread, a program leaves a limit by a new thread. (5) A write through the machine handle always writes the three values; a holder that changes one reads the schedule first. (6) The limit is not a share for a program: more threads under a limit are more threads of that weight. A share for each budget is the next layer.
704. **A program only lowers the weight and the request of a thread that lives; only the holder of the machine raises them, and a raised weight starts the thread again** (`kernel/thread_control.odin`: `thread_schedule_write`; `kernel/strategy.odin`: `strategy_weight_raised`; `SCHEDULING.md`, "The schedule of a live thread"). It replaces choices 695, 698 and 699, and the rule of the values in choices 693 and 697. Four forms of a rule that kept the place of a thread across any write each had a sequence that left a writer better than a thread that never writes: the last one was a weight written up on a thread that was far from the other threads under the weight 1, which moved the average of the queue by about half a second. The cause was the same each time: a program chooses the moment and the direction of each write. The rule takes that choice away and changes no rule of placement. (1) A write for an own thread or through a thread handle with Control only lowers: a weight above the weight of the thread, or a request above its request, is `Bad_Operation`, with the value that the thread has in the error record; 0 keeps a value. This is the rule "a program can lower or split its own share, never raise it", now literal. (2) The request has the same direction: with the bound of two requests on what a thread owes, a request written short before each wait and long after it cut that at each turn; in one direction it happens one time. (3) Only a write through the machine handle with Schedule and Inspect raises. A raised thread gets the zero values of its strategy fields and, in the list, is placed again as a new thread: what a change of strategy does, for the same reason. A thread placed as new is at the average of its queue, so a raise moves the average by nothing. No program gets it for its own thread. (4) `kernel/strategy.odin` is the file of before the schedule could be written, with three additions: `strategy_weight_raised`; `strategy_leaves_list`, for the stop of choice 696; one parameter of `eevdf_average` that leaves a thread out, which `strategy_leaves_list` needs so that it never reads the budget of the thread. The weight of the stop beside the lag, the bound of the longest turn, the conversion and the bounds applied at a write are gone: a thread that never writes has the computation of before, with the bound of two requests. (5) What stays, bounded and one time for each lowering: a weight lowered during a wait brings the thread back nearer to the average (at most one kept lag, two requests); a request lowered before a wait keeps less of one long turn (at most one time slice). Each is less than what a new thread gets, and each costs a lower value for the rest of the life of the thread. (6) The weight limit stays for the creation of threads. (7) The tests: a raise by the program is refused and changes nothing; the lag that the kernel reports for a held spinner is the same number after each write of the program and 0 after a raise through the machine handle, in rounds that go on until one had a lag below 0 and that wait for a tick of run counted by the kernel before the `Stop`.
705. **The kernel calls its strategy through a `switch` on the strategy of the queue, with direct calls** (`kernel/strategy.odin`: `queue_strategy_ready`, `queue_strategy_pick`, `queue_strategy_tick`, `queue_strategy_stopping`, `queue_strategy_wake_preempts`, `queue_strategy_move`; `SCHEDULING.md`, "A strategy is six procedures over the data of a run queue"). It replaces the table of six functions of choice 269: `Strategy_Table`, `STRATEGIES` and `strategy_of` are gone. The scheduler stays in the kernel and its strategies are a closed set, so a table of procedure addresses gave nothing that a `switch` does not give. (1) Each of the six is a procedure that the compiler puts in the place of its call, with one case for each member of `op.Strategy` and no default case: a new strategy does not build until each of the six has its call. A queue with no strategy (`None`) runs as round robin, as before. (2) The behavior does not change: the same procedures run with the same arguments, in the same order, under the same locks. A caller that kept the table of a queue for two calls now reads the strategy of the queue at each call; the strategy of a queue changes only under its run lock, which the caller holds. (3) The round robin keeps its three empty procedures (`round_robin_ready`, `round_robin_stopping`, `round_robin_move`), so that each strategy has the same six. (4) The exception of property 13 for the table leaves `PROPERTIES.md`: the kernel has no table of function pointers on a fast path. No new test: the machine test `scheduler` runs each case under each strategy.
706. **The build is incremental: a module is built again only when one of its inputs changed** (`tools/kerneltool/incremental_build.odin`; `TOOLS.md`, "The incremental build"; `REPRODUCIBLE_BUILD.md`, "The incremental build and the same bytes"). The build ran whole at each `build` and at each `test`: 84 compiler processes, then 18 boot programs one after the other. (1) The inputs of a module are not guessed: the compiler writes the list of the files that it read (`-export-dependencies:json`), and the tool adds every file of the folder of each package of the list, because a new file or another build tag makes a source with no change of a listed file. Why not a parse of the imports by the tool: a second reader of the import rules of the compiler can disagree with the compiler, and a stale module is a wrong system. Why not the whole folders of the libraries for every module: one change of `support/` would build 80 modules. (2) The build key is one BLAKE3 hash over these files, the assembly files, the module file of each import, the commands of the compiler and of `llc` with each flag, the programs of the build, and the sources of the tool with the packages that the tool imports. The executable of the tool is not an input: two builds of the tool give two files. (3) The build record of a module holds the key, the hash of each output that a later step reads, and the list. A module is kept when the key is the same and each output has its recorded hash; a record that is absent or damaged is no record. (4) No record is written when a file is not older than the start of the build by one second, or changed during the build: the hash and the compiler must have read the same bytes. (5) The 18 boot volumes have one record, by the same rule; with the signing key of the publisher of the system they are never kept. The debug image, the pointer themes, the glyph caches and the three boot images run at each build. (6) `build whole` ignores the records; `check`, `screenshots` and `repeat` always build whole, and the two procedures of the second build set it themselves. (7) Each build prints its measure: the hash of the inputs, the modules built and kept, each later step. The cost that stays: a change of a file of the tool (a scenario too) builds every module again, and one changed program still builds the 18 boot volumes one after the other; the measure decides the next layer. Host tests: `incremental_build_test.odin`.
707. **The kernel gives a debugger its four parts: the right `Debug`, the delivery of the debug exceptions, the breakpoints of a thread, and the read of the memory of a program** (`kernel/fault_ring.odin`, `kernel/breakpoints.odin`, `kernel/thread_control.odin`, `contract/operation`; `ARCHITECTURE.md`, "The debugger of a program"; `TOOLS.md`, "The debugger: the design"). No new kernel operation: a right of a program handle, a fourth argument of `Fault_Handler_Set` and of `Fault_Answer`, three commands of `Thread_Control` (`Read_Breakpoints`, `Write_Breakpoints`, `Read_Memory`) and a sixth argument of that operation. (1) The handle of a new program has `Debug`. Why: no operation adds a right to a handle, so the right must exist where the handle is made; the creator of a program already has the memory that it maps in it. The root service gives the right to no program at its start; the command `Debug` of the root ring gives it to a caller that acts for the principal of the session of the program. The other form, a right of the machine handle that makes a debug handle of any program, is ambient access to every program. (2) The root service gives the handle only for a program that has a row in the grant table and whose grants the caller all holds itself (`debug_allowed_for`). Why: a debugger acts with each handle of the program that it debugs, so the right is each grant of that program; a list of refused names has holes (a program with `Schedule` or `Events`, a program with no row), and the rule "the principal of the session" alone lets the console of the machine read the storage service. (3) A thread is the debug handler of one program, and not the fault handler of its own. Why: the ring has one count of the kernel and one writer; with two roles two programs write one ring under two locks. (4) A command through the handle needs the caller to be the debugger: the attach comes first. Why: each thing that the commands take (a stop, a breakpoint, a trap flag) is then given back by the one end of the debug handler; a stop through a handle with no handler has no end that takes it back. (5) Only the debugger writes a breakpoint, through its handle; a program and a thread handle get `Bad_Handle`. Why: a program has no use for a breakpoint on itself, and each program could else put a debug exception where the kernel copies its memory. The debugger takes both kinds, `Debug` and `Breakpoint`, never one: a breakpoint left to the program stops it. The end of the debugging removes the breakpoints of each thread. (6) A breakpoint or a step of the debugger that comes after the end of the debugging, or that finds the ring of the debugger full, is dropped under the program lock, and the thread goes on. Why: else the end of a debugger stops the program with a debug fault that the program did not make. The mark of a trap flag of the debugger stays on the thread from the write of the registers to the next write, also through the record of the step: a debugger that ends right after a step leaves no trap flag. A trap flag that the program set itself has no mark, and its trap stays a fault of the program. A full ring writes one line of the log each time that it becomes full, with the count. (6a) The stops of the debugger are counted apart inside the stop count: the debugger resumes only its own stops, each other caller only the others, and the end of the debugging takes back the stops of the debugger and no others. (6b) Two programs do not debug each other. (7) The debugger answers `Resume` only. Why: the report of `Stop` reads the stack through the page table of the processor, which is the one of the debugger there. (8) Data breakpoints too (a write, or a read or a write, of 1, 2, 4 or 8 bytes), beside the execute breakpoints: the same registers and the same checks. (9) `Read_Memory` gives a short count at a page that the program does not map, cannot read, or that has no frame, and `Bad_Address` when no byte is read; it never reads device memory. Why: a caller that walks a stack reads up to the end of a mapping with one call. (10) The cost at a switch for a thread with no breakpoint: one test of a flag of the hot line of the thread at the take, and one test of the processor record at the leave. The test: the program `debugged`, 24 cases (`kerneltool test debugger`), with the end of a debugger after a single step, the close and the revocation of its handle, a full ring, two threads, a stop of the program that stays after a detach, and data breakpoints on memory that the kernel reads. Not built: the commands of the console, and a machine test of the command `Debug` of the root ring, which needs them.
708. **Each boot volume has its own build record, the volumes are built at the same time, and a command builds only the volumes that it boots** (`tools/kerneltool/boot_volumes.odin`; `TOOLS.md`, "The incremental build"). After one changed program the build took 51.5 s, of which 2.3 s for the module: the 18 boot volumes had one record and one object folder, so each change built all of them, one after the other, 2.8 s each. (1) A volume has its object folder `build/boot/<name>/` and its record `build/boot/<name>.build_record`. Its key holds the boot image of the build and the kernel that its boot program holds, the debug image, the public key of the system, the sources of the boot program by the list of its compiler, each command with each flag, what the volume is, and the tool. With the signing key of the publisher of the system no volume is kept. (2) Each volume that must be built has its own process of the tool (the command `volume`), with the pool of the module processes, at most 8 at once when the command gives no count: one compiler with `-o:speed` on a boot image of 11 MB, mostly on one thread, and a volume of 64 MiB in memory. Two volume processes write no common file: the public key of the system is written one time before them, and the variable store of Secure Boot by the process of one volume. (3) `test <part>`, `run`, `measure` and `film` build only the volumes that they boot, by one table in the tool (part to volumes, with the procedures that start the machines of the part). A host test reads the sources of the tool and fails when a procedure of a part names a volume outside its row. A volume that a command does not need is not checked; the tool starts a machine only from a volume that the build of the same process built or kept, so a test never boots a stale volume. (4) A volume file is written up to its last byte that is not zero and then given its whole size: a read gives the same bytes, and the record hashes the content. (5) What does not change: every volume holds the debug image of every module, so one changed program still builds each volume that the command needs; the gain is the parallel build and the smaller set. Not done: one boot program for every volume, which reads its boot image and its kernel from the volume at run time; it changes what the Authenticode signature of Secure Boot covers and the test of the changed signed program, so it is a choice of the trusted boot, not of the build. Host tests: `boot_volumes_test.odin`.
709. **The machine test takes a picture when the screen is settled, with no fixed time before it** (`tools/kerneltool/screen_settle.odin`; `docs/TOOLS.md`, "When the tool takes a picture"). The fixed time of 400 ms before each picture is gone, and the fixed time of 1500 ms after a QUIC case of the peer too (it only fed a message). The compositor has a third export, `screen()` (the frames drawn, the `Present` records received, the damage that waits, whether a change of the desktop waits), and each row of `windows()` has the `Present` records of its window and whether one waits for a frame. The compositor now reads the ring of each window before it answers a call. With `remote`, the tool reads the programs, the compositor, then the programs again: a program in a wait in the two reads with the same count of kernel entries did not run between them. Without `remote`, the tool takes pictures until two in a row are the same outside the windows that draw with no end; this condition is weaker, and the guide says how. Not measured: no machine ran this.
710. **A program checks by count in a test and measures by time in a measure run** (`docs/TOOLS.md`, "A check by count, a measure by time"). The value 22 of the context (`CONTEXT_VALUE_MEASURE_RUN`) says that the machine runs for a measure; the root service writes it when the boot image holds the resource `measure_run`, which only the boot image of `kerneltool measure` has. The modules are the same bytes in the two. `clock`, `flow`, `crowd`, `disk`, `locks` with `locker`, and `scheduler` end each check after a count of work and write their rates only in a measure run; `limits` counts its first whole interval; `processors` has no pause between its first three pictures. `kerneltool measure clock|crowd|disk|flow|limits` starts one program alone and prints its lines. `kerneltool screenshots` runs the measure runs of the locks, of `clock`, of `disk` and of `scheduler` after the test, and the measure record reads their logs. The define `MEASURE_RUN` of the build and the argument `measure_run|usual_run` of `kerneltool module` are gone: `kerneltool measure windows` uses the same value of the context. The kernel has no new operation.
711. **Not done, and what follows from it.** The condition of a picture does not read the programs that work for the program of a window: a step must wait for the line that the program writes after the answer of a service. The list of the programs that draw with no end is in the tool, by name. `limits` keeps one period of one second in its check, and `scheduler` an interval of about 30 ticks of its slowest spinner, because their property is a proportion over a time. The pictures `clock`, `flow`, `crowd`, `disk`, `locks`, `scheduler` of the test change: they show counts, and `locks_alone` shows the rates.
712. **Two tests depended on a pause of the tool, and now wait for their condition.** The first fetch of `kerneltool test tcp` checks the field `Date`; `server` sends it only when the date is known, and `date` knows it up to one second after its start (it waits for the start of a second of the real-time clock): the scenario waits for "date: ok, " first. The QUIC case "crowd" starts 8 clients from one address; the server checks 4 Initial packets of one address at once, then one in each second. The time of 1500 ms after each case had let that budget fill again before "crowd"; without it, one client sent each try of its Initial while the budget was empty, its handshake ended at its deadline of 10 s, and nothing started it again. A client of the peer now starts a new connection then, and the result of the case counts them. The window of `scheduler` shows, in a test, only the rows that its check fills, and one line for the measures.
713. **The volume of a measure run is the 19th boot volume of the table, and the kept volumes are hashed at once** (`tools/kerneltool/boot_volumes.odin`; `TOOLS.md`, "The incremental build"). (1) The volume `measure` has its name, its object folder and its build record as each other volume; its boot image, `boot_measure.image`, is an input of its key only. (2) Each form of `kerneltool measure` builds that volume only, but `measure shortage`, which builds the volume of the shortage; `screenshots` builds every volume and starts its measure runs from the volume `measure`. The build define of a measure run is gone, so the build key of a module has one command for a test and for a measure. (3) A build with no change took 9.4 s with a record for each volume, against 2.3 s with one record: the tool hashed the 64 MiB of each volume on one thread, one volume after the other. Now it computes every key first, then reads and hashes the outputs of all the volumes whose key is the same at once, on 8 threads at most, and does the same for the records that it writes. (4) The record of a volume holds the files that a machine or a test reads (the volume, its boot image, the file of its signing key, the variable store), and no longer the boot program and its object, which only the build of the volume and the checks of two whole builds read. (5) Rejected: trust the size and the time of a volume file in place of its hash. QEMU opens the boot volume for writes, and a file with other bytes, the same size and the same time would be a stale volume that a test boots; the full hash stays.
714. **A thread has a placement: the set of the processors that can run it, which a program only narrows and the holder of `Schedule` writes with a limit** (`kernel/schedule.odin`: `schedule_pick`, `schedule_placement_target`, `placement_home`, `kick`; `kernel/thread_control.odin`: `thread_placement_write`; `kernel/strategy.odin`: `thread_allowed`, `pick_allowed`; `contract/operation`: `Thread_Placement`, `thread_placement_after_write`; `SCHEDULING.md`, "The placement of a thread"; "Locks of the kernel" of `ARCHITECTURE.md`). (1) No new kernel operation: two commands of `Thread_Control`, `Read_Placement` (12) and `Write_Placement` (13), with the three names of a thread and the rights of the schedule commands, and two refusals of the error record, `Processors` (15) and `Processors_Limit` (16). (2) The rule is the rule of the schedule: a write of the program or through a thread handle with Control only narrows the set; the machine handle with Schedule and Inspect writes any set and the limit; a new thread and the first thread of a started program get the limit of their creator; a set with no processor of the machine is refused. (3) No history of a strategy is converted, and no strategy code is new: a thread that leaves its processor is moved by `move`, which the balance already runs; a thread that a write takes from a list leaves it and enters the new one as after a `Stop` and a `Resume` (`strategy_leaves_list`, then `ready` as a woken thread), which is exact also when its budget has no time. A write that gives a processor to a thread that stays in its list wakes an idle processor of its set. A program only narrows, so one thread commands fewer moves than the machine has processors. (4) The invariant: the home of a thread is in its set, or the thread is on the processor of its home. A thread in a list, in a wait or held moves at the write, under the two run locks in the order of the processors; a thread on a processor moves at its next pass through `schedule`, one time slice later at most. The write sends no interrupt: an interrupt ends the turn of the thread that runs there, which can be a thread of another program. (5) A thread with no placement pays one test of one byte of its hot line (`placement_restricted`, offset 59 of 64) on three paths: a pick from another queue, the wake that looks for an idle processor, and the end of a turn. The set and the limit are outside the hot line. (6) The balance tried only the busiest other queue: a queue of threads whose budget has no processor time made each other processor idle while threads waited in a third queue. It now tries the next busiest queue when a move gives no thread, each queue one time in a pick: at most one pair of run locks for each other processor. This changes the scheduler for each thread, with or without a placement. (7) The test build stops the machine if a thread enters the list of a processor that is not in its set (`TEST_BUILD_CHECKS_PLACEMENT`, off in the source and on in the test build only). (8) Tests: eight cases of the program `scheduler` (`programs/scheduler/placement.odin`), with values of the kernel and no time, on the 4 processors of the machine test; five host tests of `contract/operation`. The case of two programs apart uses two groups of threads of one program; no machine test starts a second program under a limit. (9) Not built, by design: the kernel does not know which processors are siblings of one core, and the last-level cache stays shared (`SCHEDULING.md`, "The placement of a thread", "Side channels"). A share for each budget is a policy of the program `scheduler`, not a part of the kernel.
715. **The debugger is a service whose exports are the commands, and the root service gives the debug access by one rule over grants** (`programs/debugger`, `support/debugging`, `programs/console/debugger_commands.odin`, `programs/root/main.odin`, `programs/debug_target`, `tools/kerneltool/call_source.odin`; `TOOLS.md`, "The debugger"; `CONSOLE.md`, "The commands of the debugger"). No new kernel operation and no change of the kernel. (1) The commands are exports of a service, `debugger`, which gives tables: `attach`, `detach`, `threads`, `hold`, `release`, `continue_all`, `registers`, `stack`, `locals`, `value`, `source`, `break_at`, `watch`, `breaks`, `break_remove`, `step`, `step_line`, `memory`, `events`, `wait_stop`. Why a service: the console, a window and a developer on another machine use one implementation, and the console draws each result with its drawers. (2) The root service gives the handle with `Debug` to the service on behalf of a caller: the service names the call ring of the connection of that caller, and the root service, which made the connection, applies the rule to the caller. Why: the service must hold the handle to be the debugger for the kernel, and the rule must be about who asked. (3) The rule names no program: the caller acts for the principal of the session of the program; each grant that the program received at its start (kept in the state of the program that runs, `Started.role`) is a grant that the caller holds and that was not revoked from it; a grant refuses for each caller when what it reaches belongs to that one program (Serve, Store, History_Store, Terminal, Network, Connect, the device grants, Principals); after each revocation of a grant of a caller, the root service checks each debugging of that caller again and ends each one that the rule no longer gives. The parts of the system that the root service starts by itself have a grant of their own, System_Part, which no caller holds. Why no table by name: a program can come from a disk. Why Store refuses: a caller that acts for the principal reads each store of that principal and writes only its own; through the registers of the program a debugger would write the store of the program. (4) The service debugs one program at a time, for one caller; a second attach is refused with `Full`. (5) Breakpoints: the four debug registers, written in each thread when one is set and at `continue_all`. (6) The stack: the walk of the frame pointers, and at the first instruction of a procedure, or after its `push rbp`, the return address at the stack pointer, from the start of the procedure in the debug object. (7) A value is written by the kind of its type; a float is its bits in hexadecimal, because the system has no code that writes a float in decimal. (8) The system holds no source text: `source` gives the path, the line and the hash of the file, and `kerneltool call` prints the line from the repository when the file has that hash. (9) A caller learns of a stop with `wait_stop`, at most 3000 ms; the service takes each stop when it comes and writes one line of the log. No export waits longer than a stated bound under the 5000 ms of a call of `remote`: `attach` 2000 ms, `step` 1000 ms, `step_line` 2000 ms and 4096 instructions, with a field `pending` when the bound came first. A completion of the root service that comes after `attach` stopped waiting is taken: the handle is closed and the access ended. (10) Through `remote`, the caller is `remote`: it debugs a program with no grant, in its own session. (11) The console has 20 short names; three differ from their export because the console had the name (`resume` for `release`, `debug_memory` for `memory`) or for the word of the act (`debug` for `attach`); the command table has 256 places (was 192). (12) Limit: the service finds the reports of the program by its name and the hash of its module; two programs that run with both are refused. A result of `Handle_State` with the slot of a program handle would end the limit: not built, it is a change of the kernel. (13) Limit: a thread that the program makes after a breakpoint was set has no breakpoint until the next `continue_all` or the next change of a breakpoint. (14) Limit: `step_line` enters a called procedure; no step over a call. (15) Limit: no write of the memory or of the registers of the program through an export. Tests: `kerneltool test debugger`, second machine (the program `debug_target`: breakpoints by name and by file and line, the stack, a local of a caller, values by a path, a step, a line, a watch, the detach; the refusals of a program with a private store, of a service and of a device service; the end of a debugging by the revocation of the grant machine and of the grant to act for the principal; the same exports from the host, with the source line); host tests of `support/debugging` (the path, the frame walk, each kind of value, the search of a line and of a procedure, the location of a variable) and of the source line of the tool.
716. **The name of the mark is a font, the pictures of a page are the heron alone, and the heron of the home moves more often** (`tools/kerneltool/mark_font.odin`, `tools/kerneltool/site_mark_name.odin`; `DESIGN.md`, "The font of the name"; `SITE.md`, "The name of the mark"; `SITE.md`, "The rhythm of the animations"). (1) `kerneltool logo` writes `resources/logo/modus_mark.ttf`, a TrueType file whose glyphs are the cells of `mark.name_letters`: 7 glyphs, an em of 1024 units and 8 cells, each letter the union of its cells in one outline, no date in the file. The tool writes each table; the file writer of the font subsets (`sfnt_write`) writes the directory and the checksums. `kerneltool check` compares the bytes. (2) The site writes the name of the system as text in that font in the bar, in the foot and in the title of the home (`<span class="mark-name">`), at 16 and 48 pixels: cells of 2 and 6 pixels. The pictures of the home and of the sprite sheets are the heron alone (192 x 192). The picture for other sites and the pictures of the README keep the name: one file carries both there. (3) The still times of the animations on the site are 40 % of those of `mark.play_order`: an animation starts each 2.4 s on average, in place of each 4.7 s. The table of `support/mark` and its bounds do not change: no program plays it yet, and it stays the rhythm of the system. (4) Not verified in a browser: the look of the font at each density, and the fetch of the font under the header `Content-Security-Policy` of the host, which needs `font-src 'self'`.
717. **The trees of the navigation of the site show tree lines** (owner, 2026-11-13: "est-ce que c'est possible dans les pages de doc où il y a [...] des tables de matière arborescente[s] en particulier sur les sidebars plutôt que ce soit juste tabulé qu'il y ait justement des lignes [...] d'arborescence"; `tools/kerneltool/site_documentation.odin`, `tools/kerneltool/site_style.odin`; `SITE.md`, "The tree lines"). (1) One rule for every tree: each list under an item of a list, inside a `nav` element, has the class `tree-lines`, and the check of the pages refuses one with no class. (2) The documentation tree (3 levels) and "On this page" (2 levels) are the two trees; "On this page" was a flat list with a padding for its second level, and is now nested. (3) A level is 16 pixels: the vertical line 8 pixels from the row of the parent, a branch of 8 pixels at the middle of the first line of an item; lines of 1 pixel, square corners; the vertical line stops at the branch of the last item. (4) The lines to the current item are in the line color (`:has()`); the others are the dim color at 40 % on the track, because the track alone has a contrast of 1.17 on the surface of the tree. (5) The rows of "On this page" lose their line of the track at the left: beside the tree lines it made two lines for one level. (6) Not checked in a browser on the real pages: the outline of the focus over the lines, the light form, the narrow screen.
718. **The network service computes no checksum of UDP or TCP, and UDP has the form of the segment endpoint** (`programs/network`, `support/network`, `support/tcp`, `contract/datagram`, `programs/net`, `programs/quic_echo`, `programs/remote`; `NETWORK.md`, "The path of a datagram and of a segment"; `PROPERTIES.md`, property 5, the line "As built"). It replaces what choices 197, 198 and 299 say of the checksums. (1) The measure: the network service counts, for each endpoint and each direction, the datagrams or the segments, their bytes, and the bytes of each of its passes over them. When an endpoint ends, the log has the passes for each payload byte, a measure, and the structural fact, which `kerneltool test network`, `test tcp` and `test quic` wait for: "this service read each payload byte one time on receive and one time on send, for the copy, and computed no checksum of it: as it must". By a read of the code, the passes were 2 on receive for TCP and for UDP, 1 on send for TCP and 2 on send for UDP; they are 1 in each case. (2) The TCP checksum of a segment that arrives is checked by `tcp.step`, with the pseudo-header of the remote address and the local address of the table; a wrong one adds to `Table.bad_checksums`, changes no connection and gets no answer. (3) A datagram endpoint carries whole datagrams: the program writes the UDP header and the checksum (`network.udp_header_write`) and reads them (`network.udp_data_read`, with the rule `Verify` or `Do_Not_Verify`). QUIC programs write a real checksum and do not check one: QUIC authenticates each packet. (4) What the network service still refuses, in its own copy of the header, read after the copy: a source port that is not a local port of the endpoint, a remote that the grant does not name or that is not admitted, a length field of UDP that is not the count, and the limit of 3 times of a UDP listen grant. It writes the source address itself. (5) The cost of the rule: a frame whose port the wire damaged can reach the endpoint of another port, whose program then drops it by its checksum. A program that does not check, or that writes a wrong checksum, harms its own traffic only. (6) The measure "network: the headers and the checksum of a datagram of 1472 bytes" is now two lines: "network: the headers of IPv4 and Ethernet of a frame with a payload of 1480 bytes: N ns" and "net: the header and the checksum of a datagram of 1472 bytes: N ns, in this program (no offload)". No speed is asserted, and none is expected to change: the checksum of 1472 bytes is under 1 us of a round trip of 60 us or more. Not done: the two next steps below.



## Gaps

- **Trusted boot**: layers 1 and 2 ran on the machines of the coordinator. Layer 3 (Secure Boot), the five new machines and the fix of the machines of the TPM are written and not run.
